    names = {socket.AF_INET: "IPv4", socket.AF_INET6: "IPv6"}
    return [names.get(f, str(f)) for f in dict.fromkeys(ranked)]

# A numeric address needs no resolver, so these two lines are the same on every machine.
print("127.0.0.1       ->", families("127.0.0.1"))
print("::1             ->", families("::1"))

# A name depends on this machine's hosts file, resolver and routes: check the shape, not the order.
for host in ("localhost", "one.one.one.one"):
    found = families(host)
    print(f"{host:<15} -> at least one family: {len(found) >= 1}, all of them IP: "
          f"{set(found) <= {'IPv4', 'IPv6'}}")

127.0.0.1       -> ['IPv4']
::1             -> ['IPv6']
localhost       -> at least one family: True, all of them IP: True
one.one.one.one -> at least one family: True, all of them IP: True

The loop never names a family, and the same code handled an IPv4 address, an IPv6 address and two names. That is the whole technique.

What it deliberately does not print for the names is which families came back, or in what order, because that is a fact about the machine running it rather than about the name. getaddrinfo does not return the DNS response order — it sorts candidates using the destination address selection rules of RFC 6724, comparing each against the source addresses this host actually has. Measured in three places: on the Linux machine this course was written on, which has no IPv6 route (§2.9), one.one.one.one ranked IPv4 → IPv6 and localhost offered IPv4 alone; in a Linux container and on macOS, localhost ranked IPv6 first. Print families("one.one.one.one") on your own machine and you learn which family it prefers — worth knowing before you start debugging a connectivity problem.

Addresses are not printed either, for a second reason: two equally-ranked addresses rotate between calls. Print sockaddr[0] for one.one.one.one twice in a row and 1.1.1.1 and 1.0.0.1 swap places, as do the two IPv6 addresses. That rotation is DNS round-robin (§7.5) doing crude load distribution, and it is why a client that only ever uses the first answer still spreads its traffic across a server's addresses.

The practical consequences: a correct client tries each address in turn until one connects rather than using only the first, which is what §13.6's Happy Eyeballs formalises. And socket.create_connection((host, port)), used in §6.1, does that loop for you — which is the reason to prefer it over building the socket by hand.

AF_UNIX deserves a mention because it is the right answer more often than people realise. For two processes on one machine — an application and its sidecar, a service and its local proxy — a Unix domain socket skips the whole IP and TCP stack, is roughly twice as fast, cannot be reached from the network, and gets its access control from filesystem permissions rather than from a firewall rule you have to remember to write.

6.3 Blocking, and Why It Does Not Scale

By default every socket call blocks: recv does not return until data arrives, accept does not return until a connection does, connect does not return until the handshake completes or fails. The code in §6.1 reads well precisely because of that.

It also serves exactly one client. While blocked in recv for client A, the server cannot accept client B. The three classical escapes:

A thread per connection. Simple and genuinely fine to a few thousand connections. Each thread costs a stack (typically 8 MB of address space, a few dozen KB resident) and a scheduler entry, and the cost is paid whether the connection is busy or idle.
A process per connection. What the original Unix servers did, via fork(). Robust — a crash takes one connection with it — and far too expensive for anything modern.
Non-blocking sockets and one event loop. One thread, thousands of connections, no per-connection stack. This is what nginx, Redis, Node and every async framework do, and §6.4 is how it works.

The decisive argument for the third is that most connections are idle most of the time. A chat service with 50,000 connected clients might see 200 messages a second. Fifty thousand threads to do the work of one is not a tuning problem, it is the wrong shape.

A non-blocking socket returns immediately from every call. When there is nothing to do it raises BlockingIOError — EAGAIN/EWOULDBLOCK in C — rather than waiting, and connect is the special case that reports EINPROGRESS:

import socket, errno

s = socket.socket()
s.setblocking(False)
try:
    s.connect(("1.1.1.1", 443))
except BlockingIOError as e:
    print(f"connect returned immediately: {type(e).__name__} "
          f"({errno.errorcode[e.errno]})")
print("the handshake is now running in the background;")
print("the socket becomes writable when it completes or fails")
s.close()

connect returned immediately: BlockingIOError (EINPROGRESS)
the handshake is now running in the background;
the socket becomes writable when it completes or fails

The error is printed by name rather than by number, because the number is not portable: EINPROGRESS is 115 on Linux and 36 on macOS.

That is the whole model: start the operation, get told it is not finished, and come back when the socket is ready. Knowing when it is ready is the event loop's job.

6.4 Readiness: select, poll and epoll

The kernel knows which of your sockets have data waiting. Readiness notification is how it tells you, and there have been three generations of the interface.

Call Per-call cost How you register interest Limit
select() O(n) — kernel scans every fd, every call Pass the whole set, every call FD_SETSIZE, usually 1024
poll() O(n) — same scan, no fixed limit Pass the whole array, every call none
epoll() (Linux), kqueue (BSD/macOS) O(1) amortised Register once; kernel keeps the set none

The difference is not micro-optimisation. With select, monitoring 10,000 sockets means copying and scanning 10,000 descriptors on every iteration of the loop, even to learn that one of them is ready. epoll registers the set once in kernel memory and returns only the ready ones. That difference is why the "C10K problem" was a named problem in 1999 and is not one now.

Python hides the choice behind selectors, which picks the best mechanism available:

import selectors, socket

sel = selectors.DefaultSelector()
print("selector chosen on this platform:", type(sel).__name__)
sel.close()

selector chosen on this platform: EpollSelector

That is Linux. On macOS the same line prints KqueueSelector — the other O(1) mechanism in the table above.

Here is a complete, single-threaded echo server that handles any number of concurrent clients. This is the shape of every event-driven server ever written.

import selectors, socket, threading

sel = selectors.DefaultSelector()
srv = socket.socket()
srv.setsockopt(socket.SOL_SOCKET, socket.SO_REUSEADDR, 1)
srv.bind(("127.0.0.1", 0))
srv.listen(128)
srv.setblocking(False)
sel.register(srv, selectors.EVENT_READ, data="listener")
addr = srv.getsockname()

def clients():
    """Three concurrent clients, so the loop below is genuinely multiplexing."""
    socks = [socket.create_connection(addr) for _ in range(3)]
    for i, s in enumerate(socks):
        s.sendall(f"client-{i}".encode())
    for s in socks:
        s.recv(100)
        s.close()

threading.Thread(target=clients, daemon=True).start()

served, done = [], 0
while done < 3:
    for key, _events in sel.select(timeout=5):
        if key.data == "listener":                        # a new connection is waiting
            conn, peer = key.fileobj.accept()
            conn.setblocking(False)
            sel.register(conn, selectors.EVENT_READ, data=peer)
        else:                                             # an existing one has data or EOF
            conn = key.fileobj
            chunk = conn.recv(4096)
            if chunk:
                conn.sendall(chunk.upper())
                served.append(chunk.decode())
            else:                                         # empty read = peer closed
                sel.unregister(conn)
                conn.close()
                done += 1

print("one thread served:", sorted(served))
sel.close(); srv.close()

one thread served: ['client-0', 'client-1', 'client-2']

Four rules that this loop obeys and that broken event loops violate:

  • Every socket is non-blocking, including the listener. One blocking call anywhere stalls every connection the loop owns. This is the single most common way an async server acquires a mysterious latency spike: a DNS lookup, a file read or a database driver that is secretly synchronous.
  • accept() and recv() are distinguished by what was registered, not by inspecting the socket. Attaching your own data at registration time is how you avoid a lookup table.
  • An empty recv() is EOF, and must unregister before close(). Closing a registered descriptor leaves the selector holding a stale entry, and since descriptor numbers are reused, the next accepted connection can inherit its events — a genuinely baffling bug.
  • sel.select() returns only ready sockets. The loop never touches an idle connection, which is why the cost scales with activity rather than with the number of connections.

In practice you will write this loop through a framework — asyncio, Netty, Tokio, libuv — rather than by hand. The reason to have written it once is that every stall, every "why is my async code slow" question, and every backpressure bug is explained by the four rules above.

6.5 Socket Options Worth Knowing

setsockopt is a grab bag. Six options account for nearly everything you will need.

SO_REUSEADDR — allows binding to a port that has a lingering TIME_WAIT socket (§3.7). Without it, restarting a server fails with Address already in use for up to 60 seconds, which is why it is the first line of every server ever written.

There is a subtlety here that almost every tutorial gets wrong, and it is worth proving rather than asserting:

import socket, time

def trial(listener_had_reuse: bool) -> str:
    port = 19200 + (int(time.time() * 1000) % 300)
    srv = socket.socket()
    if listener_had_reuse:
        srv.setsockopt(socket.SOL_SOCKET, socket.SO_REUSEADDR, 1)
    srv.bind(("127.0.0.1", port)); srv.listen(5)

    cli = socket.create_connection(("127.0.0.1", port))
    conn, _ = srv.accept()
    conn.close(); cli.close(); srv.close()      # the server closes first -> its tuple TIME_WAITs
    time.sleep(0.2)

    new = socket.socket()
    new.setsockopt(socket.SOL_SOCKET, socket.SO_REUSEADDR, 1)   # the *rebinding* socket sets it
    try:
        new.bind(("127.0.0.1", port)); result = "bind OK"
    except OSError as e:
        result = str(e)
    new.close()
    return result

for had in (False, True):
    print(f"original listener set SO_REUSEADDR={str(had):<5} -> rebind: {trial(had)}")

original listener set SO_REUSEADDR=False -> rebind: [Errno 98] Address already in use
original listener set SO_REUSEADDR=True  -> rebind: bind OK

Setting SO_REUSEADDR on the socket that is rebinding is not enough. Linux records the flag on the connection when it enters TIME_WAIT, and refuses the reuse unless the socket that created it had the option set. So the option protects you against your own future restarts only if the currently running process already had it — which is precisely the case where you cannot go back and add it. Set it always, on every listener, from the first version.

That is Linux's rule, and the output above is from Linux. BSD-derived stacks apply the flag differently: on macOS the same program prints bind OK on both lines, because the rebinding socket's own flag is enough there — which means a service that restarts cleanly on a developer's Mac can still fail this way in production.

SO_REUSEPORT is a different thing despite the name. It allows several live sockets to bind the same port, with the kernel load-balancing incoming connections across them by hashing the four-tuple. This is how a multi-process server — nginx workers, a Go service with several listeners — gets kernel-level distribution without a shared accept lock, and how you achieve a zero-downtime restart: start the new process, let both accept for a moment, then stop the old one.

TCP_NODELAY disables Nagle (§4.6). Default is off, meaning Nagle is on; set it for anything request/response.

SO_KEEPALIVE and its three timers, covered in §4.7. The defaults are useless; set them per socket.

SO_RCVBUF / SO_SNDBUF set buffer sizes. Usually leave them alone — §5.5's transcript shows the kernel auto-tuning rcv_space from 198 KB to 3.3 MB on its own, and setting the option explicitly disables that auto-tuning. Raise the ceilings in net.ipv4.tcp_rmem/tcp_wmem instead.

SO_LINGER controls what close() does with unsent data. Set to zero, it sends an RST instead of a FIN and skips TIME_WAIT entirely — a legitimate choice in a load generator, and data loss anywhere else.

6.6 Timeouts: The Only Option That Is Always Your Job

A socket with no timeout can block forever. Not "for a long time" — forever, in the specific case where the peer is unreachable and the network drops packets silently (§14.2). The defaults are all wrong for an application:

  • connect() on Linux retries SYNs for about 130 seconds before giving up.
  • recv() on an established connection has no timeout at all. If the peer goes away without sending a FIN, only TCP keep-alive will ever notice, and that is two hours by default.

Python's socket timeout covers both, and is the coarse tool:

import socket, time

s = socket.socket()
s.settimeout(0.05)
start = time.perf_counter()
try:
    s.connect(("10.255.255.1", 80))          # an address with no route to it
except TimeoutError as e:
    elapsed = time.perf_counter() - start
    print(f"raised {type(e).__name__}: {e}")
    print(f"gave up after roughly the timeout, not the kernel's 130 s: {elapsed < 1.0}")
s.close()

print(f"default socket timeout: {socket.getdefaulttimeout()}  <- None means block forever")

raised TimeoutError: timed out
gave up after roughly the timeout, not the kernel's 130 s: True
default socket timeout: None  <- None means block forever

settimeout applies to each individual operation, not to the whole exchange — which is a distinction that matters. A 5-second timeout on a socket does not mean the request completes within 5 seconds; it means no single read waits longer than 5 seconds. A peer that dribbles one byte every 4 seconds keeps the connection alive indefinitely, which is exactly the Slowloris attack. What you usually want is a deadline for the whole operation:

import socket, time

def recv_until_deadline(sock, n: int, deadline: float) -> bytes:
    """Read n bytes, but give up at an absolute wall-clock deadline."""
    buf = b""
    while len(buf) < n:
        remaining = deadline - time.monotonic()
        if remaining <= 0:
            raise TimeoutError(f"deadline exceeded with {len(buf)}/{n} bytes read")
        sock.settimeout(remaining)                # shrink the per-read budget each time
        try:
            part = sock.recv(n - len(buf))
        except TimeoutError:                      # the socket's own timeout fired first
            raise TimeoutError(
                f"deadline exceeded with {len(buf)}/{n} bytes read") from None
        if not part:
            raise ConnectionError("peer closed early")
        buf += part
    return buf

a, b = socket.socketpair()
a.sendall(b"only-ten-b")                      # ten bytes, and the reader wants fifty
start = time.monotonic()
try:
    recv_until_deadline(b, 50, start + 0.1)
except TimeoutError as e:
    elapsed = time.monotonic() - start
    print(f"raised {type(e).__name__}, reporting partial progress: {'bytes read' in str(e)}")
    print(f"gave up at the deadline rather than blocking forever: {0.1 <= elapsed < 1.0}")
a.close(); b.close()

raised TimeoutError, reporting partial progress: True
gave up at the deadline rather than blocking forever: True

That except TimeoutError clause is not defensive padding — without it this function is subtly wrong. The settimeout call makes recv itself raise on expiry, so the loop's own remaining <= 0 check is usually not what fires, and the caller receives a bare timed out with no indication of how much was read. Re-raising with the byte count preserved is the difference between a log line you can act on and one you cannot.

The pattern — an absolute deadline computed once, converted into a shrinking per-call timeout — is how every well-behaved network client works, and it composes: a request's deadline can be passed down into each of the calls it makes, so a chain of services cannot collectively exceed the budget the caller allowed. §14.3 develops that into a full timeout budget.

6.7 Handling Errors Honestly

Socket calls fail in a small number of ways, and each errno names a different layer:

Error Python Means Layer
ECONNREFUSED ConnectionRefusedError An RST came back: reachable host, nothing listening (§3.8) Transport
ETIMEDOUT TimeoutError Nothing came back at all — dropped, not refused Internet
EHOSTUNREACH / ENETUNREACH OSError No route. Often the IPv6 half of a dual-stack host (§2.9) Internet
ECONNRESET ConnectionResetError An established connection was aborted by an RST Transport
EPIPE BrokenPipeError You wrote to a socket the peer already closed Transport
EAGAIN BlockingIOError Non-blocking and not ready. Not an error (§6.3) API
EMFILE OSError Out of file descriptors — usually a leak (§3.9) Process
EADDRINUSE OSError Port taken, or a TIME_WAIT without SO_REUSEADDR (§6.5) API

Two of these are routinely mishandled. ECONNRESET on a server is usually not your bug — it means a client vanished mid-request, which happens constantly on the internet as people close tabs and phones lose signal. Logging it at error level produces a stream of noise that hides real failures; log it at debug and count it as a metric.

EPIPE deserves care in the opposite direction. Writing to a closed socket raises BrokenPipeError in Python, but at the C level it also delivers SIGPIPE, whose default action is to kill the process. Python disables that for you; if you ever write network code in C, or shell out to something that does, the first thing to do is ignore SIGPIPE.

Exercises

Exercise 6.1 — Find the blocking call. An asyncio service handles 4000 connections and shows a p50 latency of 3 ms and a p99 of 900 ms, with CPU at 30% and no GC pauses. Explain the shape, list three likely causes, and give a way to find the culprit.

Solution

A blocking call inside the event loop. The shape is diagnostic: an overloaded server has a bad p50 and a bad p99. Here p50 is excellent, so the loop is normally fast — and then, occasionally, it stops entirely, and every one of the 4000 connections waits behind it. That is a single-threaded loop being held hostage, not a capacity problem. The 30% CPU confirms it: the machine is idle while requests wait, which only happens when something is blocked rather than computing.

The three likeliest causes, in the order I would check them:

  1. A synchronous library on a hot path. requests instead of httpx/aiohttp, a database driver whose async support is a thread-pool wrapper, or a cloud SDK that is synchronous underneath. This is by far the most common.
  2. DNS resolution. getaddrinfo is a blocking C call and is not made async by being called from a coroutine. asyncio's default resolver runs it in a thread pool, but a library that calls socket.getaddrinfo directly does not, and a slow or unreachable resolver then stalls the loop for the full DNS timeout (§7.9).
  3. CPU-bound work. Parsing a large JSON body, compressing a response, hashing a password with bcrypt. Correct code, wrong thread — a 200 ms bcrypt on the loop is 200 ms of latency for everyone.

How to find it, in increasing order of effort:

import asyncio

async def main():
    loop = asyncio.get_running_loop()
    loop.slow_callback_duration = 0.05   # default is 0.1 s
    ...                                  # start the service here

asyncio.run(main(), debug=True)          # debug mode logs any callback slower than the threshold

Note the shape. On Python 3.14, asyncio.get_event_loop() called outside a running loop raises RuntimeError: There is no current event loop instead of quietly creating one, so the older recipe of fetching the loop at module level fails before the service starts. Ask for the loop from inside a coroutine with asyncio.get_running_loop(), and turn debug mode on through asyncio.run(..., debug=True).

That names the offending coroutine directly and is usually enough. If it is not, sample the process with py-spy dump --pid <pid> repeatedly during a slow period — a stack that keeps showing the same synchronous frame is your answer. As a last resort, a watchdog task that sleeps 100 ms in a loop and logs when it wakes late measures loop lag directly and is worth having in production permanently.

The fix in every case is the same: move the blocking work off the loop with asyncio.to_thread (for I/O) or a process pool (for CPU), or replace the library with one that is genuinely async.

Exercise 6.2 — Why does the restart fail? A service is deployed with a rolling restart. Occasionally the new process fails at startup with [Errno 98] Address already in use and the deploy rolls back, even though the old process has definitely exited. The code does set SO_REUSEADDR. Explain, and give two fixes.

Solution

Two distinct causes produce this message, and they need different fixes.

Cause one — SO_REUSEADDR was added later. As §6.5 proves, Linux records the flag on the socket that entered TIME_WAIT, not on the one attempting to rebind. If the currently running build predates the fix, its lingering connections refuse reuse no matter what the new binary asks for. The symptom is characteristically self-healing: the deploy that introduces the option fails, and every subsequent one works.

Cause two, and the more likely one for an intermittent failure — the port is genuinely still in use. The old process exited, but a child it forked inherited the listening descriptor and is still holding it. A worker that did not get the shutdown signal, a subprocess spawned for a background job, or a supervisor that restarted a worker during shutdown will all do this. SO_REUSEADDR does not help here and should not: the port is held by a live LISTEN socket, not by a TIME_WAIT.

Distinguish them in one command, and the answer is unambiguous:

ss -tlnp '( sport = :8080 )'      # a LISTEN row with a pid  -> cause two
ss -tan  '( sport = :8080 )'      # only TIME-WAIT rows       -> cause one

The two fixes:

  1. Use SO_REUSEPORT rather than SO_REUSEADDR for this. It permits two live listeners on the same port, which turns the rolling restart from a race into a designed handover: the new process binds and starts accepting while the old one drains, then the old one exits. This removes the failure mode rather than shortening its window, and it removes the connection-refused gap between the two processes as well.
  2. Fix the descriptor inheritance. Open the listener with SOCK_CLOEXEC (Python sets close-on-exec by default, but a socket passed deliberately to a child does not have it), and make shutdown wait for children to exit rather than assuming they have.

A third option worth naming: hand the socket to the process from outside, via systemd socket activation or a supervisor. Then the listening socket outlives every restart, the port is never released, and this class of bug cannot occur.

Exercise 6.3 — Design the reads. You are writing a client for a service that streams newline-delimited JSON events over a long-lived TCP connection. Events arrive irregularly — sometimes hundreds per second, sometimes nothing for ten minutes. Specify the timeout, keep-alive and error-handling behaviour, and justify each choice.

Solution

The defining feature is that silence is normal, which rules out the obvious design. A read timeout cannot distinguish "no events right now" from "the connection is dead", so it must not be the mechanism that detects death.

Timeouts. No overall deadline — the connection is meant to live indefinitely. A read timeout, but a long one, set from the protocol's own liveness guarantee rather than from the event rate. If the server sends a heartbeat or comment line every 30 seconds (which SSE does by convention, §13 of the Backend course), set the read timeout to about 90 seconds: three missed heartbeats. If the server offers no such guarantee, ask for one — without it there is no correct value, and you are choosing between disconnecting healthy idle connections and failing to notice dead ones.

Keep-alives. Essential here, and for the middlebox reason rather than the peer-detection one (§4.7). A connection idle for ten minutes will be dropped by NATs and stateful firewalls along the path, and neither end is told. Set SO_KEEPALIVE with TCP_KEEPIDLE at 60 seconds, comfortably under the shortest NAT timeout you might meet. An application-level heartbeat is better still, because it proves the server is alive rather than its kernel, but the socket option costs one line and protects the path.

Framing. Buffer across reads and split on \n, exactly as in §3.6's exercise. A partial line must survive to the next read, and end-of-stream with a non-empty buffer is a truncation error, not a clean finish.

Errors and reconnection. Reconnect on every transport error, with jittered exponential backoff — 1 s, 2 s, 4 s, up to a cap of about 30 s, with full jitter so that a server restart does not bring every client back simultaneously. Two refinements that distinguish a real client:

  • Resume rather than restart. Track the last event id and send it on reconnect (Last-Event-ID for SSE, a cursor for anything else). Without it, a reconnect either loses events or replays them from the beginning.
  • Reset the backoff only after the connection has been healthy for a while — say 30 seconds — not immediately on connect. Otherwise a server that accepts connections and drops them instantly turns your backoff into a tight reconnect loop.

What to log. ECONNRESET and clean closes at debug level with a counter, since they are routine on a long-lived connection. Escalate on the rate: reconnecting twice an hour is normal, twice a minute is an incident.

SECTION 7

DNS

Every connection in this course started with an address, and every real one starts with a name. The Domain Name System is the distributed database that turns one into the other — and it is worth understanding properly, because it is the layer where a startling proportion of outages actually live. A DNS problem does not look like a DNS problem. It looks like an intermittently slow API, a service that works from one machine and not another, or a migration that was "done" six hours ago and still is not.

Terminology here follows RFC 9499, which exists precisely because the field had accumulated a decade of contradictory usage.

7.1 The Namespace Is a Tree

A domain name is a path through a tree, written leaf-first and separated by dots. www.example.com is three labels below an unnamed root, and the fully qualified form makes the root explicit with a trailing dot: www.example.com.

. (root)
13 root server names, hundreds of physical instances via anycast (§7.8)
com.
A top-level domain, operated by a registry — Verisign for com
example.com.
A zone: the unit of administrative control, with its own name servers
www.example.com.
A name within that zone — usually not a zone of its own

The distinction that matters is between a domain and a zone. A domain is everything below a name in the tree. A zone is the part of that a particular server is authoritative for — the domain minus anything delegated to someone else. com is an enormous domain and a tiny zone: the com servers hold almost no addresses, only delegations to 160 million other zones.

Labels are limited to 63 bytes and a full name to 255. Comparison is case-insensitive but case-preserving. And critically, the tree structure is not the administrative structure: co.uk is a registry boundary that DNS itself knows nothing about, which is why browsers ship the Public Suffix List — a hand-maintained file recording where "someone can register a name here" boundaries actually are, so a cookie set for co.uk cannot be read by every British website.

7.2 Resolvers, and Who Does the Work

Three kinds of participant, and confusing them is the source of most DNS confusion:

The stub resolver — the few hundred lines inside your operating system that getaddrinfo calls (§6.2). It knows almost nothing: it sends one query to a configured server and believes the answer. A stub resolver does not walk the tree and usually does not cache, which is why /etc/resolv.conf is the only configuration it has.
The recursive resolver — your ISP's, or 1.1.1.1, or a resolver inside your network. It does the actual work: walks the tree from the root, caches everything, and returns a final answer. Essentially all DNS caching lives in a recursive resolver, which is why its cache state, not yours, decides how fast a lookup is.
The authoritative name server — holds the actual zone data for some zone and answers only for it. An authoritative name server never recurses: it either answers from its own zone file or returns a referral, and it sets the aa flag when it does the former.

The root name servers sit at the top: thirteen names, a.root-servers.net through m.root-servers.net, held to thirteen because that was the most that fit in a 512-byte UDP response in 1995. A root name server holds only the delegations for the top-level domains — a few thousand records — and answers nothing else. There are far more than thirteen machines — each name is anycast to hundreds of sites worldwide (§7.8).

7.3 A Full Delegation Walk

dig +trace performs the recursion itself, showing each referral. This is a real walk, run while writing this section:

dig +trace +nodnssec example.com @1.1.1.1

.                       512154  IN  NS  a.root-servers.net.
.                       512154  IN  NS  b.root-servers.net.
                        ... (13 in total)
;; Received 239 bytes from 1.1.1.1#53(1.1.1.1) in 49 ms

com.                    172800  IN  NS  a.gtld-servers.net.
com.                    172800  IN  NS  b.gtld-servers.net.
                        ... (13 in total)
;; Received 836 bytes from 199.7.83.42#53(l.root-servers.net) in 9 ms

example.com.            172800  IN  NS  hera.ns.cloudflare.com.
example.com.            172800  IN  NS  elliott.ns.cloudflare.com.
;; Received 359 bytes from 192.41.162.30#53(l.gtld-servers.net) in 103 ms

example.com.            300     IN  A   104.20.23.154
example.com.            300     IN  A   172.66.147.243

Read it as three referrals and one answer. The root said "ask the com servers"; com said "ask Cloudflare"; Cloudflare gave the address. No server in that chain except the last one knew the answer, and none of them did any work on your behalf — each returned a referral and forgot you existed. That statelessness is what lets thirteen root server names serve the entire internet.

Two details worth extracting. The TTLs differ by three orders of magnitude: 512154 seconds (nearly six days) for the root NS records, 172800 (two days) for the delegations, and 300 (five minutes) for the address. Data that changes rarely is cached for a long time and the traffic never reaches the servers holding it; data that must be able to move quickly is cached briefly. That gradient is the whole reason the system scales, and choosing your own TTLs is choosing where on it you sit (§7.9).

There is a subtlety in that third referral worth spelling out, because it is a genuine chicken-and-egg problem. example.com's name servers are hera.ns.cloudflare.com and elliott.ns.cloudflare.com — names in a different zone, so resolving them is a separate lookup. But when a zone's name servers live inside the zone they serve — ns1.example.com serving example.com — the resolver must resolve ns1.example.com to ask it anything, and only ns1.example.com knows the answer.

The parent breaks the loop with a glue record: the com servers store the address of ns1.example.com alongside the delegation, even though they are not authoritative for it, and return it in the additional section. Without glue, an in-zone name server is unreachable and the whole domain fails to resolve. Two consequences follow. A glue record is the one piece of a zone's data held by the parent registry, so changing a name server's IP means updating it at the registrar, not in your own zone — a step that is routinely forgotten and produces a domain that resolves from cached answers and nowhere else. And glue is only needed for in-zone name servers, which is why using a provider's out-of-zone names (as example.com does above) sidesteps the problem entirely.

And note that this walk contradicts a fact many people still carry: example.com is served by Cloudflare and its long-familiar 93.184.216.34 address is gone. It is a small reminder that anything you "know" about a specific name should be checked rather than recalled.

The same run also produced this, which is §2.9's dual-stack failure appearing unbidden:

;; UDP setup with 2a06:98c1:50::ac40:23e4#53(2a06:98c1:50::ac40:23e4) for example.com failed: network unreachable.
;; no servers could be reached

The name servers have IPv6 addresses, this host has no IPv6 route, and dig tried them anyway before falling back. The failure was instant and harmless here. When the IPv6 path is partially broken instead of absent, the same situation costs a timeout per attempt and is one of the nastier DNS latency bugs.

7.4 Reading a Response

A DNS message has five sections, and dig prints them in order. The header line is the part to read first:

dig example.com A

;; ->>HEADER<<- opcode: QUERY, status: NOERROR, id: 48112
;; flags: qr rd ra; QUERY: 1, ANSWER: 2, AUTHORITY: 0, ADDITIONAL: 1

;; OPT PSEUDOSECTION:
; EDNS: version: 0, flags:; udp: 65494
;; QUESTION SECTION:
;example.com.                   IN      A

;; ANSWER SECTION:
example.com.            210     IN      A       104.20.23.154
example.com.            210     IN      A       172.66.147.243

;; Query time: 12 msec
;; SERVER: 127.0.0.53#53(127.0.0.53) (UDP)
  • status: NOERROR — the response code. The ones you will meet are NOERROR, NXDOMAIN (the name does not exist), SERVFAIL (the resolver tried and failed — often a DNSSEC validation failure or an unreachable authoritative server) and REFUSED (the server declines to answer you at all).
  • flags: qr rd ra — response, recursion desired, recursion available. An aa flag means the answer came from an authoritative server; its absence here means this came from a cache. An ad flag means DNSSEC validation succeeded (§7.7).
  • SERVER: 127.0.0.53 — the local stub resolver systemd-resolved, not a real name server. This is why the answer has no aa flag and why the TTL is not 300.

That last point deserves a demonstration, because it makes caching visible. Query the same name twice a few seconds apart:

dig +noall +answer example.com ; sleep 3 ; dig +noall +answer example.com

example.com.            83      IN      A       172.66.147.243
example.com.            83      IN      A       104.20.23.154
example.com.            79      IN      A       104.20.23.154
example.com.            79      IN      A       172.66.147.243

The TTL counted down from 83 to 79. That is a cache entry ageing in real time — the authoritative TTL is 300, this entry was created 217 seconds ago, and in 79 more seconds the next query will go back to Cloudflare. Watching the TTL is the fastest way to tell whether an answer came from a cache and how stale it is; a full-value TTL means you just refreshed it.

Note also that the two addresses swapped order between the two queries. That is round-robin rotation, and it is DNS's crudest form of load distribution: a client that uses only the first answer still spreads itself across both addresses. It is also why the getaddrinfo output in §6.2 could not be printed verbatim.

7.5 Record Types

A resource record is a (name, type, class, TTL, data) tuple. Class is essentially always IN. Records of the same name and type form an RRset, and a resource record is never returned alone from an RRset — all of them come together, which is why example.com returned both its addresses in §7.4. The types worth knowing:

Type Holds Notes
A An IPv4 address The common case
AAAA An IPv6 address Pronounced "quad-A". Its absence is not an error
CNAME Another name An alias. Cannot coexist with any other record at the same name
NS An authoritative name server Creates a delegation
MX A mail exchanger, with a priority Lowest priority number wins
TXT Arbitrary text SPF, DKIM, domain-ownership proofs
SOA Zone metadata One per zone; carries the negative-caching TTL
PTR A name, for reverse lookup Lives under in-addr.arpa / ip6.arpa
SRV Host and port for a service Used by SIP, XMPP, Kubernetes
CAA Which CAs may issue certificates Checked by the CA, not by clients (§8.9)
HTTPS / SVCB Connection hints: ALPN, port, IP hints Lets a client learn HTTP/3 support before connecting (§11.6)

The MX record deserves a note because it is the one record type whose data is a pair rather than a value, and because it is where the "DNS is just address lookup" model breaks down:

dig +noall +answer google.com MX

google.com.		300	IN	MX	10 smtp.google.com.

The 10 is a preference: a sender tries the lowest number first and falls back to higher ones, which is how mail survives a server outage without any client-side configuration. Two rules catch people out. An MX record must point at a hostname, never at an IP address and never at a CNAME — RFC 2181 forbids both, and violating it produces delivery failures that only some senders exhibit. And a domain with no MX at all falls back to its A record, which means an unconfigured domain silently accepts mail at its web server.

A CNAME is worth its own paragraph because of the constraint. An alias resolves to another name, which is then resolved in turn:

dig +noall +answer www.github.com

www.github.com.         389     IN      CNAME   github.com.
github.com.             35      IN      A       4.228.31.150

The resolver followed the alias and returned both records. The rule that trips people up is that a name with a CNAME may have no other records — no MX, no TXT, no NS. That is why you cannot put a CNAME at a zone apex (example.com itself), since the apex must carry SOA and NS. Every DNS provider has a proprietary workaround with a different name — ALIAS, ANAME, CNAME flattening — which resolves the target and serves the resulting A records directly.

The SOA record carries six numbers, and the last one is the one that will bite you:

dig +noall +answer example.com SOA

example.com.  1800  IN  SOA  elliott.ns.cloudflare.com. dns.cloudflare.com.
                             2411783310 10000 2400 604800 1800

In order: the primary server, the administrator's email (with @ written as a dot), the serial number, refresh, retry, expire, and minimum — which since RFC 2308 means the negative caching TTL. The field was originally a floor for positive TTLs and was redefined, so its name is misleading and its effect is not: negative caching is why a name you have just created stays missing. That last 1800 is how long resolvers will remember that a name does not exist. Creating a record after someone has queried for it means waiting out that timer, which is why the "I created the record and it still says NXDOMAIN" problem lasts exactly as long as the SOA minimum and not a second less.

7.6 Negative Answers: NXDOMAIN Versus NODATA

Two different "no", and only one of them is NXDOMAIN.

dig nope-xyzzy-98765.google.com

;; ->>HEADER<<- opcode: QUERY, status: NXDOMAIN, id: 62012
;; flags: qr rd ra; QUERY: 1, ANSWER: 0, AUTHORITY: 1, ADDITIONAL: 1
google.com.  59  IN  SOA  ns1.google.com. dns-admin.google.com. 968407802 900 900 1800 60

NXDOMAIN: the name does not exist at all, for any type. The SOA in the authority section tells the resolver how long to cache that fact — 60 seconds here, from the last field.

Now the same shape of query against a different zone:

dig nonexistent-xyzzy-12345.example.com

;; ->>HEADER<<- opcode: QUERY, status: NOERROR, id: 2501
;; flags: qr rd ra; QUERY: 1, ANSWER: 0, AUTHORITY: 1, ADDITIONAL: 1
example.com.  1800  IN  SOA  elliott.ns.cloudflare.com. dns.cloudflare.com. ...

NOERROR with zero answers — NODATA. The name exists; there are simply no records of the type asked for. Here it is because the zone has a wildcard, so every name under example.com exists. The same shape occurs constantly and legitimately when a host has an A record and no AAAA.

The distinction is not pedantry, because clients treat them differently. A resolver may keep looking on NODATA and will stop on NXDOMAIN; some libraries raise different exceptions; and a stub that receives NXDOMAIN for AAAA may conclude the whole name is gone. Historically that last one was a real interoperability bug, and it is why a correctly configured zone returns NODATA rather than NXDOMAIN for a missing AAAA.

Reverse lookups use the same machinery with the address reversed under a special suffix. 104.20.23.154 becomes 154.23.20.104.in-addr.arpa, and that is exactly the trick §2.5 used to look up an AS number. Reversal is required because DNS delegates left-to-right from the most general label, and IP addresses are written most-general-first.

7.7 Transport: UDP, TCP, EDNS and DoH

DNS runs over UDP port 53 by default: one query datagram, one response datagram, no handshake (§3.2). For a protocol whose entire job is one small request and one small response, TCP's round trip would double the cost of every lookup.

The original limit was 512 bytes. EDNS(0) (RFC 6891) raised it by having the client advertise a larger buffer in the OPT pseudo-section you saw in §7.4 — udp: 65494 there. That matters because DNSSEC signatures are large and modern responses routinely exceed 512 bytes.

But a large UDP response fragments (§2.10), and fragmented UDP is dropped by enough middleboxes to be unreliable. So there is a fallback: a server that cannot fit its answer sets the truncated flag, and the client retries the whole query over TCP port 53. Every DNS server must support both. The cost is a full TCP handshake plus the query, so a truncation costs roughly triple the latency of a normal lookup — which is why the DNS community has been shrinking advertised EDNS buffer sizes rather than raising them.

Plain DNS is also unauthenticated and unencrypted, which makes it both observable and forgeable. Three responses:

  • DNSSEC (RFC 4033–4035) signs records cryptographically, with a chain of trust from the root down. It provides authenticity — proof the answer came from the zone owner and was not modified — and no confidentiality whatsoever; the query and answer are still in plaintext. The ad flag in a response means your resolver validated it. Adoption is partial: most TLDs are signed, most second-level domains are not.
  • DNS over TLS (DoT), port 853, and DNS over HTTPS (DoH), port 443. Both encrypt the query between stub and recursive resolver, giving confidentiality against everyone on the path. Neither authenticates the data the way DNSSEC does — they secure the channel, not the record. DoH's use of port 443 makes it indistinguishable from other HTTPS traffic, which is a feature to its proponents and an objection for network operators who relied on seeing DNS.
  • DNS over QUIC (DoQ), RFC 9250, is the newest and gets DoT's properties without TCP's head-of-line blocking (§4.9).

None of these protects you from a resolver that is itself lying, which is what DNS-based filtering, captive portals and some corporate networks do by design.

7.8 Anycast

The thirteen root server names correspond to over a thousand physical servers. The mechanism is anycast: the same IP address is announced into BGP (§2.5) from many locations at once, and each router sends packets to whichever announcement is closest by its own routing metric. A query to 199.7.83.42 from São Paulo and one from Frankfurt reach entirely different machines.

Anycast is nearly ideal for DNS because DNS is stateless: any instance can answer any query, and if one disappears BGP reroutes to the next in seconds. It is what makes 1.1.1.1 and 8.8.8.8 respond in single-digit milliseconds almost everywhere on earth.

It is also the reason DNS-based failover is a weaker tool than people expect. Compare the two mechanisms honestly:

Anycast DNS failover
Failover time Seconds, at the routing layer TTL plus client cache, often much longer
Client cooperation needed None Must honour TTL — many do not
Works for stateful connections Existing connections may break on rerouting New connections only
Operational cost Needs an AS, BGP sessions, address space A record change

The row that ruins most plans is the second. Lowering a TTL to 60 seconds does not guarantee a 60-second failover, because Java's default InetAddress cache historically ignored TTL entirely, some corporate resolvers enforce a floor, and plenty of application code resolves once at startup and holds the address forever. A TTL is a hint, and the actual worst case is the most stubborn cache in your client population.

7.9 The Operational Reality

Four ways DNS causes production incidents, and what to do about each.

Lowering the TTL after you need it lowered. A TTL is honoured by caches that already hold the record, so if you cut it from 86400 to 60 an hour before a migration, some resolvers will still serve the old address for the remaining 23 hours. The rule: lower the TTL at least one old-TTL period before the change, then restore it afterwards. Write it into the runbook, because it is the step everyone forgets and its consequence is invisible until the cutover.

Resolution latency in the request path. An uncached lookup is one round trip to the recursive resolver plus, in the worst case, three more as it walks the tree. Ten to two hundred milliseconds, on the critical path of a request that has not yet started. Three mitigations: keep connections alive so you resolve rarely (§13.5), make sure your client library caches (many do not, or cache without honouring TTL), and use HTTPS/SVCB records so one lookup provides the protocol hints too.

The resolver is a single point of failure and is often forgotten. Every service in a cluster resolving through one local resolver means that resolver's failure is total. Its symptom is distinctive and easy to misread: everything fails at once, with connection errors rather than DNS errors, because most client libraries report the downstream failure rather than the resolution failure. Configure a second resolver, and check that your stub actually fails over to it rather than timing out first.

Search domains multiplying queries. /etc/resolv.conf may list search suffixes, and a name without a trailing dot gets tried against each in turn. Inside Kubernetes this is the classic one: ndots:5 means any name with fewer than five dots is tried against four search domains first, so resolving api.example.com from a pod issues five queries and gets four NXDOMAINs before the right answer. The fix is a trailing dot (api.example.com.) or an explicit ndots:1 in the pod spec — and the diagnostic is simply reading /etc/resolv.conf, which almost nobody thinks to do.

The commands worth having in your fingers:

dig +short example.com                  # just the answer
dig +trace example.com                  # walk the delegation yourself
dig @1.1.1.1 example.com                # bypass the local resolver — is it just us?
dig +noall +answer example.com          # watch the TTL count down
dig example.com SOA                     # find the negative-cache TTL
dig -x 104.20.23.154                    # reverse lookup
cat /etc/resolv.conf                    # what is this host actually configured to do
resolvectl statistics                   # cache hit rate, on systemd hosts

dig @1.1.1.1 is the single highest-value one. If a name resolves through a public resolver and not through yours, the problem is local — your resolver, its cache, or your search domains — and you have halved the search space in one command.

Exercises

Exercise 7.1 — Plan a migration. You are moving api.example.com to a new provider. The A record currently has a TTL of 86400. Write the sequence of steps with timings, and say what could still go wrong after you have followed it exactly.

Solution 
T-48h   Lower the TTL from 86400 to 300. Change nothing else.
T-24h   Every cache that held the old record has now expired it at least
        once and re-fetched with TTL 300. This wait is the whole point:
        the lowered TTL only takes effect after the *old* TTL elapses.
T-1h    Bring the new endpoint up and verify it directly by IP, with the
        Host header set — before any name points at it.
T-0     Change the A record. Old and new must both serve correctly now.
T+10m   Confirm propagation from several resolvers:
          dig @1.1.1.1 api.example.com +short
          dig @8.8.8.8 api.example.com +short
          dig @<your-authoritative-server> api.example.com +short
T+24h   Keep the old endpoint running and *watch its traffic*. Do not
        decommission on a schedule; decommission when the graph is zero.
T+48h   Restore the TTL to 86400 once the change is settled.

The T-48h step is the one that is always skipped and is the only one that matters. Lowering a TTL does not affect records already cached — those live out their original 86400 seconds. Lowering it one hour before the change buys nothing at all.

What can still go wrong after doing all of this correctly:

  • Clients that never re-resolve. A JVM with the historical networkaddress.cache.ttl of -1 caches forever; connection pools resolve once at creation; and plenty of application code does socket.gethostbyname at startup. These clients move when they restart and not before. This is why you watch the old endpoint's traffic instead of trusting the clock.
  • Resolvers with a TTL floor. Some enforce a minimum of five or ten minutes regardless of what you publish.
  • Negative caching. If anything queried the new name before it existed, NXDOMAIN is cached for the SOA minimum (§7.5) — 1800 seconds is a common default. Check that value before the migration, not during it.
  • A stale CNAME in the chain. If api.example.com is a CNAME to something else, the TTL that governs the change is the one on the record you actually altered, and the other one caches independently.

The general principle: DNS gives you no mechanism to invalidate a cache. You can only wait out the longest TTL you ever published, and hope every client honours it. Design cutovers so both endpoints work simultaneously, and you never need the guarantee DNS cannot give.

Exercise 7.2 — Read four failures. For each, say what the output means and what you would do next.

A)  ;; ->>HEADER<<- opcode: QUERY, status: SERVFAIL, id: 1234
B)  ;; ->>HEADER<<- opcode: QUERY, status: NOERROR, ANSWER: 0
    ;; AUTHORITY SECTION:  example.com.  1800  IN  SOA  ...
C)  dig works; the application reports "Name or service not known"
D)  ;; connection timed out; no servers could be reached
Solution

A — SERVFAIL: the resolver tried and could not produce an answer. It is a failure of the resolver, not a statement about the name, and it has three common causes: the authoritative servers are unreachable or all down; DNSSEC validation failed (a signature expired, or the chain is broken); or the zone's delegation is broken — for example the parent's NS records point somewhere that no longer serves the zone. Distinguish them:

dig @1.1.1.1 name.example        # another resolver: still SERVFAIL -> not local
dig +cd @1.1.1.1 name.example    # +cd disables DNSSEC checking; if this works, it is DNSSEC
dig +trace name.example          # find which referral in the chain breaks

An expired DNSSEC signature is a genuinely common cause of a whole domain vanishing, and +cd identifies it in one command.

B — NODATA, not NXDOMAIN (§7.6). The name exists; there are no records of the type you asked for. Almost always benign: an AAAA query against an IPv4-only host. It is a problem only if you expected the record to exist, in which case you created the wrong type — a CNAME where an A was needed, or a record on the wrong name.

C — dig and the application disagree, so they are doing different things. dig talks to the resolver directly; the application goes through getaddrinfo, which also consults /etc/hosts, /etc/nsswitch.conf and the search domains in /etc/resolv.conf (§7.9). Four candidates, in the order I would check them:

  • The name needs a search domain the application's environment does not have — very common between a host and a container, which have different resolv.conf files.
  • nsswitch.conf orders the sources differently, or an entry in /etc/hosts shadows DNS.
  • The application resolves AAAA only, or requires both families and one is missing.
  • The application runs in a different network namespace — a container, a chroot, a systemd unit with PrivateNetwork — and simply has a different resolver.

getent ahosts <name> is the right tool here, because it goes through exactly the same path the application does; dig does not.

D — nothing answered on port 53 at all. The resolver is down, unreachable, or being blocked. Try dig @1.1.1.1 to test a known-good target: if that works, your configured resolver is the problem; if it does not, port 53 is being blocked outbound, which is standard on networks that force you through their own resolver. Note that a captive portal produces this too, and so does a host with a resolv.conf pointing at 127.0.0.53 when systemd-resolved is not running.

Exercise 7.3 — Design the DNS for a service. You run an API with three regional deployments (Europe, North America, Asia). Choose the DNS design, specify TTLs, and say how failover works. Then say what you would use instead of DNS for the parts DNS does badly.

Solution

The design. One name, api.example.com, served by an authoritative provider that supports geographic or latency-based routing, returning the nearest regional endpoint. Behind each regional name, an anycast or load-balancer address rather than an individual host.

api.example.com          60    A/geo  -> nearest healthy region
api-eu.example.com       300   A      -> the European load balancer
api-na.example.com       300   A      -> the North American one
api-ap.example.com       300   A      -> the Asian one
example.com              3600  SOA    -> minimum 60  (negative-cache TTL)
example.com              86400 NS     -> delegation, changes almost never

The TTLs, and why each. 60 seconds on the routed name, because that is the record that must move during an incident. 300 on the regional names, which change only when infrastructure is rebuilt. 86400 on the NS records — they never change, and a long TTL keeps load off the parent. And 60 on the SOA minimum, which is the field everyone forgets: it governs how long an NXDOMAIN sticks, so a long value turns a five-minute mistake into a half-hour outage.

Failover, honestly. The provider health-checks each region and stops returning a failed one. Expected recovery is health-check interval plus TTL plus client cache — realistically two to five minutes, not sixty seconds, because of §7.8's stubborn caches. That is acceptable for a regional outage and far too slow for anything else.

What DNS does badly, and what to use instead:

  • Fast failover → anycast (§7.8). Reroutes in seconds at the routing layer with no client cooperation. This is the right tool if you can run BGP, and it is what a CDN gives you without having to (§12.6).
  • Load balancing → a real load balancer. Round-robin DNS distributes names, not load: it is blind to server capacity, current connections and health between checks, and client caching skews it badly.
  • Session affinity or connection-level control → the transport or application layer. DNS resolves once, and a long-lived connection stays where it landed no matter what the record says afterwards.
  • Rapid rollback → a feature flag or a load-balancer weight change, both of which take effect in seconds. A DNS rollback is subject to the same TTL as the change that caused the problem, so a bad DNS change cannot be undone quickly. This is the strongest argument for keeping DNS static and doing your traffic management one layer up.

The summary: use DNS to find the front door, and something else to decide what happens after that. DNS is a distributed cache with no invalidation, and every design that asks it to act quickly is fighting its architecture.

SECTION 8

TLS

Everything up to this point has been in plaintext, readable and modifiable by every device on the path. TLS is the layer that fixes that, and it does three things rather than one: it encrypts so nobody can read the traffic, it authenticates so you know who you are talking to, and it provides integrity so nobody can alter it undetected. The second is the hard one, and it is where the interesting failures live.

This section covers TLS 1.3, which is what you will actually negotiate. TLS 1.2 appears only where the difference matters.

A note on the specification, because it changed recently: TLS 1.3 was RFC 8446 (August 2018) and is now RFC 9846 (July 2026), which obsoletes it along with RFC 5246 (TLS 1.2) and four others. The revision tightens requirements and clarifies details rather than changing the protocol — it is fully backward compatible, and section numbering is unchanged — but RFC 8446 is the superseded document and citations should name RFC 9846.

8.1 What the Handshake Must Achieve

Before any application byte moves, two strangers on a hostile network must agree on a shared secret that an eavesdropper who saw every packet cannot compute, and at least one of them must prove who it is. TLS 1.3 does this in one round trip, and understanding it means understanding four separate mechanisms working together:

Key exchange — agree on a secret over a public channel. Elliptic-curve Diffie-Hellman: each side sends a public value, each combines it with its own private value, and both arrive at the same number nobody watching can derive.
Authentication — the server presents a certificate and signs part of the handshake with the matching private key. The signature proves it holds the key; the certificate chain proves someone you trust vouched for the name.
Key derivation — expand the shared secret into the several keys actually needed, via HKDF, with distinct keys per direction and per purpose.
Transcript binding — every handshake message is hashed into the key schedule, so an attacker who altered any of them produces different keys and the connection fails. This is what makes downgrade attacks detectable.

Note what is not in that list: the certificate plays no part in the key exchange. In TLS 1.3 (and in any TLS 1.2 suite worth using) the keys come from an ephemeral Diffie-Hellman exchange whose private values are discarded afterwards. That property is forward secrecy, and it is the single most valuable guarantee TLS 1.3 makes: an attacker who records today's traffic and steals the server's private key next year still cannot decrypt it, because that key was never able to decrypt it in the first place. Older RSA key-transport suites had no forward secrecy at all — the session key was encrypted to the server's long-term public key, so that one key decrypted every past session — and they are removed from TLS 1.3 entirely.

8.2 The 1-RTT Handshake

client
→ ClientHello
supported TLS versions · cipher suites · a key share already computed for its guessed group · SNI (which host) · ALPN (which protocol)
server
← ServerHello — its own key share, chosen suite. Both sides can now derive keys, so everything after this point is encrypted:
← EncryptedExtensions · Certificate · CertificateVerify (the signature) · Finished
client
→ Finished — and application data may be sent in the same flight

The trick that makes it one round trip is that the client guesses. Rather than asking which key-exchange group the server supports and then sending a share, it sends a share for its best guess immediately. If the guess is right — it nearly always is — the server can complete the exchange in its first reply. If wrong, the server sends a HelloRetryRequest naming the group it wants and the handshake costs two round trips instead. TLS 1.2 always cost two.

Here is a real handshake, from the machine this course was written on:

openssl s_client -connect example.com:443 -servername example.com </dev/null

Peer signing digest: SHA256
Peer signature type: ecdsa_secp256r1_sha256
Negotiated TLS1.3 group: X25519MLKEM768
SSL handshake has read 5079 bytes and written 1642 bytes
Verification: OK
New, TLSv1.3, Cipher is TLS_AES_256_GCM_SHA384
Protocol: TLSv1.3
This TLS version forbids renegotiation.
ALPN protocol: h2
Early data was not sent
Verify return code: 0 (ok)

Read the negotiated group carefully, because it is probably not what you expected: X25519MLKEM768 is a hybrid post-quantum key exchange. It performs an ordinary X25519 elliptic-curve exchange and an ML-KEM-768 lattice-based one, and mixes both results into the key schedule. The connection is secure if either survives — so it is no weaker than X25519 today, and it is not retroactively decryptable by a future quantum computer.

The reason this is deployed now rather than later is the "harvest now, decrypt later" threat: traffic recorded today can be stored until a machine exists that can break X25519, and forward secrecy does not help against that, because the ephemeral key itself is what would be broken. Anything whose confidentiality must outlive the next decade needs post-quantum key exchange today. As of 2026 this is the default on Chrome, Firefox and Cloudflare — note that it is deployed ahead of being standardised: RFC 9954 (July 2026, Informational) defines the general framework for hybrid key exchange in TLS 1.3, while the specific X25519MLKEM768 combination is still draft-ietf-tls-ecdhe-mlkem. That is an unusual ordering and a deliberate one, because the harvest-now threat does not wait for the standards process. If you write a section on TLS from memory you will get all of this wrong, which is the argument for running the command.

The other fields: TLS_AES_256_GCM_SHA384 is the cipher suite (§8.3), ALPN protocol: h2 is how HTTP/2 was selected (§8.6), the certificate was signed with ECDSA on P-256, and the whole handshake cost 5079 bytes down and 1642 up — mostly the certificate chain.

8.3 Cipher Suites and AEAD

A cipher suite names the algorithms for a connection. TLS 1.3 shortened the name dramatically because it removed the choices that were dangerous:

TLS 1.2: TLS_ECDHE_RSA_WITH_AES_128_GCM_SHA256
key exchange (ECDHE) · authentication (RSA) · cipher (AES-128-GCM) · hash (SHA256) — four independent choices, and dozens of combinations were insecure.
TLS 1.3: TLS_AES_256_GCM_SHA384
cipher and hash only. Key exchange is always ephemeral Diffie-Hellman and is negotiated separately; authentication is determined by the certificate. Five suites exist in total, and all five are safe.

Reducing the matrix from hundreds of combinations to five is arguably TLS 1.3's biggest practical security win. A generation of vulnerabilities — BEAST, CRIME, POODLE, Lucky13, FREAK, Logjam — came from a weak option being negotiable at all.

All TLS 1.3 ciphers are AEAD: authenticated encryption with associated data. One primitive both encrypts and authenticates, producing ciphertext plus a tag that fails to verify if a single bit was altered. This replaces the old "encrypt, then MAC separately" arrangement, whose subtly wrong orderings produced padding-oracle attacks for two decades. In an AEAD, tampering is not a decryption error to be handled — it is a fatal alert and the connection ends.

Underneath the handshake sits the record layer (RFC 9846 §5), which is the part of TLS that carries every byte after the keys are agreed. It fragments the application's stream into TLS records of at most 16,384 bytes, encrypts each one under the AEAD cipher, and prefixes a five-byte header giving the content type and length. Everything — handshake messages, application data, alerts — travels as records.

Three properties of the record layer have practical consequences. Each record is independently authenticated, so a receiver can detect tampering without buffering the whole stream — but it must also receive a complete record before it can decrypt anything, which means a 16 KB record adds a small latency floor and is why some servers reduce the record size for latency-sensitive traffic. The record header's content type is encrypted in TLS 1.3 (the outer type always reads application_data), so an observer cannot tell a handshake message from a data record. And record boundaries do not align with your send() calls any more than TCP segment boundaries do (§3.6) — TLS gives you an encrypted byte stream, not encrypted messages, and framing remains your problem.

8.4 Certificates and the Chain

A certificate is a signed statement: this public key belongs to this name, signed by someone else's private key. An X.509 certificate wraps that in a structure carrying validity dates, the permitted names, the key usage and extensions.

An X.509 certificate is the format ITU-T defined in 1988 and everyone still uses; its extensions — the SAN list, key usage, the SCTs of §8.9 — carry all the modern content, and the base X.509 fields are largely vestigial. The signer is a certificate authority, and its own certificate is signed in turn, up to a root that is not signed by anyone — it is trusted because a copy of it ships in your operating system or browser. Here is the real chain from §8.2, four deep:

 0 s:CN=example.com
   i:C=US, O=SSL Corporation, CN=Cloudflare TLS Issuing ECC CA 3
   a:PKEY: EC, (prime256v1); sigalg: ecdsa-with-SHA256
   v:NotBefore: Jul 29 22:10:08 2026 GMT; NotAfter: Oct 27 22:17:21 2026 GMT
 1 s:C=US, O=SSL Corporation, CN=Cloudflare TLS Issuing ECC CA 3
   i:C=US, O=SSL Corporation, CN=SSL.com TLS Transit ECC CA R2
 2 s:C=US, O=SSL Corporation, CN=SSL.com TLS Transit ECC CA R2
   i:C=US, O=SSL Corporation, CN=SSL.com TLS ECC Root CA 2022
 3 s:C=US, O=SSL Corporation, CN=SSL.com TLS ECC Root CA 2022
   i:C=GB, ST=Greater Manchester, ..., CN=AAA Certificate Services

Each entry's issuer (i:) is the next entry's subject (s:) — that is what makes it a certificate chain. Validation walks the certificate chain upward until it reaches a certificate in the local trust store, checking at every step that the signature verifies, the dates are valid, the issuer was permitted to issue, and the certificate has not been revoked.

Three things about that chain are worth noticing:

  • Roots are kept offline. A root's private key signs only intermediate certificates, rarely, in a ceremony. An intermediate can be revoked and replaced; a compromised root would have to be removed from every device on earth. This is why a certificate authority operates at least two tiers, and why the number of trusted roots on your machine (a few hundred) is far smaller than the number of issuing CAs.
  • The server must send the intermediates, because the certificate chain is assembled from what arrives on the wire plus what is already trusted locally. Only the root is in your trust store, so a server that sends its leaf alone produces the classic "works in my browser, fails in curl" bug — browsers often cache intermediates or fetch them via the CA Issuers URL, and most other clients do neither. Test with openssl s_client, which shows exactly what was sent.
  • The last entry is cross-signed. SSL.com TLS ECC Root CA 2022 is issued by an older Comodo root, so devices whose trust stores predate 2022 can still validate the chain by continuing one step further. Cross-signing is how a new CA becomes usable before its root has propagated to every device — and how Let's Encrypt bootstrapped itself.

The name check is a separate step from the chain check, and it uses the Subject Alternative Name extension, not the Common Name:

openssl s_client -connect example.com:443 -servername example.com </dev/null 2>/dev/null \
  | openssl x509 -noout -dates -subject -ext subjectAltName,authorityInfoAccess

notBefore=Jul 29 22:10:08 2026 GMT
notAfter=Oct 27 22:17:21 2026 GMT
subject=CN=example.com
Authority Information Access:
    CA Issuers - URI:http://i.cf-i.ssl.com/Cloudflare-TLS-I-E3.cer
    OCSP - URI:http://o.cf-i.ssl.com
X509v3 Subject Alternative Name:
    DNS:example.com, DNS:*.example.com

CN has been deprecated for name matching since RFC 2818 and is ignored entirely by modern clients; only the SAN list counts. A certificate with the right CN and a SAN list that omits the name will fail, which is a confusing error if you are reading the subject line and believing it.

Note the validity window: 90 days. Short lifetimes are now the norm, because they make revocation (§8.9) far less important — a compromised key expires on its own before most revocation mechanisms would have propagated. The direction is fixed and dated: CA/Browser Forum ballot SC-081v3 steps the maximum validity down in stages to 47 days from 15 March 2029, and Let's Encrypt intends to reach 45 days by February 2028. At that length, manual issuance is not merely inconvenient — it is impossible, which is the point.

8.5 SNI: One Address, Many Certificates

The server must send a certificate before it knows which site you want — the HTTP Host header is inside the encrypted stream, and the certificate comes before it. Without help, one IP address could serve exactly one HTTPS site.

Server Name Indication solves it by putting the hostname in the ClientHello, in plaintext, before encryption begins. Server Name Indication was added in 2003 and is now assumed by every server hosting more than one name — omitting it does not fail cleanly, it silently returns the wrong certificate. The server reads it and selects the matching certificate. This is what makes shared hosting and CDNs possible at all.

The cost is that SNI leaks which site you are visiting to anyone on the path, even though everything else is encrypted. It is the single largest metadata leak remaining in HTTPS, and it is what most national-scale filtering actually inspects. Encrypted Client Hello (ECH) fixes it by encrypting the whole ClientHello to a public key published in the domain's HTTPS DNS record (§7.5), so the outer handshake reveals only a generic front-end name. It is deployed by Cloudflare and Firefox and is not yet universal — and it depends on encrypted DNS (§7.7), since otherwise the DNS query leaks the same name.

Two operational consequences of SNI:

  • A client that omits SNI gets the server's default certificate, which is usually the wrong one. This is a common cause of "certificate name mismatch" from scripts and health checks — curl --resolve and openssl s_client -servername exist precisely to control it.
  • SNI and Host can disagree. Setting them to different values is domain fronting: connect to one site's infrastructure and address a request to another. It was used to circumvent censorship, was disabled by the major CDNs around 2018, and is worth knowing about because it explains why they now validate that the two match.

8.6 ALPN

Application-Layer Protocol Negotiation is a TLS extension carrying a list of protocol identifiers. The client offers ["h2", "http/1.1"], the server picks one, and the choice is settled inside the handshake that was happening anyway — costing zero extra round trips. That is the whole reason HTTP/2 could be deployed: an Upgrade-based negotiation (§9.7) would have cost a round trip per connection and would have been mangled by intermediaries.

The identifiers you will see: http/1.1, h2 (HTTP/2 over TLS), h3 (HTTP/3 over QUIC), and h2c for the cleartext variant that ALPN never carries. In the §8.2 transcript, ALPN protocol: h2 is the line that determined everything §10 discusses.

8.7 Resumption, Tickets and 0-RTT

A full handshake costs a round trip and a public-key operation. Session resumption avoids both for a client that has connected before. TLS 1.2 had two incompatible mechanisms for it — server-side session caches and client-held tickets — and TLS 1.3 replaced both with one, so session resumption now means exactly one thing.

TLS 1.3 unifies the older mechanisms into one: after a handshake the server may send a session ticket — an opaque blob, encrypted with a key only the server knows, containing enough state to resume. The client stores it and presents it on the next connection as a pre-shared key. TLS 1.3 uses the same pre-shared key machinery for resumption and for genuinely out-of-band shared secrets, which is why the two look identical on the wire. The server decrypts its own ticket, recovers the secret, and skips certificate verification entirely.

The cost of a handshake is easy to see:

for i in 1 2; do
  curl -sS -o /dev/null -w 'dns=%{time_namelookup} tcp=%{time_connect} tls=%{time_appconnect} total=%{time_total}\n' \
       https://example.com
done

dns=0.011512 tcp=0.080238 tls=0.801796 ttfb=0.862000 total=0.864046
dns=0.022937 tcp=0.071126 tls=0.311704 ttfb=0.385249 total=0.387084

Both are full handshakes — curl exits between runs, so nothing is resumed — and the difference between them is instructive on its own: the TLS phase took 722 ms the first time and 241 ms the second, on the same path with the same TCP connect time. The first run paid for a cold CDN edge, a cold OCSP or intermediate fetch, and cold CPU caches. This is why a single latency measurement of a TLS endpoint is close to meaningless, and why §13's advice is always about eliminating handshakes rather than making them faster.

0-RTT (RFC 9846 §8) goes one step further: the client sends application data in its first flight, encrypted with a key derived from the ticket, before the handshake completes. It saves the entire round trip.

It has a real and unavoidable weakness. Early data is replayable — an attacker who captures that first flight can send it again to the same server, which cannot distinguish it from the original, because there has been no round trip to establish freshness. The rule is therefore strict: 0-RTT is only safe for idempotent requests (§2 of the Backend course). A GET is fine; a POST that charges a card is not. In practice, servers restrict early data to safe methods and clients must not retry non-idempotent requests in it. Note the transcript above reports Early data was not sent — this is not enabled by default anywhere, and enabling it is a decision about your own endpoints' idempotency.

8.8 mTLS

Normally only the server authenticates. Mutual TLS has the client present a certificate too: the server sends a CertificateRequest, the client replies with its certificate and a signature proving it holds the key.

This is the standard authentication mechanism for service-to-service traffic inside a cluster, and it is genuinely stronger than a bearer token (§8 of the Backend course) in one specific way: the credential is never transmitted. A token is a secret that travels on every request and can be stolen from a log, a proxy or a memory dump. A client certificate's private key never leaves the client; only a signature over this connection's transcript does, and it is useless anywhere else.

The cost is certificate management for every client — issuance, distribution, rotation, revocation — which is why mTLS is common inside service meshes that automate it (Istio, Linkerd, SPIFFE) and rare on the public internet, where the enrolment problem has no good answer.

8.9 Revocation, Transparency and Issuance

Revocation is the part of PKI that never worked properly. A certificate says "valid until October"; if the key is stolen in August, something must tell clients to stop trusting it, and every mechanism for doing so has failed in a different way:

  • CRLs — a list of revoked serials, downloaded by the client. They grew to megabytes and clients stopped fetching them.
  • OCSP — ask the CA about one certificate at connection time. It adds a round trip to a third party, leaks your browsing to the CA, and — fatally — clients soft-fail when the responder is unreachable, so an attacker who can block OCSP has defeated it entirely. You can see the responder URL in the authorityInfoAccess output in §8.4. Let's Encrypt stopped including OCSP URLs in its certificates on 7 May 2025 and shut the responder down on 6 August 2025.
  • OCSP stapling — the server fetches the signed OCSP response periodically and includes it in the handshake. This fixes the privacy and latency problems, but "stapling is optional" means its absence proves nothing.
  • Short lifetimes — the mechanism that actually works, and where the industry has landed. A 90-day certificate that cannot be revoked is a bounded problem; a 3-year one is not.
  • Browser-pushed lists — CRLite, CRLSets: aggregate revocations compressed and shipped with the browser. Effective, and only available to browsers.

Certificate Transparency attacks a different problem: not "was this revoked" but "was this issued at all". Certificate Transparency works because detection is sufficient — a CA that cannot issue secretly is a CA that will not issue maliciously twice. Every publicly trusted certificate must be logged in append-only public logs, and the proofs of logging (SCTs) are embedded in the certificate — they are the reason the example.com certificate in §8.4 is 3.6 KB rather than 800 bytes. Chrome and Safari reject certificates without them. The effect is that a CA cannot secretly issue a certificate for your domain: it will appear in a public log, and services like crt.sh will let you find it. Monitoring CT logs for your own domains is a genuinely useful and rarely implemented control.

One more header belongs in this list because it is the only defence against an attacker who simply prevents TLS from being used at all. HSTS — Strict-Transport-Security — tells a browser to refuse plaintext to this host for a stated duration, so a http:// link is rewritten locally with no network request (§13.3 counts what that saves). HSTS has a bootstrap problem, since the very first visit is unprotected, and the answer is the preload list compiled into browsers.

Issuance itself is now automated by ACME (RFC 8555), the protocol behind Let's Encrypt. The client proves control of a domain by serving a token at a well-known HTTP path or publishing it as a DNS TXT record, and receives a certificate. The TXT record challenge is the one to prefer: it works for wildcard certificates, which the HTTP challenge cannot issue, and it needs no inbound connectivity to the host being certified. The CAA record (§7.5) is the complementary control: it names which CAs may issue for your domain, and CAs are required to check it. Together they mean certificate issuance can be fully automated and constrained, which is what makes 90-day lifetimes practical.

8.10 What Goes Wrong

Symptom Cause Diagnostic
certificate has expired Nobody renewed it, or renewal automation broke silently openssl x509 -noout -dates; alert at 21 days, not 7
unable to get local issuer certificate The server did not send intermediates (§8.4) openssl s_client and count the chain
certificate name mismatch SAN does not cover the name, or SNI was not sent (§8.5) -servername explicitly; read SAN, not CN
handshake failure with no detail No overlap in versions or suites — an old client against a modern server openssl s_client -tls1_2 to test
Works in browser, fails in the app Different trust store; browsers are more forgiving about intermediates Compare against curl, which uses the system store
