Intermittent failures across a fleet One server in a pool has a stale or wrong certificate Loop over the backends by IP with --resolve

The one that causes the most damage is the first, and it is worth being blunt about: certificate expiry is a scheduled, fully predictable outage that takes down services regularly at every scale. Everything needed to prevent it — the expiry date, in machine-readable form, in the handshake — is available continuously. Monitor the live endpoint rather than your issuance records, because the commonest form of certificate expiry in practice is not a forgotten renewal but a renewed certificate that was never deployed, and only the endpoint knows the difference.

Exercises

Exercise 8.1 — Count the round trips. A client 90 ms away makes an HTTPS request. Compute the time to first byte for: (a) a cold connection with TLS 1.3, (b) a cold connection with TLS 1.2, (c) a resumed TLS 1.3 connection, (d) a resumed connection with 0-RTT, (e) an existing keep-alive connection. Assume DNS is cached and the server takes 5 ms.

Solution 
rtt, server = 90, 5
scenarios = [
    ("cold, TLS 1.3",        1 + 1 + 1),   # TCP + TLS + request
    ("cold, TLS 1.2",        1 + 2 + 1),   # TLS 1.2 needs two round trips
    ("resumed, TLS 1.3",     1 + 1 + 1),   # ticket still costs one flight
    ("resumed, 0-RTT",       1 + 0 + 1),   # data rides in the first flight
    ("keep-alive, warm",     0 + 0 + 1),   # nothing but the request
]
for name, rtts in scenarios:
    print(f"{name:<20}{rtts} RTT{'s' if rtts != 1 else ' '}  ->  {rtts * rtt + server:>3} ms")

cold, TLS 1.3       3 RTTs  ->  275 ms
cold, TLS 1.2       4 RTTs  ->  365 ms
resumed, TLS 1.3    3 RTTs  ->  275 ms
resumed, 0-RTT      2 RTTs  ->  185 ms
keep-alive, warm    1 RTT   ->   95 ms

The result that surprises people is that resumption does not save a round trip in TLS 1.3. The resumed handshake is still one flight each way — the ticket replaces the certificate and the signature, saving bytes and CPU but not latency. Only 0-RTT saves the round trip, and it comes with the replay constraint of §8.7.

The ranking of what to do about it: a warm connection beats every handshake optimisation combined (275 → 95 ms, a 2.9× improvement), which is why §13.5 is about pooling. After that, upgrading any remaining TLS 1.2 endpoint is a free 90 ms. 0-RTT is worth 90 ms more but only for idempotent requests, and HTTP/3 (§11) folds the transport and TLS handshakes together to reach the same 185 ms without the replay caveat.

Note also what the measured data in §8.7 shows: real cold handshakes took 722 ms and 241 ms of TLS time on a 46 ms path — far more than the 90 ms of pure round trip this model predicts. Modelled round trips are a floor, not a forecast.

Exercise 8.2 — Debug five certificate failures. For each, give the likely cause and the command that confirms it.

  1. curl fails with unable to get local issuer certificate; the browser is fine.
  2. A health check fails with a name mismatch; a browser request to the same URL succeeds.
  3. The site works, but only from machines that have been running for months.
  4. One request in twenty fails with an expiry error; the rest succeed.
  5. openssl s_client succeeds and the application still refuses to connect.
Solution

1 — Missing intermediates. The server sends its leaf and nothing else. Browsers often survive because they cache intermediates from other sites or fetch them from the CA Issuers URL; curl does neither.

openssl s_client -connect host:443 -servername host </dev/null 2>/dev/null | grep -c '^ [0-9] s:'

One entry means the leaf alone. Fix on the server by concatenating the intermediates into the served chain.

2 — SNI is not being sent (§8.5), so the server returned its default certificate. Health checkers that connect by IP, or old clients, commonly omit it.

openssl s_client -connect 1.2.3.4:443 </dev/null 2>/dev/null | openssl x509 -noout -subject
openssl s_client -connect 1.2.3.4:443 -servername host </dev/null 2>/dev/null | openssl x509 -noout -subject

Two different subjects proves it. Configure the checker to send SNI, or use curl --resolve host:443:1.2.3.4.

3 — A stale trust store, or a cross-signed root that expired. Long-running machines have not updated ca-certificates, and a root the chain depends on may have expired — the AddTrust expiry in 2020 broke a large fraction of the internet exactly this way, and the §8.4 chain's fourth entry shows the same cross-signing pattern that caused it. Check the expiry dates of the whole chain, not just the leaf:

openssl s_client -connect host:443 -servername host -showcerts </dev/null 2>/dev/null \
  | awk '/BEGIN CERT/,/END CERT/' | openssl storeutl -noout -text /dev/stdin | grep -E 'Not After|Subject:'

4 — One backend out of a pool has a different certificate. A load balancer with twenty servers where one missed the deployment produces exactly a 1-in-20 failure rate. Test each backend individually:

for ip in $(dig +short host); do
  echo -n "$ip: "
  curl -sS --resolve "host:443:$ip" -o /dev/null -w '%{ssl_verify_result}\n' https://host || true
done

5 — The application has its own trust store or its own rules. Java uses cacerts, not the system store; Node uses a bundled list unless NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS is set; Python uses certifi. It may also pin a certificate or key, require a minimum TLS version, or reject a hostname pattern openssl accepts. Compare directly:

python3 -c "import certifi, ssl; print(certifi.where()); print(ssl.OPENSSL_VERSION)"
curl -v https://host 2>&1 | grep -E 'CAfile|SSL connection'

The general method: openssl s_client shows what the server sends; the failure is then either in the chain the server sent or in the trust store of the specific client that is failing. Establishing which of those two it is takes one command and eliminates half the possibilities.

Exercise 8.3 — Design TLS for a service. You are deploying an internal API called by twelve services inside a cluster and by a mobile app over the internet. Specify the TLS configuration for both paths, and justify each decision.

Solution

These are two different problems and should not share a configuration. The internal path controls both ends; the public path controls neither the client nor the network.

Public path (mobile app):

  • TLS 1.3 only if the app's minimum OS version allows it, otherwise 1.3 with 1.2 as a floor and only AEAD suites. A mobile app is one of the rare cases where you know your client population and can drop 1.2 entirely.
  • A publicly trusted certificate, issued by ACME with automated renewal (§8.9), 90-day lifetime, and a CAA record restricting issuance to that CA.
  • HSTS with a long max-age and includeSubDomains, so a downgrade to plaintext is refused by the client before a request is made.
  • Do not pin certificates in the app. Pinning is widely recommended and mostly a mistake: it converts a certificate-rotation error into a bricked app that cannot be fixed remotely, and it fights the short-lifetime model that is now standard. If a threat model genuinely requires it, pin the CA rather than the leaf, ship at least two pins, and build a remote kill switch first.
  • Monitor the live endpoint's expiry, not the issuance pipeline (§8.10).

Internal path (service to service):

  • mTLS, with certificates issued by an internal CA and rotated automatically — hours, not months. This is the case where mTLS's advantage is real: the credential never travels (§8.8), and the enrolment problem that makes it impractical publicly is solved by the cluster's own identity system.
  • Short-lived identity certificates carrying the workload identity (a SPIFFE ID or equivalent) rather than a hostname, so authorisation can be based on what the caller is rather than on where it connected from.
  • Verify the peer identity in the application, not only at the TLS layer. A valid certificate from your own CA proves the caller is in the cluster, not that it is allowed to call this endpoint — that is authorisation, and §9 of the Backend course argues why it belongs on every path.
  • Let the mesh or sidecar terminate it if you have one. Hand-rolling mTLS in twelve services means twelve rotation bugs.

What is shared: no TLS 1.0/1.1, no RSA key transport, no compression, AEAD ciphers only, and forward secrecy everywhere — none of which requires any thought in TLS 1.3, because it removed every alternative.

The decision worth arguing over is whether internal traffic needs TLS at all, given a private network. It does. The network is not a boundary (§1.4's ARP has no authentication, and neither does anything else at that layer), an internal compromise should not yield plaintext traffic for every service, and mTLS gives you authentication you would otherwise have to build with tokens. The cost is CPU that has been negligible since AES-NI, and operational complexity that a mesh absorbs.

SECTION 9

HTTP/1.1 on the Wire

The Backend & API Design course treats HTTP as a contract: what PUT promises, what 409 claims, how Cache-Control works. This section treats it as bytes, because the framing rules — how a receiver decides where one message ends and the next begins — are where the interoperability bugs and an entire class of security vulnerabilities live.

HTTP/1.1 is specified in RFC 9112 (June 2022), which replaced RFC 7230 and is short and readable. Its most important property is one you can see with your own eyes: it is text, line-oriented, and you can speak it by hand.

9.1 A Complete Exchange, By Hand

There is no substitute for typing the protocol yourself. This is a real request, made by piping raw bytes into an openssl TLS tunnel:

printf 'GET / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: example.com\r\nUser-Agent: curl/8\r\nConnection: close\r\n\r\n' \
  | openssl s_client -connect example.com:443 -servername example.com -quiet

HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Date: Mon, 24 Aug 2026 02:27:03 GMT
Content-Type: text/html
Transfer-Encoding: chunked
Connection: close
Server: cloudflare
last-modified: Tue, 18 Aug 2026 20:06:42 GMT
allow: GET, HEAD
Accept-Ranges: bytes
Age: 10964
cf-cache-status: HIT
CF-RAY: a2feea69eddf5326-GRU

22f
<!doctype html><html lang="en"><head><title>Example Domain</title>...</html>

0

Everything about HTTP/1.1 is visible in those bytes:

  • A request is a request line, then field lines, then a blank line, then an optional body. The blank line is the only delimiter between the header section and the body, and it is \r\n\r\n.
  • Line endings are CRLF, not LF. Writing \n instead of \r\n produces a request that many servers accept and some reject, which is a wonderful source of "works locally, fails in production".
  • The Host header is mandatory in HTTP/1.1 and its absence is a 400. It is what allows one address to serve many sites — the same problem SNI solves one layer down (§8.5).
  • The response is a status line, field lines, a blank line, and a body. Same shape. The status line is the only part that differs structurally from a request: version, code, reason phrase, in that order.
  • 22f and 0 are not part of the document. They are chunk framing, and §9.4 explains them.

Note the header case is inconsistent — Date and Content-Type capitalised, last-modified and allow lowercase. Field names are case-insensitive (RFC 9112 §5), so both are valid and a receiver must treat them identically. Hold that observation; §10.2 makes it interesting.

9.2 The Request Line and the Request Target

GET / HTTP/1.1

Three tokens separated by single spaces: the method, the request target, and the version. The target has four forms, and knowing them explains several confusing behaviours:

Form Example Used for
origin-form GET /path?q=1 HTTP/1.1 Almost everything. Path and query only; the host is in the Host header
absolute-form GET http://example.com/path HTTP/1.1 Requests to a forward proxy, which needs the whole URL to know where to go (§12.2)
authority-form CONNECT example.com:443 HTTP/1.1 Only CONNECT, to open a tunnel (§12.3)
asterisk-form OPTIONS * HTTP/1.1 Server-wide options, rather than options for a resource

Only a forward proxy normally receives absolute-form, because only it has to decide which server to contact. A server must accept it even in a direct request, and must prefer it over Host when both are present — a rule that exists for proxy compatibility and is a reliable source of request-smuggling variants (§9.8).

The status line is the mirror image: HTTP/1.1 200 OK. Its reason phrase (OK) is decorative, may be any text or empty, and must never be parsed — a client that branches on it rather than on the numeric code will break the first time it meets a server that writes HTTP/1.1 200 with nothing after it, which is legal.

9.3 Field Lines

Content-Type: text/html

A name, a colon, optional whitespace, and a value running to the CRLF. The rules that matter in practice:

  • Names are case-insensitive, values generally are not.
  • A field may appear more than once, and the receiver must treat repeats as a comma-separated list — with one exception that ruins the rule, Set-Cookie, which may not be joined because its value contains commas in dates. Every HTTP library has a special case for this.
  • Leading and trailing whitespace in the value is stripped. Internal whitespace is preserved.
  • Obsolete line folding — continuing a value on the next line with leading whitespace — was permitted and is now forbidden by RFC 9112 §5.2. A receiver must reject it, or replace it with spaces if acting as a proxy. It is forbidden because parsers disagreed about it, which is the theme of this entire section.
  • There is no length limit in the specification, and every implementation imposes one anyway — commonly 8 KB per line and 8–16 KB total. Exceeding it gives 431 Request Header Fields Too Large, or, less helpfully, a dropped connection.

Two categories of field are worth distinguishing because they behave differently along the path. End-to-end fields are forwarded by every intermediary. Hop-by-hop fields apply only to the current connection and must be removed before forwarding: Connection, Keep-Alive, Transfer-Encoding, TE, Upgrade, Proxy-Authorization. Any field named in the Connection header also becomes hop-by-hop, which is a mechanism for extending the list — and, inevitably, another smuggling vector.

9.4 Message Body Length: The Central Problem

TCP is a stream with no message boundaries (§3.6), so a receiver must be told where the body ends. HTTP/1.1 has exactly three ways to say it, and the order in which they are consulted is the single most security-relevant rule in the protocol.

1. Transfer-Encoding: chunked — the body is a sequence of self-describing chunks. Wins over everything else if present.
2. Content-Length — exactly that many bytes follow.
3. Connection close — the body ends when the connection does. Responses only; a request framed this way could never be answered.

RFC 9112 calls the first of these a transfer coding — a transformation applied to the message body for transport, distinct from a Content-Encoding like gzip, which is a property of the representation and survives end to end. The chunked transfer coding is the only one in general use; gzip and deflate are registered as transfer codings too and are almost never sent that way. The practical distinction is that a proxy may re-frame a chunked transfer into a Content-Length body without altering the message, and may not touch a Content-Encoding at all.

Chunked framing is what the transcript in §9.1 shows. Each chunk is a hexadecimal length, CRLF, that many bytes, CRLF; a zero-length chunk ends the body:

def parse_chunked(raw: bytes) -> tuple[bytes, int]:
    """Decode a chunked body. Returns (body, chunk_count)."""
    body, count, pos = b"", 0, 0
    while True:
        end = raw.index(b"\r\n", pos)
        size = int(raw[pos:end].split(b";")[0], 16)     # ';' introduces chunk extensions
        if size == 0:
            return body, count
        start = end + 2
        body += raw[start:start + size]
        pos = start + size + 2                          # skip the chunk's trailing CRLF
        count += 1

wire = (b"22f\r\n" + b"A" * 559 + b"\r\n"
        b"1a\r\n" + b"B" * 26 + b"\r\n"
        b"0\r\n\r\n")
body, n = parse_chunked(wire)
print(f"{n} chunks, {len(body)} bytes total")
print(f"first chunk length 0x22f = {0x22f} bytes, second 0x1a = {0x1a}")
print(f"body starts {body[:8]!r} and ends {body[-8:]!r}")

2 chunks, 585 bytes total
first chunk length 0x22f = 559 bytes, second 0x1a = 26
body starts b'AAAAAAAA' and ends b'BBBBBBBB'

That 0x22f is exactly the number from the real capture. Chunked encoding exists so a server can start sending before it knows the total size — a streamed database export, a generated report, a proxied response whose upstream length is unknown. It also permits a trailer section after the final chunk, carrying fields that could only be computed after the body: a checksum, a row count, a gRPC status.

Content-Length is simpler and is what you should use whenever the size is known: it lets the receiver allocate correctly, allows HEAD to be answered honestly, and is required for range requests.

Connection close is HTTP/1.0's mechanism and survives as the fallback. It has an unfixable flaw: the receiver cannot distinguish a complete response from a truncated one. Both look like "bytes, then EOF". This is precisely the failure discussed in §13 of the Backend course, and it is why a streamed export should use chunked framing — a missing terminal chunk is detectable.

Now the security-relevant part. What if a message has both Content-Length and Transfer-Encoding? RFC 9112 §6.1 is unambiguous: Transfer-Encoding wins, and the Content-Length must be removed or the message rejected. In practice, servers disagreed for years — and when two servers on the same path disagree, you get request smuggling.

9.5 Persistent Connections

In HTTP/1.0 every request opened a new TCP connection and closed it afterwards. At three round trips of setup (§5.1) per request, a page with forty resources spent almost all of its time handshaking.

HTTP/1.1 made persistent connections the default. A connection stays open after a response and the next request reuses it; Connection: close on either side signals the end. This is the single largest performance change in HTTP's history, and everything in §13.5 about connection pools depends on it.

The rules:

  • Persistence is the default. A server that wants to close says so; the Connection: keep-alive header is an HTTP/1.0 compatibility artefact and means nothing in 1.1.
  • A message must be framed (§9.4) for the connection to be reusable — the receiver has to know where the response ended in order to send the next request. This is why "close-delimited" and "persistent" are mutually exclusive.
  • Either side may close at any time, including in the gap between your last read and your next write. A client must therefore be prepared for a request on an idle pooled connection to fail immediately, and retry it on a fresh connection. Nearly every mysterious intermittent ECONNRESET in an HTTP client pool is this (§14.4).

In the §9.1 transcript the client sent Connection: close explicitly, which is why the response carries it back — without it the server would have kept the connection open awaiting another request, and the pipe would not have closed.

9.6 Pipelining, and Why It Failed

Pipelining allows a client to send several requests without waiting for the responses, and the server answers them in order. In principle it removes a round trip per request.

In practice it was a failure, and the reason is worth understanding because it motivates all of HTTP/2:

  • Responses must come back in request order. If the first request is slow, every response behind it waits. This is head-of-line blocking (§4.9) at the application layer, and it is worse than TCP's because it is caused by server processing time rather than by a lost packet.
  • Retrying is ambiguous. If the connection drops mid-pipeline, the client does not know which requests were processed. For non-idempotent requests that is unresolvable.
  • Intermediaries mishandled it. Enough proxies mis-ordered or corrupted pipelined responses that browsers could not enable it safely.

Every major browser shipped pipelining disabled by default and eventually removed it. The lesson HTTP/2 drew is that the fix is not to send requests in order faster — it is to remove the ordering requirement, which needs multiplexing and therefore needs a different framing layer (§10.3).

9.7 Upgrade

The Upgrade header switches an established HTTP/1.1 connection to a different protocol. It is hop-by-hop (§9.3), so an Upgrade header must be regenerated by every proxy along the path rather than forwarded — which is why a load balancer that does not explicitly support WebSocket silently breaks it:

GET /chat HTTP/1.1
Host: example.com
Upgrade: websocket
Connection: Upgrade
Sec-WebSocket-Key: dGhlIHNhbXBsZSBub25jZQ==
Sec-WebSocket-Version: 13

HTTP/1.1 101 Switching Protocols
Upgrade: websocket
Connection: Upgrade
Sec-WebSocket-Accept: s3pPLMBiTxaQ9kYGzzhZRbK+xOo=

After the 101, the connection is no longer HTTP — the bytes are WebSocket frames. This is how WebSocket reaches a server through infrastructure that only understands HTTP: it starts as an HTTP request, which every proxy and load balancer will route, and then stops being one.

Upgrade was also HTTP/2's original cleartext negotiation path (h2c), and it was abandoned for HTTPS because it costs a round trip and because intermediaries strip the header. ALPN (§8.6) does the same job inside the TLS handshake for free. HTTP/2 over TLS is negotiated by ALPN and never by Upgrade — a distinction worth knowing, because h2c is still what you use between a load balancer and a backend on a trusted network.

Cleartext HTTP/2 in practice does not use Upgrade either. RFC 9113 §3.3 permits a client that already knows the server speaks HTTP/2 to skip negotiation entirely and send the connection preface immediately — starting with prior knowledge, no round trip and no header. It is only usable when the client has out-of-band certainty, which is exactly the situation inside your own infrastructure: a sidecar talking to its application, or a gateway to a backend it was configured to reach. Prior knowledge is how gRPC over cleartext works, and attempting it against a server that does not speak HTTP/2 fails outright rather than falling back — no negotiation cost, and no negotiation safety.

9.8 Request Smuggling

This is the vulnerability that the framing rules exist to prevent, and it is the strongest argument for reading RFC 9112 §6 carefully.

The setup is any deployment with two HTTP implementations in series — a CDN in front of a load balancer, a load balancer in front of an application server, an API gateway in front of a service. Both parse the same bytes. If they disagree about where one request ends, an attacker can arrange for the front-end to see one request where the back-end sees two.

The classic form uses both length headers:

POST / HTTP/1.1
Host: example.com
Content-Length: 6
Transfer-Encoding: chunked

0

G

A front-end that honours Content-Length sees a 6-byte body (0\r\n\r\nG) and forwards the whole thing as one request. A back-end that honours Transfer-Encoding sees the body end at the 0 chunk — and treats the leftover G as the first byte of the next request on that connection. The next legitimate user's request gets G prepended, turning their POST /account into GPOST /account.

That is the mechanism. What it buys an attacker is severe: prefixing another user's request with attacker-controlled bytes means their credentials can be redirected to an attacker-visible endpoint, front-end access controls can be bypassed entirely, and responses can be poisoned for other users. The variants are named by which side does what — CL.TE, TE.CL, TE.TE with an obfuscated header — but the cause is always the same disagreement.

Four defences, in order of effectiveness:

  1. Use HTTP/2 end to end. Its framing is binary and length-prefixed with no ambiguity (§10.1), which removes the entire class. Note the caveat: a front-end that downgrades HTTP/2 to HTTP/1.1 for the back-end reintroduces it, and that downgrade is extremely common.
  2. Reject any message with both headers, rather than choosing one. RFC 9112 permits rejection, and rejection is the only behaviour that cannot disagree with anyone.
  3. Normalise at the edge. The front-end should rewrite requests into a canonical form rather than forwarding bytes it did not fully parse.
  4. Do not reuse back-end connections across clients if you can afford not to. Smuggling depends on a shared connection carrying another user's request next.

The related response splitting vulnerability is the same idea in reverse, and it needs only one attacker-controlled header value rather than two disagreeing parsers — which makes response splitting easier to introduce and much easier to fix: if an application copies unvalidated input into a response header and that input contains \r\n, the attacker terminates the header section early and injects a whole second response. The fix is trivial and non-negotiable — reject CR and LF in any header value you construct, which every modern framework does by default and which hand-rolled header writing does not.

9.9 What HTTP/1.1 Costs

Six connections per origin, one request at a time on each, and text headers repeated in full on every message. The consequences shaped a decade of web development practice:

requests, header_bytes, rtt_ms, connections = 40, 700, 46, 6

serial = requests * rtt_ms
parallel = -(-requests // connections) * rtt_ms
overhead = requests * header_bytes

print(f"{requests} requests, {connections} parallel connections, {rtt_ms} ms RTT")
print(f"  strictly serial            {serial:>6} ms")
print(f"  over {connections} connections        {parallel:>6} ms  ({serial / parallel:.1f}x better)")
print(f"  plus {connections} handshakes at 3 RTT {connections * 3 * rtt_ms:>5} ms of setup")
print(f"  header bytes, uncompressed {overhead:>6} B for {requests} requests")

40 requests, 6 parallel connections, 46 ms RTT
  strictly serial              1840 ms
  over 6 connections           322 ms  (5.7x better)
  plus 6 handshakes at 3 RTT   828 ms of setup
  header bytes, uncompressed  28000 B for 40 requests

Six connections is a browser convention, not a specification limit, chosen to balance parallelism against being rude to servers. And notice that the handshakes cost more than the requests — 828 ms of setup for 322 ms of transfer. (The speed-up is 5.7×, not the 6× you might expect from six connections: 40 requests over 6 connections is seven rounds, not six and two-thirds, because the last round is only partly full.)

The workarounds this produced were all attempts to reduce the number of requests, and all of them have real costs:

  • Concatenating scripts and stylesheets — one large file instead of twenty small ones. Cost: a one-line change invalidates the whole bundle in every cache.
  • Image sprites — many images in one file, positioned with CSS. Cost: complexity, and no ability to load one image lazily.
  • Inlining assets as data URIs — zero requests. Cost: no caching at all, and base64 is 33% larger.
  • Domain sharding — serving assets from static1.example.com and static2.example.com to get 12 or 18 connections instead of 6. Cost: an extra DNS lookup and TLS handshake per shard, often exceeding the benefit.

Every one of these is an anti-pattern under HTTP/2, where requests are nearly free and sharding actively hurts by preventing connection reuse. If you inherit a build pipeline doing any of them, the first question is which protocol the traffic actually uses — and §10 is why.

Exercises

Exercise 9.1 — Frame four responses. For each, say how the client determines the body length, and whether truncation is detectable.

A) HTTP/1.1 200 OK / Content-Length: 1024 / (1024 bytes)
B) HTTP/1.1 200 OK / Transfer-Encoding: chunked / (chunks, then 0)
C) HTTP/1.1 200 OK / Content-Type: text/html / (bytes until close)
D) HTTP/1.1 204 No Content / (nothing)
Solution

A — Content-Length. Read exactly 1024 bytes. Truncation is detectable: if the connection closes after 900, the client knows 124 are missing and must report an error. A client that returns the partial body as if it were complete is broken, and this is worth checking in any HTTP client you rely on.

B — chunked. Read chunks until the zero-length one. Truncation is detectable, and this is chunked encoding's underrated advantage: the terminal 0\r\n\r\n is an explicit end-of-message marker, so a connection that dies mid-stream is unambiguously an error. This is why a large streamed export should be chunked even when the size is known.

C — close-delimited. No length header at all, so the body is everything up to EOF. Truncation is undetectable — a complete response and a response cut off by a crashed server look identical. This is the HTTP/1.0 fallback and should never be produced deliberately.

D — no body at all. 204 is one of the status codes defined to have no body, along with 304 and any response to HEAD. The client must not read a body regardless of what the headers say, and this is a real trap: a 204 with a Content-Length: 50 is malformed, and an implementation that trusts the header reads 50 bytes of the next response off the connection. That desynchronises the connection, and it is a smuggling primitive (§9.8).

The general rule: the status code and method can override the framing headers, and any parser that consults only the headers is wrong.

Exercise 9.2 — Spot the smuggle. Your architecture is CDN → nginx → application. A penetration test reports that requests with both Content-Length and Transfer-Encoding are handled inconsistently. Walk through what an attacker achieves, and give the fix at each layer.

Solution

What the attacker achieves. With three implementations in series there are two boundaries where a disagreement can occur, and only one needs to differ. Suppose the CDN honours Content-Length and nginx honours Transfer-Encoding (the CL.TE variant of §9.8). The attacker sends one request that the CDN forwards whole; nginx consumes part of it and leaves the remainder in the connection buffer. Because the CDN-to-nginx connection is shared between clients, that remainder is prepended to whichever request arrives next.

Three concrete consequences, in increasing severity:

  • Bypassing front-end access control. The CDN sees POST /public and applies its rules; nginx sees the smuggled GET /admin/users and applies none, because it believes the request came from the trusted CDN.
  • Capturing another user's request. The smuggled prefix can be POST /comments with a Content-Length large enough to swallow the next victim's request into the body — including their Cookie and Authorization headers — and then the attacker reads their own comment back.
  • Cache poisoning. If a smuggled request causes an attacker-controlled response to be cached against a legitimate URL, every subsequent user gets it. This turns a request-level bug into a persistent, site-wide compromise.

The fixes, layer by layer:

  • CDN. Configure it to reject any request carrying both headers rather than normalising one away. Most major CDNs now do this by default; verify rather than assume, because the default changed at different times for different vendors.
  • nginx. Modern versions reject the combination, so the immediate action is to confirm the version and that no custom configuration reintroduces forwarding of unparsed bytes. Ensure proxy_http_version 1.1 and that hop-by-hop headers are not being passed through.
  • Application. It should never see the combination if the layers above are correct, but defence in depth costs nothing: reject it there too. And never construct a response header from unvalidated input (§9.8's response splitting).
  • Architecturally — the real fix. Speak HTTP/2 between the CDN and nginx, and between nginx and the application. Binary length-prefixed framing has no ambiguity to disagree about. Be aware that if nginx downgrades to HTTP/1.1 for the application, the boundary reappears there — the vulnerability lives at every HTTP/1.1 hop, not at the edge.

How to verify the fix rather than trusting the configuration: send the ambiguous request yourself and observe the response code. A 400 is correct at every layer. Anything else — a 200, a hang, a delayed response — means the request was processed, and the timing-based detection used by tools like Burp's smuggling scanner is the next step.

Exercise 9.3 — Audit a build pipeline. You inherit a front-end build that concatenates 40 JavaScript modules into one bundle, sprites 30 icons into one PNG, inlines all CSS as a data URI, and serves assets from four sharded subdomains. The site is behind a CDN serving HTTP/2. What do you change, and what do you measure?

Solution

Every one of those four techniques is a workaround for HTTP/1.1's six-connection limit (§9.9), and under HTTP/2 three of them are actively harmful.

Domain sharding — remove it first, and it is unambiguous. Under HTTP/2 all requests to one origin share a single connection (§10.3). Four shards means four DNS lookups, four TCP handshakes and four TLS handshakes — from §8's arithmetic, roughly three extra round trips each, on the critical path, buying nothing. This is the change with the largest benefit and no downside.

Inlined CSS — mostly remove, with one exception. A data URI cannot be cached separately, is re-downloaded with every HTML response, and costs 33% in base64 overhead. The exception is genuinely critical above-the-fold CSS, small enough to fit in the initial congestion window (§5.2's 14 KB), where inlining still removes a round trip before first paint. Inline that; make everything else a cached file.

Sprites — remove, but check first. Thirty separate images under HTTP/2 cost thirty stream ids and almost nothing else, and each becomes independently cacheable and lazily loadable. The check worth doing: if the icons are genuinely tiny, thirty responses of 200 bytes each carry thirty sets of response headers, and HPACK (§10.4) compresses those well but not to zero. Prefer an SVG sprite or an icon font if they are all one style — that is a different argument from HTTP framing and may still favour one file.

Bundling — keep it, but rebalance. This is the one where the HTTP/1.1 answer and the HTTP/2 answer are both wrong at the extremes. Forty separate modules means forty requests the browser must discover, and discovery is sequential when one module imports another. One giant bundle means a one-line change invalidates everything in every cache. The right answer is a handful of chunks split along cache-lifetime lines: vendor code that changes monthly, application code that changes weekly, route-level chunks loaded on demand. Modern bundlers do this by default.

What to measure, before and after, because intuition about this is unreliable:

  • Time to first byte and largest contentful paint, from real users rather than a lab — the shards' cost is round trips, which vary enormously by client location.
  • The number of connections opened, in the browser's network panel. If it is not one per origin after removing the shards, something is preventing coalescing.
  • Cache hit ratio per asset after the rebundling, which is the metric that tells you whether the split matched the change frequency.
  • Total bytes transferred on a repeat visit — the number the inlining was silently inflating.

And confirm the premise first: check that clients actually negotiate h2 (curl -sI --http2 https://... and read the status line). If a meaningful share of traffic is still HTTP/1.1 — an old corporate proxy, a downgrade at the CDN — the trade-offs shift back, and it is worth knowing that before changing anything.

SECTION 10

HTTP/2

HTTP/1.1's limits are all consequences of one decision: a connection carries one message at a time, and messages are delimited by scanning text. HTTP/2 (RFC 9113) keeps every bit of HTTP's semantics — the same methods, status codes, and header fields, all still defined by RFC 9110 — and replaces the framing entirely.

That separation is the thing to hold onto. A request is the same request in HTTP/1.1, HTTP/2 and HTTP/3. Only its encoding changes. Everything the Backend & API Design course teaches about designing an API remains true; what changes is what it costs to make a request.

10.1 Binary Framing

The connection is a stream of frames. Each has a 9-byte header:

Length
24 bits
Type
8
Flags
8
R + Stream Identifier
1 + 31 bits

Length-prefixed and binary. There is no scanning for delimiters, no ambiguity about where a message ends, and therefore no request smuggling (§9.8) — a receiver reads a 24-bit length and consumes exactly that many bytes. The whole class of vulnerability disappears by construction.

import struct

FRAME_TYPES = {0x0: "DATA", 0x1: "HEADERS", 0x2: "PRIORITY", 0x3: "RST_STREAM",
               0x4: "SETTINGS", 0x5: "PUSH_PROMISE", 0x6: "PING", 0x7: "GOAWAY",
               0x8: "WINDOW_UPDATE", 0x9: "CONTINUATION"}

def parse_frame_header(nine: bytes) -> str:
    length = int.from_bytes(nine[0:3], "big")
    ftype, flags = nine[3], nine[4]
    stream = int.from_bytes(nine[5:9], "big") & 0x7FFFFFFF     # top bit is reserved
    names = []
    if ftype in (0x0, 0x1) and flags & 0x1: names.append("END_STREAM")
    if ftype == 0x1 and flags & 0x4:        names.append("END_HEADERS")
    if ftype in (0x4, 0x6) and flags & 0x1: names.append("ACK")
    return (f"{FRAME_TYPES.get(ftype, '?'):<13} stream={stream:<3} len={length:<5} "
            f"flags={'|'.join(names) or '-'}")

for raw in [bytes.fromhex("000000" "04" "00" "00000000"),   # SETTINGS, connection-level
            bytes.fromhex("000000" "04" "01" "00000000"),   # SETTINGS ACK
            bytes.fromhex("00002a" "01" "05" "00000001"),   # HEADERS, stream 1, end of both
            bytes.fromhex("0005dc" "00" "00" "00000001"),   # DATA, stream 1, more coming
            bytes.fromhex("000000" "00" "01" "00000001"),   # empty DATA closing the stream
            bytes.fromhex("000004" "08" "00" "00000000")]:  # WINDOW_UPDATE, connection-level
    print(parse_frame_header(raw))

SETTINGS      stream=0   len=0     flags=-
SETTINGS      stream=0   len=0     flags=ACK
HEADERS       stream=1   len=42    flags=END_STREAM|END_HEADERS
DATA          stream=1   len=1500  flags=-
DATA          stream=1   len=0     flags=END_STREAM
WINDOW_UPDATE stream=0   len=4     flags=-

The stream identifier is what makes multiplexing possible: it labels every frame with the exchange it belongs to, so frames from forty requests can be interleaved arbitrarily and still be reassembled. Stream 0 is the connection itself — SETTINGS, PING, GOAWAY and connection-level WINDOW_UPDATE live there. Every other stream id belongs to one request/response exchange. Client-initiated streams are odd-numbered, server-initiated ones even, so the two sides can allocate ids without coordinating.

The maximum frame payload is 16,384 bytes by default, negotiable up to 16 MB via SETTINGS. A large response is many DATA frames.

10.2 Pseudo-Headers, and Lowercase Everything

A request's control data — method, path, scheme, authority — is carried as pseudo-headers, names beginning with a colon. They must come before all regular fields. You can see them directly:

curl --http2 -v -o /dev/null https://example.com

* using HTTP/2
* [HTTP/2] [1] OPENED stream for https://example.com/
* [HTTP/2] [1] [:method: GET]
* [HTTP/2] [1] [:scheme: https]
* [HTTP/2] [1] [:authority: example.com]
* [HTTP/2] [1] [:path: /]
* [HTTP/2] [1] [user-agent: curl/8.18.0]
* [HTTP/2] [1] [accept: */*]
> GET / HTTP/2
> Host: example.com
< HTTP/2 200
< date: Mon, 24 Aug 2026 02:27:31 GMT
< content-type: text/html
< server: cloudflare
< last-modified: Tue, 18 Aug 2026 20:06:42 GMT
< cf-cache-status: HIT

Four pseudo-headers replace HTTP/1.1's request line and Host header: :method, :scheme, :authority, :path. Responses have exactly one, :status — note the response line above is HTTP/2 200 with no reason phrase, because RFC 9113 removed it entirely. Any code still parsing "OK" is now definitively broken (§9.2 warned about this; here is the protocol making it real).

:authority replaces Host. curl displays Host: example.com in its human-readable summary for familiarity, but no Host header is sent on the wire — a server that receives both must verify they agree.

Now compare the two responses from the same origin. Over HTTP/1.1 (§9.1) the headers came back as Date:, Content-Type:, Connection:. Over HTTP/2 the identical response is entirely lowercase: date:, content-type:, server:. That is RFC 9113 §8.2.1 — field names must be lowercase, and an uppercase byte in a field name is a protocol error that kills the stream.

This matters more than it sounds. Field names were always case-insensitive (§9.3), so any correct implementation already handled it — but plenty of code compares headers["Content-Type"] against a raw dictionary and works fine until the day the traffic is upgraded to HTTP/2 and every key is lowercase. It is a genuinely common migration bug, and it is entirely invisible until protocol negotiation changes.

10.3 Multiplexing

This is the point of the whole protocol. Many streams share one connection, their frames interleaved, and responses may arrive in any order.

requests, rtt, conns_h1 = 40, 46, 6

h1_rounds = -(-requests // conns_h1)
print(f"HTTP/1.1: {conns_h1} connections, {h1_rounds} rounds  -> {h1_rounds * rtt} ms")
print(f"          plus {conns_h1} handshakes at 3 RTT        -> {conns_h1 * 3 * rtt} ms setup")
print(f"          total                                   {h1_rounds * rtt + conns_h1 * 3 * rtt} ms")
print()
print(f"HTTP/2:   1 connection, all {requests} in flight     -> {rtt} ms")
print(f"          plus 1 handshake at 3 RTT                -> {3 * rtt} ms setup")
print(f"          total                                   {rtt + 3 * rtt} ms")

HTTP/1.1: 6 connections, 7 rounds  -> 322 ms
          plus 6 handshakes at 3 RTT        -> 828 ms setup
          total                                   1150 ms
HTTP/2:   1 connection, all 40 in flight     -> 46 ms
          plus 1 handshake at 3 RTT                -> 138 ms setup
          total                                   184 ms

Six times faster in this model, and most of the saving is not the multiplexing — it is the five handshakes that no longer happen. That is worth internalising, because it means HTTP/2's benefit is largest exactly where HTTP/1.1 was forced to open many connections, and near zero for a client making one request.

Multiplexing also removes the ordering constraint that killed pipelining (§9.6). Responses are labelled with their stream id, so a slow one delays nothing. A client can issue a request and, without waiting, issue thirty more.

Two consequences follow that people find counter-intuitive:

  • Domain sharding becomes harmful. Splitting assets across subdomains was how HTTP/1.1 escaped the six-connection limit. Under HTTP/2 it forces multiple connections where one would do, adding a DNS lookup and a handshake for each (§9.9's exercise).
  • Connection coalescing goes the other way: if two hostnames resolve to the same IP and one certificate covers both, a browser may reuse a single connection for both. This is why a wildcard certificate can improve performance, and why the SAN list has a performance dimension as well as a security one.

10.4 HPACK

Forty requests to one origin carry forty nearly identical header sets — the same user-agent, accept, cookie, differing only in :path. That was 28 KB of redundancy in §9.9's arithmetic. HPACK (RFC 7541) compresses it, using two tables and one encoding.

The static table holds 61 predefined entries: common names, and some name/value pairs outright. :method: GET is index 2, so it encodes as one byte.

The dynamic table is where the real saving is. Each side maintains an identical, ordered table of header fields seen on this connection, and a field already in it is sent as an index. So user-agent: curl/8.18.0 costs its full length once and one byte thereafter.

STATIC = {(":method", "GET"): 2, (":method", "POST"): 3, (":path", "/"): 4,
          (":scheme", "https"): 7, (":status", "200"): 8, ("accept-encoding", "gzip, deflate"): 16}

def encode(headers, dynamic):
    """Rough HPACK sizing: 1 byte for an indexed field, name+value+32 otherwise."""
    total = 0
    for name, value in headers:
        if (name, value) in STATIC or (name, value) in dynamic:
            total += 1
        else:
            total += len(name) + len(value) + 32     # RFC 7541 s4.1 per-entry overhead
            dynamic.add((name, value))
    return total

request = [(":method", "GET"), (":scheme", "https"), (":authority", "example.com"),
           (":path", "/api/items"), ("user-agent", "curl/8.18.0"), ("accept", "*/*"),
           ("cookie", "session=" + "a" * 48)]

dynamic = set()
first = encode(request, dynamic)
later = encode([(k, v) if k != ":path" else (k, "/api/items/42") for k, v in request], dynamic)
uncompressed = sum(len(k) + len(v) + 4 for k, v in request)

print(f"uncompressed (HTTP/1.1 style) {uncompressed:>5} bytes")
print(f"HPACK, first request          {first:>5} bytes")
print(f"HPACK, subsequent request     {later:>5} bytes")
print(f"over 40 requests: {uncompressed * 40:>6} -> {first + later * 39:>5} bytes "
      f"({uncompressed * 40 / (first + later * 39):.0f}x)")

uncompressed (HTTP/1.1 style)   178 bytes
HPACK, first request            290 bytes
HPACK, subsequent request        56 bytes
over 40 requests:   7120 ->  2474 bytes (3x)

The first request is 63% larger than uncompressed, because each new dynamic-table entry carries 32 bytes of RFC 7541 accounting overhead on top of the field itself. From the second request onward almost everything is a one-byte index and the cost drops to 56 bytes — the only field that changed is :path, which is not in any table.

Two honest caveats about that 3×. The model ignores Huffman coding, which HPACK also applies to literal values and which typically saves another 20–30%, so real ratios are better. And it ignores that the dynamic table has a size limit (4096 bytes by default), so a connection seeing many distinct header values will evict entries and re-pay the literal cost. The direction is right and the exact figure is not a measurement.

On a connection carrying a large cookie or authorization header on every request — the common case for an API — the saving is much larger than this, because that single field dominates the uncompressed size and collapses to one byte after the first request.

HPACK was designed specifically to resist CRIME, the attack that broke generic TLS-level compression: an attacker who can inject a guessed string into a request and observe the compressed size can extract a secret one character at a time. HPACK's defences are that indexing is per-field rather than over a byte stream, and that a field may be marked "never indexed" — which is what a well-configured server does for authorization and cookie.

The dynamic table's cost is shared mutable state between the two endpoints, and it must stay synchronised. That has consequences: a proxy cannot forward a HEADERS frame's bytes untouched, because the indices are meaningful only in the context of one connection's table — it must decode and re-encode. And an attacker who can make a server allocate large table entries has a memory-amplification primitive, which is why SETTINGS_HEADER_TABLE_SIZE exists and why the compression-related CVEs in HTTP/2 implementations have mostly been here.

10.5 Flow Control

HTTP/2 has its own flow control, separate from and in addition to TCP's (§4.5). This is not redundancy: TCP's window covers the whole connection, and HTTP/2 needs to stop one stream — a client that paused a video download — without stalling the forty others sharing the connection.

The mechanism is credit-based. Each side starts with a 65,535-byte window per stream and another for the connection as a whole, DATA frames consume it, and WINDOW_UPDATE frames replenish it. Only DATA frames count; HEADERS are exempt, so control traffic cannot be starved.

The default 65,535 bytes is the number that matters operationally, and §4.4's arithmetic applies unchanged: on a 46 ms path that caps each stream at about 11 Mbit/s, and the connection window caps the total at the same figure. A large download over HTTP/2 that inexplicably runs at 11 Mbit/s is a server that never raised its window — and because both windows apply, raising only the stream window achieves nothing. Every serious HTTP/2 server has a setting for this, and its default is often still 65,535.

10.6 SETTINGS, GOAWAY and RST_STREAM

Three control frames account for nearly all connection management.

A SETTINGS frame is sent by both peers at connection start and may be re-sent at any time. Because a SETTINGS frame must be acknowledged, a peer that changes a limit knows exactly when the other side began honouring it: It is not a negotiation — each side simply states its own limits, and the peer must respect them and acknowledge:

Setting Default Why you would change it
HEADER_TABLE_SIZE 4096 HPACK dynamic table size
ENABLE_PUSH 1 Set to 0 to refuse server push (§10.7)
MAX_CONCURRENT_STREAMS unlimited The real parallelism limit. Commonly set to 100–128
INITIAL_WINDOW_SIZE 65535 Flow control per stream (§10.5)
MAX_FRAME_SIZE 16384 Larger frames, fewer headers, less interleaving
MAX_HEADER_LIST_SIZE unlimited A guard against header-bomb attacks

MAX_CONCURRENT_STREAMS is the one to know. "Unlimited multiplexing" is not what you get — a server advertising 100 means a client with 300 requests queues 200 of them locally. That queueing is invisible in most client metrics and is a real source of unexplained latency under load.

GOAWAY is a graceful shutdown. The sender names the highest stream id it will process; anything above that was never handled and is safe to retry on a new connection. This is how a server drains connections before a deploy without failing in-flight requests, and honouring it correctly is the difference between a clean rolling restart and a burst of errors.

RST_STREAM cancels one stream, leaving the connection intact. A browser navigating away mid-download sends one. It is cheap for the client and costs the server real work to process — which is exactly the Rapid Reset attack (CVE-2023-44487): open a stream and immediately reset it, thousands of times per second. Because a reset stream no longer counts toward MAX_CONCURRENT_STREAMS, the limit provided no protection, and this produced the largest DDoS attacks recorded at the time. The mitigation, now standard, is to track the rate of resets and close connections that abuse it — a reminder that a limit on concurrency is not a limit on throughput.

10.7 Server Push Is Dead

PUSH_PROMISE allowed a server to send resources the client had not requested — push the CSS along with the HTML, saving a round trip.

It failed. Chrome disabled it by default in version 106 (2022) and Chromium-based browsers followed; it is off almost everywhere. The reasons are worth knowing because they recur in other "helpful prefetch" designs:

  • The server does not know the client's cache. Pushing a file the browser already has wastes the bytes entirely, and there was never a good mechanism for the client to say so in time.
  • It competes with what was actually requested. Pushed data consumes the same connection and congestion window as the HTML that is blocking rendering.
  • Measurements did not support it. Chrome's own data showed no clear net gain and frequent regressions — and only about 1.25% of HTTP/2 sites ever used it, so the feature cost every implementation complexity while benefiting almost nobody.

The replacement is 103 Early Hints (RFC 8297), which inverts the control: the server sends a provisional response containing Link: rel=preload headers before the real response, and the client decides what to fetch. The server contributes what it knows (which resources this page needs) and the client contributes what it knows (what is already cached). §13.8 covers it.

10.8 The Head-of-Line Blocking That Remains

HTTP/2 removed head-of-line blocking at the application layer. It could not remove it at the transport layer, and this is its one unfixable flaw.

All those independent streams travel over one TCP connection, and TCP guarantees in-order delivery of the entire byte stream (§4.9). It has no idea the bytes belong to different requests. So a single lost packet stalls delivery of every stream until it is retransmitted:

streams, rtt, loss_position = 40, 46, 0.3

print(f"a single packet lost {loss_position:.0%} of the way through a response:")
print(f"  HTTP/1.1 over 6 connections: stalls the 1 stream on that connection")
print(f"                               {40 // 6} others on it wait, 33 unaffected")
print(f"  HTTP/2 over 1 connection:    stalls ALL {streams} streams for {rtt} ms")
print(f"  HTTP/3 over QUIC:            stalls 1 stream; {streams - 1} unaffected")
print()
print(f"paradox: at 2% loss, HTTP/2 can be SLOWER than HTTP/1.1,")
print(f"because HTTP/1.1's {40 // 6} connections are {40 // 6} independent failure domains")

a single packet lost 30% of the way through a response:
  HTTP/1.1 over 6 connections: stalls the 1 stream on that connection
                               6 others on it wait, 33 unaffected
  HTTP/2 over 1 connection:    stalls ALL 40 streams for 46 ms
  HTTP/3 over QUIC:            stalls 1 stream; 39 unaffected

paradox: at 2% loss, HTTP/2 can be SLOWER than HTTP/1.1,
because HTTP/1.1's 6 connections are 6 independent failure domains

That last line is measured reality, not a thought experiment: on lossy networks — mobile, congested Wi-Fi, satellite — HTTP/2 sometimes performs worse than HTTP/1.1, precisely because consolidating onto one connection consolidated the blast radius of every lost packet. HTTP/2 traded six small failure domains for one large one, and on a clean network that is a clear win.

This is the entire motivation for HTTP/3. Not new semantics, not better compression — moving the transport to something that understands streams, so a loss affects only the stream it belongs to. §11.

Exercises

Exercise 10.1 — Why is it not faster? A team migrates their API from HTTP/1.1 to HTTP/2 and measures no improvement. Their clients are mobile apps making one API call every few seconds. Explain, and say when HTTP/2 would have helped.

Solution

Every one of HTTP/2's advantages requires concurrency on one connection, and this workload has none.

Take them in turn. Multiplexing saves nothing when there is one request in flight — there is nothing to interleave. HPACK needs a warm dynamic table, and a connection carrying one request every few seconds may not even survive between calls; if the connection is torn down, the table starts empty and the first request is larger than uncompressed (§10.4). Server push is dead (§10.7). Binary framing removes parsing ambiguity, which is a correctness and security win, not a latency one.

Meanwhile the costs are unchanged: the same DNS lookup, TCP handshake and TLS handshake dominate, and §5.1's arithmetic shows those are four round trips against one for the request itself.

Where HTTP/2 does help this team, in descending order of value:

  • If the connection is kept alive between calls — which HTTP/2 makes easier because a single connection serves everything, and mobile clients pool poorly. That is worth three round trips per call and dwarfs everything else. The real question for this team is not "which protocol" but "is the connection warm", and they should measure that first.
  • If the app ever makes several calls at once — a screen that loads a profile, a feed and a notification count together. Three requests on one warm HTTP/2 connection cost one round trip; on HTTP/1.1 they cost either three round trips or three handshakes.
  • If requests carry large headers — a long bearer token or a fat cookie on every call. HPACK reduces a repeated 1 KB authorization header to one byte, which matters on a metered mobile link.

And one case where it hurts: a mobile network with meaningful packet loss suffers HTTP/2's transport-level head-of-line blocking (§10.8) with none of the multiplexing benefit to offset it. For this exact workload — one request at a time over a lossy link — HTTP/2 is at best neutral.

The honest recommendation: enable HTTP/2 (it costs nothing and removes the smuggling class), but expect the win to come from connection reuse, and go to HTTP/3 for mobile clients, where the loss behaviour is the actual problem.

Exercise 10.2 — Diagnose the throughput ceiling. A file download over HTTP/2 runs at exactly 11.4 Mbit/s regardless of file size, on a 46 ms path with 1 Gbit/s available. ss -tin shows cwnd:340, snd_wnd:6291456, no retransmissions and no app_limited. What is limiting it?

Solution

HTTP/2 flow control (§10.5), at its default window.

The elimination is what makes this diagnosable. cwnd:340 is 492 KB — the congestion window is not the limit. snd_wnd of 6 MB says the peer's TCP receive window is not the limit. No retransmissions rules out loss. No app_limited says the sender is not starved. Every TCP-level explanation is excluded, so the constraint is above TCP.

Confirm it with arithmetic:

window, rtt = 65535, 0.046
print(f"{window * 8 / rtt / 1e6:.1f} Mbit/s at the default HTTP/2 window")
print(f"for 1 Gbit/s you need a window of {1e9 * rtt / 8 / 1024 / 1024:.1f} MiB")

11.4 Mbit/s at the default HTTP/2 window
for 1 Gbit/s you need a window of 5.5 MiB

11.4 Mbit/s is exactly 65,535 bytes per 46 ms round trip. A number that matches a default this precisely is never a coincidence, and recognising it is the skill — the same shape of reasoning identified the unscaled TCP window in §4.4's exercise.

The fix has a trap in it. There are two windows and both apply: SETTINGS_INITIAL_WINDOW_SIZE governs each stream, and a separate connection-level window governs the sum. Raising only the stream window leaves the connection window at 65,535 and changes nothing. Both must be raised, to something like the bandwidth-delay product — 8 MB is a reasonable setting for a server on long paths — and the connection window is adjusted with WINDOW_UPDATE on stream 0 rather than through SETTINGS, which is why some servers expose two unrelated configuration options for it.

Worth noting: this is a case where HTTP/2 added a bottleneck HTTP/1.1 did not have. The flow control exists for a good reason — to let one stream be paused without stalling forty others — but its default was chosen in 2015 and has not aged well on fast paths.

Exercise 10.3 — Migrate an architecture. You run browser → CDN → nginx → three application servers, all HTTP/1.1. You are asked to "turn on HTTP/2". Specify what changes at each hop and what you check afterwards.

Solution

Each hop is a separate decision, and only the first is obvious.

Browser → CDN: yes, and it is probably already on. Enable ALPN advertising h2 (§8.6). This is where nearly all the benefit is, because it is the hop with a real round-trip cost, many parallel requests, and repeated headers. Verify with curl -sI --http2 https://site and read the status line for HTTP/2 200.

CDN → nginx: yes, and this is the security-relevant one. An HTTP/1.1 hop here means the CDN downgrades, which reintroduces the request-smuggling boundary (§9.8) that HTTP/2 removes. Configure the CDN's origin protocol as HTTP/2. If the CDN cannot, that boundary must be defended explicitly by rejecting ambiguous framing.

nginx → application servers: probably not, and this is the counter-intuitive one. On a low-latency local network, HTTP/2's advantages largely evaporate — there is no round trip to save and header repetition is cheap. Meanwhile nginx's proxy_pass to an HTTP/2 upstream historically opened a connection per request in some configurations, which is worse than HTTP/1.1 keep-alive. Measure before changing. The exception is gRPC, which requires HTTP/2, and a backend under heavy concurrency where connection count is itself a problem.

Check afterwards, in this order:

  1. Protocol actually negotiated, at each hop, not just the browser: curl -sI --http2, and nginx's $server_protocol in the access log. It is common to discover one hop silently stayed on 1.1.
  2. MAX_CONCURRENT_STREAMS at the CDN and nginx (§10.6). A default of 100 with a client issuing 300 requests means silent local queueing.
  3. Flow-control windows (§10.5), if you serve anything large — the exercise above.
  4. Header case in application code. Every field name is now lowercase (§10.2). Grep for headers["X- and any case-sensitive header comparison; this is the single most likely functional regression.
  5. The build pipeline. Domain sharding now hurts (§9.9); check whether the front-end is still doing it.
  6. Reset rate monitoring for Rapid Reset (§10.6), if you terminate HTTP/2 yourself rather than behind a CDN.

And one thing not to do: do not remove Connection: keep-alive handling or HTTP/1.1 support. Some clients, health checkers and monitoring tools do not speak HTTP/2, and ALPN falls back gracefully only if the fallback still exists.

SECTION 11

QUIC and HTTP/3

HTTP/2 solved application-layer head-of-line blocking and then hit the transport-layer version underneath it (§10.8). Fixing that requires a transport that understands streams, and TCP does not and cannot be made to — it is implemented in kernels and middleboxes that take a decade to change, and its ordering guarantee is baked into the wire format.

So QUIC does something more radical: it implements a complete reliable, ordered, congestion-controlled, encrypted transport in user space, on top of UDP. HTTP/3 (RFC 9114) is then just HTTP's semantics mapped onto QUIC's streams.

11.1 Why UDP

UDP is not chosen for its unreliability. It is chosen because it is the only way to get arbitrary packets across the internet's middleboxes.

A new IP protocol number is undeployable. SCTP and DCCP both exist, both are technically better than TCP for this, and both are dropped by most NATs and firewalls, which forward only protocols 6 and 17. Ossification is not a theory; it is why the transport layer has not changed since 1981.
Changing TCP requires changing kernels. A TCP extension needs both endpoints' operating systems to ship it — years for servers, longer for the long tail of clients. QUIC lives in the application, so a browser update deploys a transport change.
Encryption prevents re-ossification. QUIC encrypts nearly its whole header, including the parts a middlebox would want to inspect. A middlebox that cannot parse it cannot come to depend on its layout — which is a deliberate design goal, learned from watching TCP's option space become unusable.

The cost is real and worth stating: UDP throughput on commodity hardware has historically been well below TCP's, because TCP benefits from decades of kernel optimisation and NIC offload (segmentation, checksums, receive-side scaling) that UDP is only now acquiring. Early QUIC deployments burned two to three times the CPU per byte. Segmentation offload for UDP has narrowed that considerably, but a server terminating QUIC still costs more CPU than one terminating TCP.

11.2 What QUIC Provides

The QUIC transport (RFC 9000) is complete in a way UDP is not: it provides reliability, ordering, flow control and congestion control, all of which UDP lacks. Comparing the QUIC transport with the TCP+TLS stack it replaces:

TCP + TLS 1.3 QUIC
Connection setup 1 RTT (TCP) + 1 RTT (TLS) = 2 1 — combined
Resumed setup 2 RTT 0 — with 0-RTT
Reliability unit The whole connection Per stream
Loss affects Every stream (§10.8) Only the stream that lost a packet
Connection identity The four-tuple A connection ID — survives an address change
Header encryption None — TCP header is plaintext Nearly all of it
Implemented in Kernel User space
Congestion control Kernel's choice The application's choice, per connection

The first row is the headline and the third is the reason the protocol exists.

Combining the handshakes works because QUIC embeds the TLS 1.3 handshake in its own — the CRYPTO frames of QUIC's first flight carry the ClientHello, and the keys they establish protect everything after. There is no separate transport handshake to complete first, so what cost two round trips costs one:

rtt = 90
for name, setup_rtts, note in [
        ("TCP + TLS 1.3, cold",      2, "one round trip each"),
        ("TCP + TLS 1.3, resumed",   2, "resumption saves bytes, not a round trip"),
        ("QUIC, cold",               1, "handshakes combined"),
        ("QUIC, 0-RTT resumed",      0, "data in the very first flight")]:
    total = (setup_rtts + 1) * rtt          # +1 for the request itself
    print(f"{name:<26}{setup_rtts} RTT setup  ->  first byte at {total:>3} ms   {note}")

TCP + TLS 1.3, cold       2 RTT setup  ->  first byte at 270 ms   one round trip each
TCP + TLS 1.3, resumed    2 RTT setup  ->  first byte at 270 ms   resumption saves bytes, not a round trip
QUIC, cold                1 RTT setup  ->  first byte at 180 ms   handshakes combined
QUIC, 0-RTT resumed       0 RTT setup  ->  first byte at  90 ms   data in the very first flight

Ninety milliseconds saved on every new connection, and another ninety on a resumed one. On a mobile network where the round trip is 150 ms and connections are constantly re-established, that is the difference users notice.

QUIC's 0-RTT carries the same replay caveat as TLS's (§8.7) and for the same reason — there has been no round trip to prove freshness. Restrict it to idempotent requests.

11.3 Streams Without Head-of-Line Blocking

This is the substance. QUIC packets carry frames, and a STREAM frame names the stream it belongs to. Loss recovery is per-packet, and delivery to the application is per-stream:

streams, rtt = 4, 46
arrival = {1: 5, 2: 8, 3: 11, 4: 14}          # ms, all fine
lost = 2                                      # stream 2's packet is dropped
arrival[lost] += rtt                          # retransmitted a round trip later

print("HTTP/2 over TCP — one ordered byte stream:")
blocked_until = max(arrival.values())
for s in sorted(arrival):
    ready = max(a for k, a in arrival.items() if k <= s)
    print(f"   stream {s}: data at {arrival[s]:>3} ms, delivered at {ready:>3} ms")

print("\nHTTP/3 over QUIC — independent streams:")
for s in sorted(arrival):
    print(f"   stream {s}: data at {arrival[s]:>3} ms, delivered at {arrival[s]:>3} ms")

HTTP/2 over TCP — one ordered byte stream:
   stream 1: data at   5 ms, delivered at   5 ms
   stream 2: data at  54 ms, delivered at  54 ms
   stream 3: data at  11 ms, delivered at  54 ms
   stream 4: data at  14 ms, delivered at  54 ms

HTTP/3 over QUIC — independent streams:
   stream 1: data at   5 ms, delivered at   5 ms
   stream 2: data at  54 ms, delivered at  54 ms
   stream 3: data at  11 ms, delivered at  11 ms
   stream 4: data at  14 ms, delivered at  14 ms

Compare the two blocks line by line. Streams 1 and 2 are identical in both — the lost packet genuinely cost stream 2 a round trip, and no protocol can avoid that. The difference is streams 3 and 4: under TCP they arrived at 11 ms and 14 ms and were withheld until 54 ms, because TCP must deliver bytes in order and stream 2's bytes came earlier in that order. Under QUIC they are delivered the instant they arrive, because QUIC knows they are unrelated.

One loss delayed 43 ms of already-arrived data on two innocent streams, and that is with only four. With sixty concurrent streams — a normal page load — the same single loss delays fifty-eight of them. The benefit scales with loss rate and with the number of concurrent streams, which is exactly the mobile-browser case.

QUIC streams also come in two shapes that TCP has no equivalent for: bidirectional (like a TCP connection) and unidirectional (one-way, used by HTTP/3 for control and QPACK state). And a stream can be reset individually without touching the connection, which is RST_STREAM's equivalent.

11.4 Connection IDs and Migration

A TCP connection is its four-tuple (§3.1). Change any element — as happens when a phone moves from Wi-Fi to cellular and gets a new IP address — and the connection is dead. Every request in flight fails, and a new connection with a new handshake is required. This is why an app appears to hang for a few seconds when you walk out of a building.

QUIC identifies a connection by a connection ID carried in the packet, independent of the addresses. When the client's address changes, it sends a packet from the new address with the same connection ID, the server validates the new path, and the connection continues — same keys, same streams, same congestion state, no handshake. That is connection migration.

The path validation step is not optional and is worth understanding: on seeing a new address the server sends a PATH_CHALLENGE with random bytes and requires a PATH_RESPONSE echoing them. Without it, an attacker could spoof the connection ID and redirect the server's traffic to a victim address — an amplification attack. Migration also resets the congestion controller, because the new path's capacity is unknown.

Connection IDs also solve a load-balancing problem elegantly. A TCP load balancer hashes the four-tuple to pick a backend, so a client's address change sends it to a different server. A QUIC-aware balancer routes on the connection ID, which the server itself chose — so the server can encode its own identity into the ID it hands out, and every packet routes to the right place regardless of the source address.

There is a privacy consideration in the same mechanism. A stable connection ID visible on the wire would let an observer track a device across networks — precisely the linkage migration is designed to survive. QUIC's answer is that each endpoint issues several connection IDs and the peer switches to an unused one when it migrates, so the old and new paths cannot be trivially correlated.

11.5 QPACK

HPACK's dynamic table (§10.4) assumes ordered delivery: both sides must apply table updates in the same sequence, or their tables diverge. QUIC delivers streams out of order, so HPACK cannot be used as-is.

QPACK (RFC 9204) solves it by splitting header state onto separate unidirectional streams — an encoder stream carrying table updates and a decoder stream carrying acknowledgments — and by making each header block state which table entries it depends on. If a block references an entry whose update has not arrived, the decoder blocks that one stream until it does.

The trade-off is explicit and configurable. An encoder may reference recent dynamic entries for maximum compression at the risk of head-of-line blocking on the header stream, or restrict itself to entries it knows the peer has acknowledged for slightly worse compression and no blocking. Most implementations lean conservative, which is the right default: re-introducing head-of-line blocking to save a few bytes would defeat the point of the protocol.

11.6 Discovery: Alt-Svc and the HTTPS Record

A client cannot simply try QUIC — UDP 443 is blocked on many networks, and a failed attempt costs a timeout. So HTTP/3 is discovered rather than assumed, by two mechanisms.

Alt-Svc is a response header advertising an alternative endpoint. The client connects over HTTP/2 as usual, sees the header, and uses HTTP/3 for subsequent connections:

for h in cloudflare.com www.google.com nghttp2.org; do
  printf "%-20s " "$h"; curl -sSI "https://$h" | grep -i alt-svc
done

cloudflare.com       alt-svc: h3=":443"; ma=86400
www.google.com       alt-svc: h3=":443"; ma=2592000,h3-29=":443"; ma=2592000
nghttp2.org          alt-svc: h3=":443"; ma=3600

h3=":443" means "the same host, port 443, over HTTP/3"; ma is how long to remember it. Google additionally advertises h3-29, a pre-standard draft version, for older clients — a small archaeological record of QUIC's long standardisation.

The weakness is that the first connection is always HTTP/2. You cannot use HTTP/3 until you have already spoken to the server.

The HTTPS DNS record (RFC 9460) removes that. It is a SVCB-family record carrying connection hints, fetched during the DNS lookup you were doing anyway:

dig +short cloudflare.com HTTPS
dig +short www.google.com HTTPS

1 . alpn="h3,h2" ipv4hint=104.16.132.229,104.16.133.229 ipv6hint=2606:4700::6810:84e5,2606:4700::6810:85e5
1 . alpn="h2,h3"

The client now knows HTTP/3 is supported before opening any connection, and gets address hints in the same answer — so a single DNS round trip replaces what would have been a separate A lookup plus an HTTP/2 connection plus a later upgrade. Note the two zones list their ALPN preferences in different orders, which is a hint to the client about what the operator prefers.

The HTTPS record is also where Encrypted Client Hello keys are published (§8.5), which makes it the mechanism through which the last plaintext identifier in an HTTPS connection gets closed off.

11.7 What This Machine Cannot Show You

Every other transcript in this course was produced on the machine it was written on. This one cannot be:

curl -V | grep -o HTTP3 || echo "NO HTTP3 SUPPORT"

NO HTTP3 SUPPORT

curl 8.18.0 here is built against OpenSSL without an HTTP/3 backend, so curl --http3 is unavailable. That is worth stating plainly rather than fabricating output, and it is also a genuine fact about HTTP/3's deployment: the tooling lags the protocol. HTTP/3 support in curl requires a TLS library with QUIC support (quictls, GnuTLS, wolfSSL, or the ngtcp2/nghttp3 combination) and is still not the default in most distributions years after standardisation.

If your build does have it, the commands to know are:

curl --http3 -sI https://cloudflare.com          # force HTTP/3, fail if unavailable
curl --http3-only -v https://cloudflare.com      # no fallback, to prove it works

You can confirm UDP 443 reachability without curl, which is the more useful diagnostic anyway — if this fails, HTTP/3 will silently never be used on your network:

nc -u -z -w 3 cloudflare.com 443 && echo "UDP 443 reachable"

And in a browser, the network panel's protocol column shows h3 per request, which is the fastest way to check what is actually being negotiated.

11.8 When HTTP/3 Helps, and When It Does Not

The honest summary, because HTTP/3 is often oversold:

Clear win: lossy networks with many parallel requests. Mobile, congested Wi-Fi, satellite. This is where per-stream recovery (§11.3) pays, and the measured improvements are large.
Clear win: clients that change networks. Connection migration turns a multi-second stall into nothing (§11.4).
Clear win: high-latency paths. One round trip saved per connection, two with 0-RTT.
Marginal: a clean, fast network with few requests. Nothing is being lost, so there is no head-of-line blocking to remove, and one saved round trip on a 5 ms path is 5 ms.
Actively worse: inside a data centre. Sub-millisecond RTT, negligible loss, and QUIC's higher CPU cost per byte is the only difference that remains. Use HTTP/2, or HTTP/1.1.
Blocked: networks that drop UDP 443. Some corporate and public networks do. Clients fall back to HTTP/2, so nothing breaks — but you must keep HTTP/2 working, permanently.

Two operational consequences that catch teams out. Your existing network tooling largely does not work: everything in §15 that reads TCP state (ss -tin, TCP retransmission counters, connection-state graphs) sees nothing, because there is no TCP connection and QUIC's state lives inside the server process. Whatever terminates QUIC must export its own metrics, and qlog is the standard format for that.

And QUIC is an amplification vector if implemented carelessly: a small spoofed Initial packet could elicit a large server response. RFC 9000 §8 requires a server to send no more than three times the bytes it has received from an unvalidated address, which is why QUIC Initial packets are padded to at least 1200 bytes — the padding buys the server the budget to reply with a certificate chain.

Exercises

Exercise 11.1 — Predict the benefit. For each deployment, estimate whether HTTP/3 helps and by roughly how much.

  1. A mobile game client on cellular, sending small position updates 20 times per second.
  2. A single-page app loading 60 assets from a CDN, users worldwide, 2% packet loss on mobile.
  3. Two microservices in the same availability zone, 0.4 ms RTT, gRPC.
  4. A video streaming service delivering large segments over long distances.
Solution

1 — Helps, but mostly for a reason other than the obvious one. Twenty small messages a second is one stream's worth of traffic, so per-stream recovery buys little. The real win is connection migration (§11.4): a player walking between cell towers keeps the session instead of stalling for a reconnect and handshake. Secondary win: 0-RTT on reconnection after a genuine drop. Note that a game sending position updates may not want reliability at all — QUIC's unreliable datagram extension (RFC 9221) is arguably the right answer here, giving you the connection and the encryption without retransmitting a position that is already stale.

2 — The strongest case there is, and the improvement is large. Sixty parallel streams and 2% loss is exactly §10.8's pathology: under HTTP/2 each lost packet stalls all sixty, and with 60 streams a loss somewhere is near-certain. Expect a meaningful improvement in largest contentful paint on the mobile cohort — this is the workload Google and Cloudflare's published measurements are drawn from. The saved handshake round trip is a bonus on top.

3 — No, and it is likely to be slightly worse. At 0.4 ms RTT the saved handshake is 0.4 ms. Loss inside an availability zone is negligible, so there is no head-of-line blocking to eliminate. What remains is QUIC's higher CPU cost per byte (§11.1) and the loss of every TCP diagnostic (§11.8). Stay on HTTP/2, which gRPC requires anyway.

4 — Mixed, and it depends on the segment size. Long distances mean a high bandwidth-delay product, so the saved handshake helps and 0-RTT helps more. But a video player requesting one large segment at a time has one stream in flight, so per-stream recovery does nothing — a lost packet stalls the only stream either way. The genuine wins here are migration for mobile viewers and the ability to choose a congestion controller per connection (BBR, §5.5), which matters more for bulk transfer than the framing does.

The pattern across all four: HTTP/3's benefit comes from concurrency × loss for head-of-line blocking, from round-trip time for the handshake, and from client mobility for migration. Score a deployment on those three and the answer falls out. None of them is "HTTP/3 is newer".

Exercise 11.2 — Why is nobody using it? You enable HTTP/3 at your CDN. A week later, 4% of requests are HTTP/3 and you expected 60%. List the causes you would check, in order.

Solution

In the order I would actually check them, cheapest first:

  1. Are you measuring the right thing? A client's first connection to an origin is always HTTP/2 unless an HTTPS DNS record exists (§11.6). If most sessions are a single page load, most requests legitimately use HTTP/2 even with everything configured perfectly. Segment by returning visitors before concluding anything.

  2. Is the HTTPS DNS record published? Without it, discovery depends entirely on Alt-Svc, which requires a prior connection. Publishing it is one DNS record and is the single highest-impact fix for a low adoption rate. Check with dig +short yourdomain HTTPS.
