  3. Is Alt-Svc actually being sent? Check on the real production response, not a staging one — some configurations strip it, and any intermediary between your CDN and the client can too. curl -sSI https://yoursite | grep -i alt-svc.

  4. Is UDP 443 reachable from your users? Corporate networks, some public Wi-Fi and a few mobile carriers block or heavily rate-limit UDP 443. Clients fail silently back to HTTP/2, which is the correct behaviour and gives you no signal. If your users are predominantly on corporate networks, 4% may simply be the ceiling.

  5. Client support. Chrome, Edge, Firefox and Safari all support HTTP/3, but enterprise-managed browsers may have it disabled by policy, and non-browser clients — mobile SDKs, server-side integrations, monitoring — mostly do not support it at all (§11.7 shows even curl commonly does not). Check the user-agent breakdown of the HTTP/2 traffic; if it is dominated by API clients rather than browsers, that is the answer.

  6. Is ma too short? alt-svc: h3=":443"; ma=3600 means the client forgets after an hour. Cloudflare uses 86400 and Google 2592000 (30 days) for good reason.

  7. Happy Eyeballs for QUIC. Some clients race an HTTP/3 attempt against HTTP/2 and take whichever completes first. If your QUIC endpoint is slower to respond — a cold path, a different anycast footprint, an under-provisioned QUIC tier — HTTP/2 wins the race every time even though HTTP/3 works.

The diagnostic that settles it fastest: open your own site in Chrome with the network panel's Protocol column enabled, twice. If the second load shows h3 and production traffic does not, the problem is population — network blocking or client mix — not configuration. If your own second load still shows h2, it is configuration, and steps 2 and 3 will find it.

Exercise 11.3 — What breaks operationally? Your organisation moves its public edge to HTTP/3. Which of your existing monitoring, debugging and security tooling stops working, and what replaces each?

Solution

Everything that reads TCP state, everything that reads packet headers, and everything that terminates TLS separately.

Monitoring. Connection-state dashboards built on ss -tan (§3.9) go blank — there are no TCP connections to count. TCP retransmission counters, cwnd and RTT from ss -tin (§5.7) all vanish, and with them the diagnostic sequence that section teaches. Replacement: the QUIC terminator's own metrics, and qlog — a standardised JSON event log for QUIC connections, supported by most implementations, which carries far more detail than ss ever did (per-stream state, congestion events, loss recovery) but requires new tooling to read.

Packet capture. tcpdump still captures the UDP datagrams, but the payload is encrypted including most of the header, so you see packet sizes and timing and nothing else. Wireshark cannot dissect it without keys. Replacement: export TLS keys via SSLKEYLOGFILE for a test client, which lets Wireshark decrypt; and for production, qlog rather than packet capture. This is a genuine loss of capability, and it is deliberate — the same encryption that stops middleboxes ossifying the protocol stops you inspecting it.

Load balancers. An L4 balancer hashing the four-tuple (§12.4) breaks connection migration outright: the client's new address hashes to a different backend and the connection dies, defeating the feature. Replacement: a QUIC-aware balancer routing on connection ID (§11.4), with server IDs encoded into the IDs the servers issue.

Firewalls and IDS. Rules permitting TCP 443 do not permit UDP 443, so the first thing to check is whether your own egress and ingress rules allow it. Any IDS doing HTTP inspection on the wire sees nothing. Replacement: inspect at the terminator, where the traffic is plaintext, rather than on the network — which is the direction security tooling has been moving anyway.

DDoS protection. UDP-based volumetric attacks are a different profile from SYN floods (§3.4), and SYN-cookie-style defences do not apply. QUIC has its own address-validation mechanism — the Retry packet — which serves the same purpose. Replacement: ensure your terminator implements Retry and the 3× amplification limit (§11.8), and that upstream scrubbing understands QUIC rather than treating all UDP as suspicious.

Health checks and synthetic monitoring. Most check tooling speaks HTTP/1.1 or HTTP/2 only (§11.7), so your synthetics will silently keep testing the HTTP/2 path and tell you nothing about HTTP/3. Replacement: at minimum, one synthetic per region that forces --http3-only, so an HTTP/3-specific failure is detected rather than masked by fallback.

The strategic point: because clients fall back to HTTP/2 automatically, an HTTP/3 outage is invisible — traffic quietly shifts and nothing alerts. You must monitor the protocol distribution itself as a first-class metric. A sudden drop in the HTTP/3 share is the only signal that your QUIC path is broken, and without it the failure can persist for weeks.

SECTION 12

Proxies, Load Balancers and CDNs

The four-layer model of §1.1 has an unofficial fifth layer that appears in no RFC and is present in almost every real connection: the path. When you request https://example.com, you are almost certainly not talking to a machine that anybody at that organisation operates. You are talking to a CDN edge node a few milliseconds away, which may be talking to a load balancer, which is talking to a reverse proxy, which is talking to an application server.

Each of these is an HTTP implementation that parses your request, may modify it, and re-issues it. Every one is a place where the meaning can change — and the difference between a connection that works and one that fails inexplicably is very often something in this section.

12.1 Who Are You Actually Talking To?

Start with evidence. Two requests to example.com, a second apart:

curl -sSI https://example.com | grep -iE 'server|age|cf-cache-status|cf-ray'
sleep 1
curl -sSI https://example.com | grep -iE 'age|cf-cache-status|cf-ray'

server: cloudflare
age: 11771
cf-cache-status: HIT
cf-ray: a2fefe1cca026d55-GRU
age: 11772
cf-cache-status: HIT
cf-ray: a2fefe214d4af1d7-GRU

Read what that tells you. cf-cache-status: HIT — the response came from a cache, and the origin server was not contacted at all. age: 11771 — the cached copy is 11,771 seconds old, about three and a quarter hours. And in the second request the age is 11,772: it incremented by exactly one second, which is the cache entry ageing in real time, the same phenomenon as the DNS TTL countdown in §7.4.

cf-ray: …-GRU names the edge location: GRU is the IATA code for São Paulo, which is where this course was written. A reader in Frankfurt requesting the same URL gets a different suffix and a different machine.

Some CDNs will tell you directly:

curl -sS https://cloudflare.com/cdn-cgi/trace

h=cloudflare.com
ip=45.165.23.69
ts=1787539231.000
visit_scheme=https
uag=curl/8.18.0
colo=GRU
http=http/2
loc=BR
tls=TLSv1.3
sni=plaintext

Three things in that output are worth pausing on. ip=45.165.23.69 is this client's public address — compare it with the 192.168.2.12 from §1.7, and you are looking at NAT (§2.6) from the outside. colo=GRU confirms the edge. And sni=plaintext is §8.5's leak stated by the server itself: the hostname was visible on the wire to everyone along the path, even though everything else was encrypted. That field exists because Cloudflare supports Encrypted Client Hello and will report encrypted when it is used.

12.2 Forward Proxies

A forward proxy sits in front of clients and is configured by them. The client sends its request to the proxy, using absolute-form (§9.2) so the proxy knows where to go:

GET http://example.com/path HTTP/1.1
Host: example.com

Forward proxies exist for four reasons, and they are worth distinguishing because they have different failure modes:

  • Egress control. A corporate network permits outbound HTTP only through a proxy that enforces an allowlist and logs everything. Your service inside such a network must be configured with HTTP_PROXY/HTTPS_PROXY, and the classic failure is that one library reads those variables and another does not.
  • Caching for many clients. Once common, now largely obsolete: near-universal HTTPS means a shared proxy cannot read or cache the content without terminating TLS.
  • Anonymity. The origin sees the proxy's address, not the client's.
  • Inspection. Which requires breaking TLS, and is §12.5.

12.3 CONNECT and Tunnelling

A forward proxy cannot read an HTTPS request — the URL is inside the encrypted stream. So the client asks for a tunnel first, using the CONNECT method with authority-form (§9.2). The CONNECT method is unlike every other HTTP method in that its success does not produce a response body — it produces a change in what the connection is:

CONNECT example.com:443 HTTP/1.1
Host: example.com:443
Proxy-Authorization: Basic dXNlcjpwYXNz

HTTP/1.1 200 Connection Established

After the 200, the proxy stops being an HTTP participant and becomes a blind byte relay. The client performs its TLS handshake through the tunnel, directly with the origin, and the proxy sees only ciphertext.

Two consequences worth knowing. The proxy still learns the destination hostname — it is right there in the CONNECT line, in plaintext — so tunnelling gives confidentiality of content, not of destination. And CONNECT is not restricted to HTTPS: it will relay any TCP protocol, which is why an open CONNECT proxy is a serious misconfiguration. It can be used to reach internal services, relay spam, or scan a private network from outside. Any proxy accepting CONNECT must restrict the ports it will relay to — 443 and little else.

12.4 Reverse Proxies and Load Balancers

A reverse proxy sits in front of servers and is invisible to clients. The client believes it is talking to the origin; the reverse proxy accepts the connection, chooses a backend, and forwards the request. nginx, HAProxy, Envoy, and every cloud load balancer work this way.

The critical distinction is which layer it operates at, because it determines what the proxy can do and what it costs:

Layer 4 (transport) Layer 7 (application)
Sees Addresses and ports The full HTTP request
Routing on Four-tuple hash, connection count Path, headers, method, cookie
TLS Passes through untouched Must terminate it
Per-connection cost Very low; can splice in kernel Parses and re-issues every request
Requests per connection All go to one backend Each may go to a different backend
Health checks Can it accept a TCP connection? Does /healthz return 200?
Retries a failed request No — it never saw a request Yes, if idempotent

The names come from the OSI numbering rather than §1.1's four layers: layer 4 is the transport (TCP), layer 7 the application (HTTP). A layer 4 balancer can therefore see only addresses and ports, and a layer 7 one must decrypt and parse. An L4 balancer distributes connections; an L7 balancer distributes requests. That single sentence explains most of the operational differences. With HTTP/2 or HTTP/1.1 keep-alive, one connection carries many requests, so an L4 balancer pins a client to one backend for the connection's whole life. If your clients are ten services holding persistent connections, an L4 balancer in front of twenty backends will leave most of them idle — a genuinely common and confusing load-imbalance bug.

TLS termination is the row with the largest architectural consequences. An L7 proxy must decrypt to read the request, so it holds the private key and sees every byte in plaintext — which is what makes routing, caching, header rewriting and WAF inspection possible at all. Three arrangements exist and the choice is worth making deliberately:

  • TLS termination at the edge, plaintext behind it. Simplest and fastest; acceptable only when the network behind the proxy is genuinely trusted, which §8's exercise argues is rarer than people assume.
  • TLS termination and re-encryption. The proxy decrypts, inspects, and opens its own TLS connection to the backend. Costs two handshakes and full CPU on both, and is the standard arrangement in any regulated environment.
  • TLS passthrough. An L4 balancer forwards the encrypted bytes untouched, so the backend terminates and the balancer never holds a key. It cannot route on anything above the transport — though it can read the SNI hostname (§8.5), which is plaintext, and that is exactly how an L4 balancer performs hostname-based routing without decrypting.

The three routing algorithms worth knowing:

  • Round-robin — simple, and blind to how long requests take. One slow endpoint mixed with fast ones distributes work badly.
  • Least connections — send to the backend with fewest in flight. Usually the best default, because it self-corrects when a backend slows down.
  • Consistent hashing — the same key always reaches the same backend. Essential for cache locality, and its property is that adding or removing a backend remaps only 1/n of keys rather than all of them.

Power of two random choices deserves a mention because it is the algorithm most modern balancers actually use: pick two backends at random and send to the less loaded of the two. It performs nearly as well as true least-connections while requiring no global state, which matters when the balancer itself is distributed.

12.5 What the Proxy Changes

A reverse proxy terminates the client's connection and makes its own. That means the backend sees the proxy's address, not the client's, and everything derived from the client's address — rate limiting, geolocation, audit logging, IP allowlists — is wrong unless the proxy passes it along.

The conventional mechanism is X-Forwarded-For, a comma-separated list appended to by each hop:

X-Forwarded-For: 203.0.113.7, 198.51.100.4, 10.0.1.9
X-Forwarded-Proto: https
X-Forwarded-Host: example.com

RFC 7239 standardised this as a single Forwarded header, which is better designed and less widely deployed. The Forwarded header carries the protocol, host and original address in one structured value rather than three ad-hoc X- headers, which removes the ambiguity about how they correspond:

Forwarded: for=203.0.113.7;proto=https;host=example.com, for=198.51.100.4

These headers are attacker-controlled unless you handle them correctly, and getting it wrong is a real vulnerability. A client can simply send X-Forwarded-For: 127.0.0.1 and, if the application trusts the leftmost value, bypass an IP allowlist or attribute its actions to someone else. The rule:

def client_ip(xff_header: str, peer: str, trusted_proxies: int) -> str:
    """The client address, counting back from the peer through hops you control.

    Never trust the leftmost entry: anything the client sent is in the list too.
    Only the entries appended by *your own* proxies are trustworthy, and you know
    how many of those there are.
    """
    chain = [h.strip() for h in xff_header.split(",") if h.strip()] + [peer]
    if trusted_proxies >= len(chain):
        raise ValueError("trusted_proxies exceeds the chain length")
    return chain[-(trusted_proxies + 1)]

# An attacker behind one CDN and one load balancer, both of which we operate.
# Each proxy appends the address it received the request FROM.
forged  = "127.0.0.1, 10.0.0.1"          # invented by the attacker, sent in the request
by_cdn  = ", 203.0.113.7"                # the CDN appends the attacker's real address
by_lb   = ", 198.51.100.4"               # the balancer appends the CDN's address
xff     = forged + by_cdn + by_lb
peer    = "10.0.1.9"                     # the balancer, as the app's socket sees it

print("full chain:          ", xff, "| peer", peer)
print("naive (leftmost):    ", xff.split(",")[0].strip())
print("correct (2 hops):    ", client_ip(xff, peer, trusted_proxies=2))

full chain:           127.0.0.1, 10.0.0.1, 203.0.113.7, 198.51.100.4 | peer 10.0.1.9
naive (leftmost):     127.0.0.1
correct (2 hops):     203.0.113.7

The naive reading returns the attacker's forgery. Count back from the peer by the number of proxies you actually operate — a number you know and the attacker cannot change, because they can prepend entries but cannot remove the ones your proxies append.

Getting the count right matters, and it is easy to be off by one. Two trusted proxies append two entries, so the client's address is the third from the right of the combined chain. Set trusted_proxies too low and you return the CDN's address for every request; too high and you are back to reading attacker-controlled data. And the count changes when the architecture does — adding a WAF in front of the CDN silently breaks this, which is an argument for having the proxy set a single header you trust (CF-Connecting-IP, True-Client-IP) and stripping any inbound copy of it at the edge.

Other things a proxy changes, each of which has caused a production incident somewhere:

  • HTTP version. A CDN speaking HTTP/2 to the client may speak HTTP/1.1 to the origin, reintroducing request smuggling (§9.8) at that boundary.
  • Header normalisation. Case is changed, duplicates are merged, unknown headers are sometimes dropped. Never rely on receiving a header exactly as sent.
  • Buffering. Many proxies buffer the entire response before forwarding, which breaks streaming and Server-Sent Events. nginx needs proxy_buffering off for those.
  • Timeouts. The proxy has its own, usually shorter than the backend's, and a request that exceeds it becomes a 504 even though the backend completed successfully. §14.3 argues for a timeout budget that accounts for every hop.
  • Body size limits. A default that rejects a large upload with 413 before the backend sees it.

12.6 CDNs

A content delivery network is a globally distributed set of reverse proxies — edge servers — placed close to users, with the origin server behind them. It does two distinct things, and conflating them causes bad decisions:

It caches. A cacheable response is served from the edge without contacting the origin — that is the cf-cache-status: HIT in §12.1.
It shortens the round trip, even for uncacheable requests. The TCP and TLS handshakes complete against an edge node milliseconds away rather than a server on another continent, and the edge-to-origin leg runs over a warm, pre-established, well-tuned connection.

The second is underappreciated and is why putting a CDN in front of a purely dynamic API still helps:

rtt_origin, rtt_edge, rtt_edge_to_origin = 240, 12, 235

cold_direct = 3 * rtt_origin                       # TCP + TLS + request
cold_cdn_miss = 3 * rtt_edge + rtt_edge_to_origin  # handshakes local, one warm hop back
cdn_hit = 3 * rtt_edge

print(f"direct to origin, cold      {cold_direct:>4} ms")
print(f"via CDN, cache MISS         {cold_cdn_miss:>4} ms   ({cold_direct / cold_cdn_miss:.1f}x better)")
print(f"via CDN, cache HIT          {cdn_hit:>4} ms   ({cold_direct / cdn_hit:.0f}x better)")

direct to origin, cold       720 ms
via CDN, cache MISS          271 ms   (2.7x better)
via CDN, cache HIT            36 ms   (20x better)

A cache miss through a CDN is still 2.7× faster than going direct, because two of the three round trips now happen locally. That is the argument for fronting an API that caches nothing at all.

The cache key determines what counts as the same response, and getting it wrong is the most common CDN mistake in both directions. By default the cache key is method plus full URL. Adding Vary: Accept-Encoding splits it by encoding, which is correct. Adding Vary: User-Agent splits it thousands of ways and destroys the hit rate. Forgetting to vary on something that matters — a language, an authorisation state — serves one user's content to another, which is the same failure §10 of the Backend course warns about with Cache-Control: private.

Two more mechanisms worth knowing by name. Origin shield designates one edge location as the only one allowed to contact the origin, so a hundred edges missing simultaneously produce one origin request rather than a hundred — a thundering herd defence. And stale-while-revalidate lets an edge serve a slightly stale response immediately while fetching a fresh one from the origin server in the background, which converts a latency spike into a small staleness window and is almost always the right trade.

12.7 Health Checks and Draining

A load balancer must know which backends can serve traffic, and the two questions it can ask are not the same:

  • Liveness — is the process alive? A failure means restart it.
  • Readiness — should it receive traffic now? A failure means remove it from the pool, but do not restart it.

Conflating them causes a specific and severe failure. If your readiness probe checks the database, then a database outage makes every instance report unready, the load balancer removes all of them, and a degraded service becomes a total outage. A readiness check should test what this instance needs to serve a request, not what its dependencies are doing — dependency health belongs in the response to a real request, where it can be a 503 with a Retry-After rather than a removal from the pool.

The other half is connection draining. When an instance is removed — a deploy, a scale-down — it has in-flight requests and open keep-alive connections. Terminating it immediately fails all of them. The correct sequence:

  1. Mark the instance unready, so the balancer stops sending new requests.
  2. Wait for in-flight requests to complete, up to a bounded grace period.
  3. Send Connection: close (HTTP/1.1) or GOAWAY (HTTP/2, §10.6) so clients open a new connection elsewhere.
  4. Only then terminate.

Step 3 is the one that gets skipped, and its absence is why rolling deploys produce a burst of client errors even when everything else is configured correctly: the client's pooled connection to the old instance is still open, it sends a request, and the connection dies underneath it (§14.4).

12.8 Reading the Path

When something along the path is misbehaving, these establish where:

curl -sSI https://example.com | grep -iE 'server|via|x-cache|cf-ray|age'

Via is the standardised header naming each proxy; many strip it. Server, X-Cache, CF-Ray, X-Served-By and Age are vendor-specific and more commonly present. Age is the giveaway that a cache is involved at all — its presence means the response was stored somewhere, and its value is how long ago.

curl -sS --resolve example.com:443:104.20.23.154 -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' https://example.com

--resolve pins the connection to one address while keeping the correct hostname for SNI and Host. This is the single most useful command for path debugging: it lets you test one specific edge node or backend rather than whichever the DNS round-robin happens to give you, which is how you find the one machine in twenty that is misconfigured (§8.10).

curl -sS -o /dev/null -w 'dns=%{time_namelookup} tcp=%{time_connect} tls=%{time_appconnect} ttfb=%{time_starttransfer}\n' https://example.com

The phase breakdown localises latency to a layer, and §13.3 develops it into a full budget.

To determine whether the origin or the edge is at fault, bypass the CDN: point at the origin's address directly with --resolve. If the problem follows, it is the origin; if it does not, it is the edge or the path to it. That one comparison halves the search space, and it is worth having the origin address recorded somewhere findable before you need it at three in the morning.

Exercises

Exercise 12.1 — The load imbalance. Ten client services hold persistent HTTP/2 connections to an L4 load balancer in front of twenty backends. Three backends are at 90% CPU and the rest are near idle. Explain, and give two fixes.

Solution

An L4 balancer distributes connections, not requests (§12.4), and there are only ten connections to distribute among twenty backends.

The arithmetic is the whole diagnosis: ten long-lived connections hashed across twenty backends leaves at least ten backends with no connection at all, and the hash is unlikely to be even — several backends get two, many get zero. Then HTTP/2 multiplexing (§10.3) concentrates the damage: each connection carries all of that client's requests, so a client with heavy traffic loads one backend completely while nineteen others idle. The balancer is behaving exactly as designed; the design does not fit the traffic.

Note the diagnostic signature, which distinguishes this from an ordinary hot-spot: the number of busy backends is close to the number of client connections, not to anything about the requests.

Two fixes:

  1. Use an L7 load balancer. It parses each request and can route it independently, so ten connections carrying ten thousand requests spread across all twenty backends. This is the correct fix, and its cost is that the balancer must terminate TLS and parse HTTP — real CPU, and a place where headers can be altered (§12.5).

  2. Make the clients open more connections and cycle them. If L7 is not available, have each client maintain a pool of several connections rather than one, and give connections a maximum lifetime (say 60 seconds) so they are periodically re-hashed to different backends. This is a mitigation rather than a fix — the distribution is still random and still lumpy — but it is a client-side change that needs no infrastructure work.

A third option worth knowing: client-side load balancing, where each client resolves all backend addresses and chooses among them itself, using least-connections or power-of-two-choices. This is what gRPC does natively and what a service mesh does transparently, and it gives the best distribution of the three because the decision is made per request with knowledge of what that client is doing. It requires service discovery, which is why it is common inside meshes and rare outside them.

Exercise 12.2 — Whose fault is the 504? Users report intermittent 504 Gateway Timeout. Your architecture is CDN → ALB → nginx → application. Application logs show the requests completing successfully in 45 seconds. Diagnose, and specify the correct configuration.

Solution

A timeout mismatch: some hop's timeout is shorter than 45 seconds, and it gives up while the application is still working. The application completing successfully is the clue — this is not a failure, it is a disagreement about how long is acceptable.

A 504 is emitted by a gateway, so the layer that produced it is the one whose timeout expired. Find it by elimination, and the response headers name the culprit:

curl -sS -o /dev/null -D - "https://site/slow-endpoint" | grep -iE 'server|cf-ray|via|x-amzn'

A 504 carrying server: cloudflare came from the CDN; one carrying an AWS request id came from the ALB. Then read each layer's configured timeout — typical defaults are 100 s for a CDN, 60 s for an ALB, 60 s for nginx's proxy_read_timeout, and often unlimited in the application.

The correct configuration is a decreasing budget from the outside in, so that the innermost component always gives up first and can return a meaningful error rather than being cut off:

CDN                 100 s
ALB                  90 s
nginx proxy_read     80 s
application           70 s   <- the only one that should ever fire

Each layer must be longer than the one it fronts, so a timeout surfaces as an application-generated 503 or 504 with a useful body, not as an opaque gateway error with no request id.

But the configuration is not the real fix. A 45-second request is the actual problem, and no timeout arrangement makes it acceptable — it is far beyond what any user waits for, it holds a connection and a worker for the duration, and it will fail whenever any hop hiccups. The right answer is §12 of the Backend course's asynchronous pattern: accept the work with 202, return a status URL, and let the client poll or subscribe. Where that is not possible, stream the response (§13 of that course) so bytes flow immediately and no idle timeout can fire.

One more thing to check, because it produces the same symptom and is missed: is it the read timeout or the idle timeout? A request that streams data every 10 seconds for 45 seconds passes a 60-second read timeout but may still be killed by an idle timeout if a proxy is buffering the response (§12.5) and therefore sees no bytes at all.

Exercise 12.3 — Design the caching. You are putting a CDN in front of an application with: static assets with content-hashed filenames, an HTML shell, a public product catalogue API, and a per-user account API. Specify the cache configuration for each, and say how you would purge.

Solution

Static assets — cache forever, at the edge and in the browser.

Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable

The filename contains a content hash, so the URL changes whenever the content does and a stale response is impossible. immutable tells the browser not even to revalidate on a reload. Never purge these — a new deploy produces new URLs, and the old ones can expire naturally.

The HTML shell — short edge cache, no browser cache, revalidate.

Cache-Control: public, max-age=0, s-maxage=60, stale-while-revalidate=600

max-age=0 for the browser and s-maxage=60 for the shared cache: users always get a fresh check, the edge absorbs the load, and stale-while-revalidate means the sixty-first-second request is served instantly from a stale copy while the edge refreshes behind it. This document references the hashed asset URLs, so it is the one thing that must update promptly on deploy — purge it explicitly as the last step of every deploy.

The public catalogue API — cache with a deliberate key, and vary carefully.

Cache-Control: public, s-maxage=300, stale-while-revalidate=60
Vary: Accept-Encoding, Accept-Language
ETag: "sha256-…"

Vary only on what genuinely changes the bytes. Do not vary on User-Agent — it fragments the cache into thousands of variants and the hit rate collapses. Include query parameters that matter in the cache key and strip ones that do not: tracking parameters like utm_source change nothing about the response and, left in the key, guarantee a miss on every shared link. Purge by tag or surrogate key when a product changes, rather than by URL, since one product appears in many list responses.

The per-user account API — do not cache at the edge, ever.

Cache-Control: private, no-store

private forbids shared caches; no-store forbids storing it anywhere. This is the case where a mistake is a security incident rather than a performance problem — a cached authenticated response served to the next user is a data breach, and it has happened to well-known companies. Two defences beyond the header: configure the CDN to bypass the cache entirely for any request carrying an Authorization header or a session cookie, so a missing header on one endpoint cannot leak; and verify it, by requesting an authenticated endpoint twice as different users and confirming cf-cache-status never reports HIT.

Purging strategy overall. Prefer surrogate keys (tag responses with the entities they contain, purge by tag) over URL purging, which requires knowing every URL an entity appears in. Prefer versioned URLs over purging where possible — the static assets need no purge mechanism at all, which is why content hashing is worth the build complexity. And note that purge is eventually consistent across a global network: it takes seconds to tens of seconds to reach every edge, so it is not a safety mechanism. If serving a stale response for thirty seconds would be a correctness problem, do not cache it.

SECTION 13

Latency, Throughput and the Browser's Network Stack

Every previous section explained a mechanism. This one is about the number those mechanisms add up to, and it exists because almost all performance work on networked applications is aimed at the wrong quantity. Teams buy bandwidth, compress payloads and optimise queries while the actual cost of their requests is round trips they never counted.

The organising idea is simple enough to state in one line and takes a section to make usable: bandwidth is a resource you can buy, and latency is a budget set by physics and by how many times you cross it.

13.1 The Numbers, Measured

Start with real measurements from the machine this course was written on, because intuitions about these are usually off by an order of magnitude:

ping -c 3 -q 127.0.0.1     | tail -1
ping -c 3 -q 192.168.2.1   | tail -1
ping -c 3 -q example.com   | tail -1
ping -c 3 -q 8.8.8.8       | tail -1

rtt min/avg/max/mdev = 0.158/0.288/0.527/0.169 ms      loopback
rtt min/avg/max/mdev = 4.232/6.244/8.249/1.639 ms      the local gateway, over Wi-Fi
rtt min/avg/max/mdev = 47.006/47.211/47.494/0.206 ms   a CDN edge
rtt min/avg/max/mdev = 52.491/57.234/65.620/5.946 ms   a public resolver

Four scales, each about an order of magnitude apart. Two observations that are more useful than the numbers themselves:

The local gateway is 6 ms, not 0.5 ms. That is Wi-Fi, and it is twenty times the loopback figure — the first hop of a home network is often a larger fraction of total latency than anything in the internet core. Note its mdev of 1.6 ms, five times the CDN's: the wireless link is also the least predictable hop.

The CDN edge is more stable than the resolver, despite being at a similar distance: 0.2 ms of deviation against 5.9 ms. That is the difference between a well-provisioned anycast endpoint a few hops away and one whose path crosses more of the internet.

Here is the wider scale, with the physics stated explicitly:

C_FIBRE_KM_PER_MS = 200          # light in glass, ~2/3 of c

places = [("same machine", 0), ("same rack", 0), ("same data centre", 1),
          ("same city", 30), ("neighbouring country", 800),
          ("across a continent", 4000), ("across an ocean", 6000),
          ("halfway round the world", 20000)]

print(f"{'':26}{'distance':>10}{'physics floor':>15}{'typical real':>14}")
for name, km in places:
    floor = 2 * km / C_FIBRE_KM_PER_MS          # round trip
    real = {0: 0.3, 1: 0.5, 30: 5, 800: 20, 4000: 70, 6000: 90, 20000: 250}[km]
    print(f"{name:26}{km:>8} km{floor:>13.1f} ms{real:>12} ms")

                            distance  physics floor  typical real
same machine                     0 km          0.0 ms         0.3 ms
same rack                        0 km          0.0 ms         0.3 ms
same data centre                 1 km          0.0 ms         0.5 ms
same city                       30 km          0.3 ms           5 ms
neighbouring country           800 km          8.0 ms          20 ms
across a continent            4000 km         40.0 ms          70 ms
across an ocean               6000 km         60.0 ms          90 ms
halfway round the world      20000 km        200.0 ms         250 ms

The physics floor is not a rhetorical device. London to Sydney is 17,000 km, and no amount of money reduces the round trip below about 170 ms. Real figures run 1.2× to 2× the floor because cables do not follow great circles and each router adds processing and queueing. When someone proposes to make a cross-ocean API call faster, the honest first question is how far it is from the floor — if it is 90 ms on a 60 ms floor, there is nothing to win.

13.2 Latency, Throughput, Jitter

Three quantities, routinely conflated:

Latency — how long one round-trip time takes. Set by distance and by queueing. Cannot be bought, which is why every budget in this section counts round trips rather than bytes.
Throughput — bytes per second once data is flowing. Set by the bottleneck link and by the window (§4.4). Can be bought.
Jitter — variation in latency. Set by queueing (§5.8). Invisible in an average and decisive for anything interactive.

Total delay decomposes into four parts, and knowing which one dominates tells you what to fix:

  • Propagation delay — distance ÷ the speed of light in the medium. Fixed by geography and irreducible: the figures in §13.1's table are the propagation delay plus a little. Fix only by moving closer (§12.6).
  • Transmission — bytes ÷ link rate. A 1500-byte packet on 1 Gbit/s is 12 µs; on 1 Mbit/s it is 12 ms. Fix by sending fewer bytes, or by a faster link.
  • Queueing delay — waiting behind other packets in a buffer. Highly variable, and the source of jitter and bufferbloat (§5.5). It is the only one of the four that other people's traffic controls, and queueing delay is what AQM (§5.8) exists to bound.
  • Processing — routers, proxies, TLS, your application. Usually small at each hop and there are many hops.

The trade-off between the first two is worth making concrete, because it decides whether compression is worth anything:

def transfer_ms(bytes_, mbps, rtt_ms, rtts_of_setup=0):
    return rtts_of_setup * rtt_ms + bytes_ * 8 / (mbps * 1e6) * 1000

for label, size in [("2 KB API response", 2_000), ("200 KB page", 200_000),
                    ("5 MB video segment", 5_000_000)]:
    for mbps, rtt in [(100, 90), (1000, 90)]:
        t = transfer_ms(size, mbps, rtt, rtts_of_setup=1)
        wire = transfer_ms(size, mbps, rtt)
        print(f"{label:<20}{mbps:>5} Mbit/s  {rtt} ms RTT  ->  {t:>7.1f} ms "
              f"({100 * wire / t:>4.1f}% of it on the wire)")
    print()

2 KB API response     100 Mbit/s  90 ms RTT  ->     90.2 ms ( 0.2% of it on the wire)
2 KB API response    1000 Mbit/s  90 ms RTT  ->     90.0 ms ( 0.0% of it on the wire)

200 KB page           100 Mbit/s  90 ms RTT  ->    106.0 ms (15.1% of it on the wire)
200 KB page          1000 Mbit/s  90 ms RTT  ->     91.6 ms ( 1.7% of it on the wire)

5 MB video segment    100 Mbit/s  90 ms RTT  ->    490.0 ms (81.6% of it on the wire)
5 MB video segment   1000 Mbit/s  90 ms RTT  ->    130.0 ms (30.8% of it on the wire)

A tenfold bandwidth increase makes the API response 0.2 ms faster and the video segment 3.8× faster. (Note the last row's on-the-wire share falls from 81.6% to 30.8% — at 1 Gbit/s even a 5 MB segment is becoming latency-bound.) That single table should settle most arguments about where to spend effort: below roughly 100 KB, latency is everything and bandwidth is nothing; above a few megabytes, the reverse. And note this ignores slow start (§5.2), which makes the small-transfer case worse than shown.

13.3 A Latency Budget

The useful discipline is to account for every round trip before optimising anything.

rtt = 90

budget = [
    ("DNS lookup (uncached)",           1, "eliminate: cache, or an HTTPS record (§11.6)"),
    ("TCP handshake",                   1, "eliminate: connection reuse"),
    ("TLS handshake",                   1, "eliminate: reuse; halve with HTTP/3 (§11.2)"),
    ("HTTP request -> first byte",      1, "irreducible — this is the actual request"),
    ("redirect to the canonical URL",   3, "eliminate: link to the right URL"),
    ("a second, dependent API call",    1, "eliminate: one endpoint that returns both"),
]

total = 0
print(f"{'phase':<34}{'RTTs':>5}{'ms':>7}   how to remove it")
for name, rtts, fix in budget:
    total += rtts
    print(f"{name:<34}{rtts:>5}{rtts * rtt:>7}   {fix}")
print(f"{'':<34}{'-' * 5:>5}{'-' * 7:>7}")
print(f"{'total':<34}{total:>5}{total * rtt:>7} ms")
print(f"{'irreducible':<34}{1:>5}{rtt:>7} ms   ({total}x improvement available)")

phase                              RTTs     ms   how to remove it
DNS lookup (uncached)                 1     90   eliminate: cache, or an HTTPS record (§11.6)
TCP handshake                         1     90   eliminate: connection reuse
TLS handshake                         1     90   eliminate: reuse; halve with HTTP/3 (§11.2)
HTTP request -> first byte            1     90   irreducible — this is the actual request
redirect to the canonical URL         3    270   eliminate: link to the right URL
a second, dependent API call          1     90   eliminate: one endpoint that returns both
                                  ------------
total                                 8    720 ms
irreducible                           1     90 ms   (8x improvement available)

The redirect costs three round trips, not one — a redirect to a different origin needs its own DNS, TCP and TLS before it can serve the 301. An http:// to https:// redirect is the most common instance and is entirely avoidable with HSTS, which makes the browser rewrite the scheme locally with no network involvement.

The general method: count round trips, not milliseconds. Round trips are the invariant; milliseconds vary by user. A change that removes a round trip helps everyone proportionally, and it helps your most distant users most.

13.4 The First 14 KB

Slow start (§5.2) begins with a ten-segment congestion window — about 14.6 KB with a 1448-byte MSS. The first 14 KB of any response arrives in one round trip; the next portion costs another.

MSS, INITIAL_CWND, rtt = 1448, 10, 90

def rtts_to_deliver(size, cwnd=INITIAL_CWND):
    sent, rounds = 0, 0
    while sent < size:
        sent += cwnd * MSS
        cwnd *= 2
        rounds += 1
    return rounds

for label, size in [("HTML, inlined critical CSS", 14_000), ("typical HTML shell", 40_000),
                    ("heavy page", 200_000), ("very heavy page", 1_000_000)]:
    r = rtts_to_deliver(size)
    print(f"{label:<30}{size:>9,} B  {r} RTT{'s' if r > 1 else ' '}  {r * rtt:>4} ms")

HTML, inlined critical CSS       14,000 B  1 RTT     90 ms
typical HTML shell               40,000 B  2 RTTs   180 ms
heavy page                      200,000 B  4 RTTs   360 ms
very heavy page               1,000,000 B  7 RTTs   630 ms

This is where the "inline critical CSS" advice comes from, and it is one of the few front-end performance rules with a precise mechanical justification. It is also why the returns diminish so sharply: getting a page from 200 KB to 40 KB saves two round trips; getting it from 40 KB to 14 KB saves one more; below that, nothing.

The number is not fixed. A server can raise its initial window (ip route change … initcwnd 30), and a warm connection has already grown its window, which is another reason connection reuse dominates every other optimisation.

13.5 Connection Reuse

Given §13.3, the highest-value optimisation available is to stop opening connections. A warm connection removes DNS, TCP and TLS — three round trips of the eight — and starts with a grown congestion window.

For a client, this means a connection pool: a set of established connections held open and lent out per request. A connection pool is the mechanism by which the three saved round trips of §13.3 are actually realised, and its settings decide whether they are:

Setting Guidance
Pool size per host Enough for peak concurrency; too many wastes server memory and, behind an L4 balancer, skews distribution (§12.4)
Idle timeout Shorter than the server's, or you will race a server-side close (§14.4)
Max connection lifetime Bounded — 60 s to a few minutes — so DNS changes are picked up and balancers can rebalance
Retry on idle-connection failure Required, for idempotent requests. This failure is normal, not exceptional

The idle-timeout relationship is the one that causes intermittent, unreproducible errors. If your client holds connections for 90 seconds and the server closes at 60, then between 60 and 90 seconds the client has a connection the server has already discarded. It sends a request into it and gets an ECONNRESET — not because anything is broken, but because the two timeouts disagree. Always set the client's idle timeout below the server's, and always retry idempotent requests on a fresh connection.

Two related mechanisms worth naming. Connection: keep-alive is the HTTP/1.1 default and needs no configuration (§9.5). And connection coalescing lets an HTTP/2 client reuse one connection for several hostnames that resolve to the same address and share a certificate (§10.3) — a reason to prefer one wildcard certificate over several separate ones.

13.6 Happy Eyeballs

A dual-stack client (§2.9) must choose between IPv6 and IPv4, and choosing wrong costs a full connection timeout. Happy Eyeballs (RFC 8305) removes the risk by racing:

Start the DNS A and AAAA queries together; do not wait for both.
Begin connecting to the first IPv6 address as soon as it arrives.
After a short delay — the Connection Attempt Delay, typically 250 ms — start an IPv4 attempt in parallel without cancelling the first.
Use whichever completes first; abandon the other. Remember the winner for subsequent connections.

The worst case becomes 250 ms of extra latency instead of a 130-second timeout (§14.2), and the preference for IPv6 is preserved when IPv6 works. You saw the underlying situation in §6.2, where getaddrinfo on this host demoted every IPv6 address because there was no IPv6 route.

The practical point: browsers do this and many HTTP clients do not. A backend service on a dual-stack host, using a library that tries only the first address getaddrinfo returns, will fail entirely when IPv6 is broken. Check your client, because the failure appears only when something else breaks and is therefore never caught in testing.

13.7 Compression

Compression trades CPU for bytes, and its value is exactly the transmission-time term from §13.2. Here are real measurements against one document:

for enc in identity gzip br; do
  printf '%-9s ' "$enc"
  curl -sS -o /dev/null -w '%{size_download} bytes\n' \
       -H "Accept-Encoding: $enc" https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/HTTP
done

identity  221456 bytes
gzip      29749 bytes
br        24339 bytes

7.4× for gzip, 9.1× for brotli on real HTML. The extra 18% brotli gives over gzip is worth having, and the reason is a shared static dictionary of common web strings — which also explains why brotli's advantage shrinks on data that is not text.

Three rules from those numbers:

  • Compress text; do not compress what is already compressed. JPEG, PNG, MP4, WOFF2 and anything gzipped are incompressible, and running them through gzip burns CPU to make them very slightly larger.
  • Brotli at a low level for dynamic responses, high for static. Brotli level 11 is dramatically slower than gzip and is fine for a file compressed once at build time; for a response generated per request, level 4–5 beats gzip on both ratio and speed.
  • Vary: Accept-Encoding is mandatory when compressing, or a cache may serve a compressed body to a client that cannot decode it (§12.6).

There is a security constraint that overrides all of this. Do not compress a response that mixes a secret with attacker-controlled input. Compression ratio leaks information about content, which is the BREACH attack — the same idea HPACK was designed to resist (§10.4). If a response contains a CSRF token and a reflected search term, an attacker can extract the token by observing sizes. Either do not compress such responses, or mask the secret per response.

13.8 The Browser's Resource Loading

A browser's network stack does more than issue requests, and the hints it accepts are the interface to it:

Hint Effect Use when
<link rel=dns-prefetch> Resolve a hostname early A third-party origin is used later
<link rel=preconnect> DNS + TCP + TLS early You are certain the origin will be used — otherwise it is waste
<link rel=preload> Fetch a specific resource now, at high priority A font or hero image discovered late by the parser
<link rel=prefetch> Fetch at low priority for a future navigation The next page is predictable
103 Early Hints Send preload hints before the response is ready The server takes time to generate the page

103 Early Hints (RFC 8297) is the one worth knowing about, because it is server push done right (§10.7). While the origin is still computing the response, the CDN sends a provisional 103 carrying Link: rel=preload headers. The browser starts fetching the CSS and fonts immediately, then the real response arrives. The server contributes what it knows — which resources this page needs — and the client decides what to fetch, using its own cache. That inversion is precisely what server push got wrong.

The browser's own view of all this is the waterfall — the network panel's chart of one bar per request, positioned by start time and segmented by phase. It is the single most informative thing to look at when a page is slow, and reading it is a skill:

  • Bars that start at the same instant are requests the browser discovered together and issued in parallel. Under HTTP/2 there should be many; under HTTP/1.1 you will see them arrive in groups of six (§9.9).
  • A staircase — each bar starting where the previous one ended — is sequential discovery, and it is the expensive pattern. HTML must be parsed to find the CSS, the CSS parsed to find the font, and each step costs a round trip. This is what preload and Early Hints exist to flatten.
  • A long pale segment before the coloured part is queueing or connection setup, not the server. If the waterfall shows most bars waiting rather than transferring, the problem is connection count or discovery order, not payload size.
  • One bar much longer than the rest localises the problem immediately; everything else is a distraction.

The counter-advice matters as much: each hint costs something, and hints are commonly over-applied. preconnect to an origin you never use wastes a DNS lookup and a full TLS handshake. preload on ten resources means ten things compete for the same congestion window as the HTML that is blocking rendering. Preloading everything is the same as prioritising nothing.

13.9 Tail Latency

An average latency of 50 ms tells you almost nothing about user experience. What matters is the tail — p95, p99, p99.9 — for a reason that is arithmetic rather than philosophical:

p99_single, calls = 0.01, 20                 # 1% of calls exceed the threshold
none_slow = (1 - p99_single) ** calls

print(f"one service call, p99 threshold exceeded {p99_single:.0%} of the time")
print(f"a page making {calls} calls: all fast {none_slow:.1%} of the time")
print(f"so {1 - none_slow:.1%} of page loads contain at least one slow call")
print()
for n in (1, 5, 20, 100):
    print(f"{n:>4} calls -> {1 - (1 - p99_single) ** n:>5.1%} of requests hit the p99 of something")

one service call, p99 threshold exceeded 1% of the time
a page making 20 calls: all fast 81.8% of the time
so 18.2% of page loads contain at least one slow call

   1 calls ->  1.0% of requests hit the p99 of something
   5 calls ->  4.9% of requests hit the p99 of something
  20 calls -> 18.2% of requests hit the p99 of something
 100 calls -> 63.4% of requests hit the p99 of something

A page making twenty calls hits somebody's p99 on 18% of loads. With a hundred calls — an ordinary microservice fan-out — it is 63%. The tail is not an edge case; at any realistic fan-out it is the common case, and this is why an average is actively misleading as a service-level objective.

The causes of a long tail are worth knowing because they are mostly not "the code is slow": garbage collection pauses, a cold cache, a TCP retransmission timeout (§4.2's 200 ms floor), a connection-pool wait, a DNS lookup that missed, a noisy neighbour on shared hardware, a rehashing table.

Two techniques specifically target it. Hedged requests: if a response has not arrived by the p95, send a second request to a different backend and take whichever returns first. This costs a few percent more traffic and can cut p99 dramatically — but it must only be used for idempotent requests, and it must be rate-limited so that a general slowdown does not double your load exactly when you can least afford it. And request reduction: the most reliable way to avoid hitting a tail twenty times is to make five calls instead of twenty, which is an API design decision (§4 of the Backend course) rather than a network one.

Exercises

Exercise 13.1 — Budget a page load. A single-page app in Sydney loads from a server in Frankfurt: 270 ms RTT. It does a DNS lookup, redirects example.com → www.example.com, loads a 60 KB HTML shell, then a 300 KB JS bundle, then makes two sequential API calls. Compute the time to interactive, then propose changes in order of value.

Solution 
rtt = 270
MSS, INITIAL_CWND = 1448, 10

def rtts_for(size, cwnd=INITIAL_CWND):
    sent, rounds = 0, 0
    while sent < size:
        sent += cwnd * MSS; cwnd *= 2; rounds += 1
    return rounds

steps = [("DNS", 1), ("TCP", 1), ("TLS", 1), ("request -> 301", 1),
         ("TCP to www (new origin)", 1), ("TLS to www", 1),
         ("request -> HTML", 1), ("rest of 60 KB HTML", rtts_for(60_000) - 1),
         ("JS bundle request", 1), ("300 KB bundle", rtts_for(300_000) - 1),
         ("API call 1", 1), ("API call 2 (depends on 1)", 1)]

total = 0
for name, r in steps:
    total += r
    print(f"{name:<28}{r:>3} RTT   cumulative {total * rtt:>5} ms")
print(f"\ntime to interactive: {total} RTTs = {total * rtt / 1000:.1f} s")

DNS                           1 RTT   cumulative   270 ms
TCP                           1 RTT   cumulative   540 ms
TLS                           1 RTT   cumulative   810 ms
request -> 301                1 RTT   cumulative  1080 ms
TCP to www (new origin)       1 RTT   cumulative  1350 ms
TLS to www                    1 RTT   cumulative  1620 ms
request -> HTML               1 RTT   cumulative  1890 ms
rest of 60 KB HTML            2 RTT   cumulative  2430 ms
JS bundle request             1 RTT   cumulative  2700 ms
300 KB bundle                 4 RTT   cumulative  3780 ms
API call 1                    1 RTT   cumulative  4050 ms
API call 2 (depends on 1)     1 RTT   cumulative  4320 ms

time to interactive: 16 RTTs = 4.3 s

Sixteen round trips, 4.3 seconds, and the payload is 360 KB — which at any reasonable bandwidth transmits in well under a second. Almost all of this is latency.

Changes in order of value:

  1. A CDN edge in Sydney — 3.5 RTTs at 270 ms become 3.5 at 15 ms, and the static assets are served from cache. This is the single largest win because it reduces every remaining round trip rather than removing one. Even with a cache miss on the API, the handshakes are local (§12.6).
  2. Remove the redirect — 3 RTTs, 810 ms, for nothing. Link to the canonical URL, and use HSTS preload so the scheme upgrade never touches the network (§13.3).
  3. Make the two API calls concurrent, or combine them — 1 RTT. If the second genuinely depends on the first, that is an API design problem: an endpoint returning both is worth creating.
  4. Shrink the bundle — 300 KB is 4 round trips of slow start. Code-splitting to 100 KB saves 2 (§13.4). Compression helps here too, and §13.7's 7–9× on text means this is one of the cases where it is worth real effort.
  5. Preconnect to the API origin while the HTML parses, so its handshake overlaps with the bundle download instead of following it.

Applying 1 and 2 alone takes this from 4.3 s to roughly 0.5 s. Neither is a code change, which is the general shape of this kind of work.

Exercise 13.2 — Which optimisation? For each, say whether it helps, and by roughly how much.

  1. Enabling brotli on an API returning 800-byte JSON responses.
  2. Upgrading a server's link from 1 Gbit/s to 10 Gbit/s; users are worldwide on 20 Mbit/s connections.
  3. Adding preconnect for six third-party origins in the page head.
  4. Moving from 3 sequential API calls to 1 combined call, on a 100 ms path.
  5. Raising the server's initial congestion window from 10 to 30 segments.
Solution

1 — Essentially no help, and possibly harmful. An 800-byte response transmits in microseconds; §13.2's table shows sub-2 KB responses spend 0.2% of their time on the wire. Compression saves perhaps 500 bytes and costs CPU on both ends. Worse, brotli at a high level on a per-request response adds measurable server latency. And if the response mixes a secret with reflected input, you have introduced a BREACH exposure (§13.7) for no benefit. Enable compression above a threshold — 1 KB is the usual default — and leave small responses alone.

2 — No help at all. The bottleneck is the client's 20 Mbit/s link, not the server's. A server link matters only when it is saturated in aggregate, which is a capacity question rather than a per-user latency one. This is the classic case of buying bandwidth to fix latency.

3 — Harmful as described. Each preconnect costs a DNS lookup, a TCP handshake and a TLS handshake — three round trips of work — and they compete with the HTML for the same congestion window at exactly the moment rendering is blocked. Six of them is a lot of speculative work. preconnect is worth it for one or two origins you are certain will be used soon (a font provider, your API). For the rest, dns-prefetch costs far less and captures most of the benefit.

4 — Saves 200 ms, and it is the best item on this list. Three sequential calls are three round trips; one is one. On a 100 ms path that is 300 ms → 100 ms. It also cuts the tail-latency exposure from three chances of hitting a p99 to one (§13.9). The cost is API design work and a less orthogonal interface, which is the trade §4 of the Backend course discusses.

5 — Helps responses between 14 KB and 43 KB, and nothing else. Raising initcwnd to 30 makes the first flight about 43 KB, so a 40 KB HTML shell arrives in one round trip instead of two — 90 ms saved on a 90 ms path. Below 14 KB nothing changes; well above 43 KB the saving is one round trip out of several. It is a genuine, cheap win for typical HTML documents, and it is worth knowing that it is not free: a larger initial burst is more likely to overflow a shallow buffer on a constrained path, which costs a retransmission timeout. Ten was raised from four for good reasons and thirty is above what the RFC recommends.

The ranking: 4 is unambiguously worth doing, 5 is a cheap targeted win, 1 and 2 are neutral-to-negative, and 3 is actively harmful at the scale described.

Exercise 13.3 — Explain the tail. Your API's p50 is 12 ms and its p99 is 340 ms. CPU is at 25%, the database's p99 is 8 ms, and there is no GC pressure. Give four candidate explanations and the measurement that would distinguish each.

Solution

The shape — an excellent p50 with a p99 nearly thirty times worse, on an idle machine — says requests are waiting, not computing. Four candidates:

1. Connection-pool exhaustion. Requests wait to borrow a connection to a downstream service or database. The database's own p99 of 8 ms is measured from when the query starts, so it is entirely consistent with a request that waited 300 ms for a pool slot. Measure: instrument the wait time to acquire a pool connection as a separate metric from query time. This is the single most common cause of this exact profile, and the most commonly un-instrumented.

2. TCP retransmission timeouts. §4.2 established Linux's 200 ms TCP_RTO_MIN. A single lost packet with no subsequent data to trigger fast retransmit costs 200 ms, and 340 ms is suspiciously close to 200 plus a normal request. Measure: nstat -az | grep -i retrans and ss -tin on live connections. Correlate the slow-request rate with the retransmission rate; if they move together, this is it.

3. Cold connections. Requests that arrive when the pool has no warm connection pay DNS, TCP and TLS — three round trips (§13.3). At 90 ms that is 270 ms on top of a 12 ms request. Measure: record whether each request reused a connection, and compare the two distributions. A bimodal histogram with a cluster near 12 ms and another near 280 ms is the signature.

4. Head-of-line blocking. If requests are multiplexed over one HTTP/2 connection (§10.8), a single lost packet stalls every concurrent stream. It would also produce a correlated tail — several requests slow at the same instant rather than randomly distributed. Measure: check whether slow requests cluster in time. Independent slowness points at 1 or 3; simultaneous slowness across unrelated requests points at 4 or at a GC-style pause.

The measurement that separates all four at once, and the one to build first: a span breakdown per request — time waiting for a pool connection, time in DNS, time in handshake, time waiting for the downstream response, time computing. A p99 that is 300 ms of pool wait and 12 ms of everything else answers the question immediately, and this is exactly what §15.3's distributed tracing exists to provide.

One thing not to do: raise the timeout. It converts a slow request into a slow request, and removes your only signal.

SECTION 14

Failure Modes, Timeouts and Retries

Every mechanism in this course works correctly and still produces outages, because the failure modes of a distributed system are not the failure modes of its parts. A network does not simply work or not work: it works slowly, works in one direction, works for small messages and not large ones, works until a cache expires, or reports success for a request that was never processed.

This section is the catalogue. It is deliberately the least theoretical section in the course, because these are the things that will actually page you.

14.1 The Two Hard Truths

Truth one: you cannot distinguish a slow peer from a dead one. This is not an implementation limitation, it is a property of asynchronous networks. When you send a request and hear nothing, the possibilities are: the request was lost; the request arrived and is being processed slowly; the request arrived, was processed, and the response was lost; or the peer is gone. These are indistinguishable from the outside, forever. The only tool is a timeout, and a timeout is a guess.

Truth two: a timeout tells you nothing about what happened. This follows from the first and is the one that has consequences for correctness. When a POST /payments times out, the payment may have been taken. Retrying may charge twice; not retrying may lose it. There is no way to know without asking.

Both are instances of the same underlying condition: partial failure, where some part of a distributed operation has succeeded and you cannot determine which. A single machine either runs your code or does not; a network gives you partial failure as the normal case, and every pattern in this section is a way of living with it. In particular they mean that retrying is a correctness decision, not a resilience decision — which is why the answer is idempotency (§11 of the Backend course) rather than a better retry library.

14.2 Where the Default Timeouts Are

Almost every default in the stack is far longer than any application should tolerate:

print("connect(): the Linux SYN retry schedule with tcp_syn_retries = 6")
total = 0
for i in range(7):
    delay = 2 ** i
    print(f"   SYN {i + 1}: sent at t={total:>3}s, next retry in {delay:>2}s")
    total += delay
print(f"   gives up at t={total}s\n")

print(f"{'layer':<34}{'default':>12}   consequence")
for layer, default, note in [
        ("TCP connect (tcp_syn_retries=6)", "~127 s", "a silently dropped SYN costs two minutes"),
        ("TCP established, no keep-alive", "~15 min", "tcp_retries2=15, before the socket dies"),
        ("TCP keep-alive first probe", "7200 s", "two hours — useless against NAT (§2.6)"),
        ("socket recv(), no timeout set", "infinite", "blocks forever if the peer vanishes"),
        ("DNS resolution (glibc)", "5 s x 2", "per resolver in resolv.conf"),
        ("HTTP client library", "often none", "check yours; many default to infinite")]:
    print(f"{layer:<34}{default:>12}   {note}")

connect(): the Linux SYN retry schedule with tcp_syn_retries = 6
   SYN 1: sent at t=  0s, next retry in  1s
   SYN 2: sent at t=  1s, next retry in  2s
   SYN 3: sent at t=  3s, next retry in  4s
   SYN 4: sent at t=  7s, next retry in  8s
   SYN 5: sent at t= 15s, next retry in 16s
   SYN 6: sent at t= 31s, next retry in 32s
   SYN 7: sent at t= 63s, next retry in 64s
   gives up at t=127s

layer                                  default   consequence
TCP connect (tcp_syn_retries=6)         ~127 s   a silently dropped SYN costs two minutes
TCP established, no keep-alive         ~15 min   tcp_retries2=15, before the socket dies
TCP keep-alive first probe              7200 s   two hours — useless against NAT (§2.6)
socket recv(), no timeout set         infinite   blocks forever if the peer vanishes
DNS resolution (glibc)                 5 s x 2   per resolver in resolv.conf
HTTP client library                 often none   check yours; many default to infinite

That ~127 seconds is a number worth recognising on sight. A connection attempt that fails at roughly two minutes was not refused — it was dropped silently, which means a firewall configured to DROP rather than REJECT, a black-holed route, or the wrong address entirely (§3.8). A refused connection fails in one round trip. The timing alone distinguishes them.

The remedy is that every network operation your application performs must have a timeout you chose, and §6.6 gives the mechanism. The defaults are chosen for a 1990s network where a two-minute retry was reasonable; they are not chosen for a request a user is waiting on.

14.3 A Timeout Budget

Individual timeouts are not enough, because they compose badly. A request that calls three services, each with a 10-second timeout, can take 30 seconds — while the client that made it gave up at 5.

The discipline is a budget: the caller states a deadline, and every hop works within what remains.

class Deadline:
    """An absolute deadline, propagated down a call chain."""
    def __init__(self, seconds, clock):
        self.at = clock() + seconds
        self.clock = clock

    def remaining(self):
        return max(0.0, self.at - self.clock())

    def for_call(self, reserve=0.05):
        """Budget for one downstream call, reserving time to handle its failure."""
        r = self.remaining() - reserve
        if r <= 0:
            raise TimeoutError("deadline exhausted before the call was made")
        return r

now = [0.0]                                   # an injected clock: no sleeping in a test
clock = lambda: now[0]

d = Deadline(2.0, clock)
for service, cost in [("auth", 0.30), ("catalogue", 0.80), ("pricing", 0.60)]:
    budget = d.for_call()
    print(f"call {service:<10} budget {budget:.2f}s", end="  ")
    now[0] += cost                            # the call takes this long
    print(f"took {cost:.2f}s, {d.remaining():.2f}s left")

try:
    d.for_call()
except TimeoutError as e:
    print(f"call inventory  refused: {e}")

call auth       budget 1.95s  took 0.30s, 1.70s left
call catalogue  budget 1.65s  took 0.80s, 0.90s left
call pricing    budget 0.85s  took 0.60s, 0.30s left

Three properties make this correct where a fixed per-call timeout is not. The deadline is absolute, so it survives being passed across a network as a header (gRPC does this natively; HTTP has no standard equivalent, so pick one and be consistent). Each call gets what is actually left, not a fixed allowance. And a call that cannot possibly complete is refused immediately rather than started and abandoned — which matters because starting it consumes a connection, a thread and downstream work for a result nobody will read.

Choosing the numbers: set the timeout from the latency distribution, not from a round number. A p99 of 200 ms suggests a timeout around 1 s — high enough that normal variation does not trip it, low enough to be useful. A timeout at the p99 itself would fail 1% of healthy requests; a 30-second timeout on a 200 ms service detects nothing.

14.4 The Half-Open Connection

The most common intermittent failure in any service with a connection pool, and it looks like a bug in your code.

A connection is half-open when one side believes it is established and the other does not. It happens constantly:

A NAT or firewall dropped the mapping after an idle period (§2.6). Neither endpoint is told. Both still believe the connection exists.
The peer's process died without a clean close — an OOM kill, a power loss. No FIN was sent.
The server closed while your request was in flight — an idle timeout expiring at the moment you reused the connection. This is a race you cannot win, only handle.

The third case is worth stating precisely because it is unavoidable: there is always a window in which a pooled connection has been closed by the server and the client does not yet know. No amount of health-checking removes it — the close can happen between your check and your write.

The defences, in order:

  1. Set the client's idle timeout below the server's (§13.5). If the server closes at 60 s and the client discards at 45 s, the client removes connections before the server does, and the race window nearly disappears.
  2. Retry idempotent requests on a fresh connection. A failure on the first write to a pooled connection is the signature: the request was almost certainly never processed, so retrying is safe even for a POST if you can establish that nothing was sent.
  3. Enable TCP keep-alive with sane values (§4.7) — 60 s idle, not the 7200 s default — which both detects dead peers and refreshes middlebox state so they do not become dead.
  4. Bound connection lifetime, not just idle time. A connection that has lived for five minutes should be retired regardless of activity, which also picks up DNS changes (§7.9) and lets load balancers rebalance (§12.4).

14.5 The MTU Black Hole

The most confusing failure in this section, because small requests work perfectly.

The mechanism, from §4.8: a link somewhere on the path has an MTU below 1500 — a VPN, a cloud overlay, a PPPoE connection. Your host sends a full-size packet with Don't Fragment set. The router drops it and sends back ICMP type 3 code 4, naming the smaller MTU. A firewall blocks the ICMP. Your host never learns, retransmits the same oversized packet, and the connection hangs forever with no error.

The signature is unmistakable once you know it:

  • GET requests succeed; POST with a body over ~1400 bytes hangs.
  • The TLS handshake completes for some servers and hangs for others — because the certificate chain size varies, and a 5 KB chain (§8.2) exceeds the path MTU while a small one does not.
  • It works from one network and not another.
  • There is no error, only a timeout.

Diagnose by binary search with Don't Fragment set:

ping -M do -s 1472 -c 1 <host>     # 1472 + 8 + 20 = 1500
ping -M do -s 1400 -c 1 <host>
ping -M do -s 1372 -c 1 <host>     # 1372 + 28 = 1400, a typical tunnel MTU

The largest size that succeeds, plus 28, is the path MTU. On this machine, sizes above the local interface MTU are rejected locally before leaving:

PING 1.1.1.1 (1.1.1.1) 1472(1500) bytes of data.
1480 bytes from 1.1.1.1: icmp_seq=1 ttl=53 time=45.1 ms

PING 1.1.1.1 (1.1.1.1) 1473(1501) bytes of data.
ping: sendmsg: Message too long

The fixes, in order of correctness: permit ICMP types 3 and 11 (the real fix — blocking all ICMP is a superstition that breaks Path MTU Discovery and traceroute for everyone behind you); enable net.ipv4.tcp_mtu_probing=1, which probes downward when a connection stalls with data outstanding; clamp the MSS at the tunnel endpoint (iptables ... TCPMSS --clamp-mss-to-pmtu), which is standard on VPN gateways; and only as a last resort, lower the interface MTU, which costs efficiency on every packet.

14.6 Pool Exhaustion and Cascading Failure

A connection pool bounds concurrency, which is its purpose and also its failure mode. When a downstream service slows from 20 ms to 2 s, every pooled connection is occupied a hundred times longer, the pool empties, and requests that do not need that downstream at all now queue behind ones that do.

pool_size, normal_ms, degraded_ms, arrival_rate = 50, 20, 2000, 1000   # req/s

for label, service_ms in [("healthy", normal_ms), ("degraded", degraded_ms)]:
    capacity = pool_size * 1000 / service_ms          # Little's law
    print(f"{label:<10} service {service_ms:>5} ms  ->  capacity {capacity:>7.0f} req/s", end="  ")
    if capacity >= arrival_rate:
        print("keeps up")
    else:
        print(f"queue grows by {arrival_rate - capacity:.0f} req/s")

healthy    service    20 ms  ->  capacity    2500 req/s  keeps up
degraded   service  2000 ms  ->  capacity      25 req/s  queue grows by 975 req/s

A hundredfold slowdown becomes a hundredfold capacity reduction — Little's law, and it is why partial degradation so reliably becomes total outage. The queue grows without bound, latency climbs until every request times out, and the service is fully down while its CPU sits idle.

The defences are the same set §11 of the Backend course argues for, and their network justification is the arithmetic above:

  • Bulkheads — a separate pool per downstream, so a slow one cannot starve the others. This is the highest-value single change, and it directly prevents the "requests that do not need that downstream" part.
  • Circuit breakers — after N consecutive failures, fail fast without attempting the call, and probe periodically. This converts a 2-second timeout into a 0-second rejection, which restores capacity immediately.
  • Bounded queues with load shedding — reject when the queue exceeds a depth. An unbounded queue does not add capacity; it converts a rejection into a timeout, which is worse for everyone.
  • Timeouts short enough to matter (§14.3). A 30-second timeout on a 20 ms service means degradation is indistinguishable from a hang.

14.7 Retries, and How They Cause Outages

Retrying is the obvious response to a transient failure and is the standard way a partial outage becomes a total one.

The mechanism is amplification. If every client retries three times, a service that is failing sees four times its normal load at exactly the moment it is least able to serve it. With retries at several layers — client, SDK, proxy, load balancer — the multiplication compounds:

layers = [("browser/client", 3), ("mobile SDK", 2), ("API gateway", 2), ("service mesh", 3)]
total = 1
for name, attempts in layers:
    total *= attempts
    print(f"{name:<18} x{attempts}   cumulative amplification: {total:>3}x")
print(f"\none user action can become {total} requests to the failing service")

browser/client     x3   cumulative amplification:   3x
mobile SDK         x2   cumulative amplification:   6x
API gateway        x2   cumulative amplification:  12x
service mesh       x3   cumulative amplification:  36x

one user action can become 36 requests to the failing service

Thirty-six requests from one user action. Retries must be configured at one layer, and every other layer must be told not to.

The rules for the layer that does retry:

  • Only idempotent requests, or requests carrying an idempotency key. This is the correctness constraint from §14.1 and it is not negotiable.
  • Exponential backoff with jitter. Exponential backoff alone synchronises clients into waves — every client that failed together retries together; jitter spreads them. Full jitter — a random value between zero and the current backoff — is the variant to use.
  • A retry budget, not a retry count. Cap retries at a small percentage of total requests (10% is a common figure), so a widespread failure cannot amplify at all. This is strictly better than a per-request limit, which does nothing to bound aggregate load.
  • Do not retry a 4xx. It will fail again. Retry timeouts, connection errors, 502, 503, 504, and 429 — the last only after Retry-After.
import random

def backoff_schedule(attempts, base=0.1, cap=10.0, seed=7):
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    for i in range(attempts):
        window = min(cap, base * 2 ** i)
        yield i + 1, window, rng.uniform(0, window)      # full jitter

print(f"{'attempt':>8}{'window':>9}{'actual wait':>13}")
for n, window, wait in backoff_schedule(7):
    print(f"{n:>8}{window:>8.1f}s{wait:>12.2f}s")

 attempt   window  actual wait
       1     0.1s        0.03s
       2     0.2s        0.03s
       3     0.4s        0.26s
       4     0.8s        0.06s
       5     1.6s        0.86s
       6     3.2s        1.17s
       7     6.4s        0.37s

The windows double; the actual waits are random within them. Two clients failing simultaneously wait different amounts and do not return together — which is the entire point, and what a fixed backoff fails to achieve.

14.8 Failures That Do Not Look Like Failures

The catalogue of things that are broken while reporting success:

Symptom Actual cause How to confirm
Truncated response, no error Close-delimited body cut short (§9.4) Compare Content-Length to bytes received; use chunked framing instead
Works for hours, fails at 3 a.m. A certificate expired (§8.10) openssl x509 -noout -dates against the live endpoint
Works everywhere but one region One backend has stale config curl --resolve against each address (§12.8)
Fails only for large uploads MTU black hole (§14.5) or a proxy body limit ping -M do; check the proxy's client_max_body_size
Fine at low load, collapses at high Pool exhaustion (§14.6) Instrument wait-for-connection separately from service time
Intermittent, unreproducible resets Half-open pooled connection (§14.4) Correlate with idle time; compare client and server idle timeouts
Latency doubles under load, no errors Bufferbloat (§5.5) rtt versus minrtt in ss -tin
A whole region goes dark at once DNS, not the service (§7.9) dig @1.1.1.1 versus dig @<your resolver>
Everything slow, CPU idle Blocking call in an event loop (§6.3) Loop-lag watchdog, or py-spy dump

The pattern across all of these: the error message names the layer that gave up, not the layer that failed. A 504 from a gateway says the gateway timed out, not why. The diagnostic method is always the same — move down the stack until you find the layer whose behaviour is anomalous, using the tool that observes that layer, which is §15.

Exercises

Exercise 14.1 — Set the timeouts. A user-facing API handles a request by calling an auth service (p50 5 ms, p99 40 ms), a database (p50 8 ms, p99 90 ms) and a recommendation service (p50 60 ms, p99 800 ms, and optional). The user-facing SLO is 500 ms at p99. Specify every timeout and retry policy, and justify them.

Solution 
overall request deadline          450 ms   (SLO 500 ms, minus headroom to return an error)
  auth          timeout  150 ms   ~4x its p99; retry once (idempotent read)
  database      timeout  250 ms   ~3x its p99; retry once ONLY for reads
  recommendations timeout 120 ms  DELIBERATELY BELOW its p99 — see below

Auth: 150 ms. Roughly four times the p99, so normal variation never trips it. It is a fast idempotent read, so one retry is safe and cheap — and a failure here fails the whole request, which justifies the retry.

Database: 250 ms. Reads may be retried once; writes may not, unless they carry an idempotency key. This is §14.1's second truth: a timed-out write may have committed.

Recommendations: 120 ms, which is below its own p99 — and that is deliberate. The service is optional, so the correct behaviour is to render the page without recommendations rather than to blow the budget waiting. Cutting off at 120 ms means about 5–10% of requests lose recommendations and 100% of requests meet the SLO. No retry — a retry on an optional call spends the budget of a required one. This inversion is the interesting decision in the exercise: for an optional dependency, the timeout should be set from what you can afford, not from what the dependency needs.

The arithmetic that makes it fit:

budget = 450
print(f"worst realistic case: auth 40 + db 90 + recs 120 = {40 + 90 + 120} ms")
print(f"with one auth retry:  +150 -> {40 + 90 + 120 + 150} ms, still under {budget}")
print(f"all timeouts fire:    {150 + 250 + 120} ms -> exceeds {budget}, so the deadline")
print(f"                      must be enforced globally, not per call")

worst realistic case: auth 40 + db 90 + recs 120 = 250 ms
with one auth retry:  +150 -> 400 ms, still under 450
all timeouts fire:    520 ms -> exceeds 450, so the deadline
                      must be enforced globally, not per call
